TL;DR: A carbon-aware scheduler for AI agent workflows (AgentDecarbonizer) reduces emissions by up to 57.9% relative to carbon-agnostic baselines and up to 37.5% versus a start-time carbon-optimal grid; uses deadline-aware, cache-aware, and grid-aware scheduling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agent workflows (long-running LLM-driven tasks with many model calls and tool interactions) incur substantial carbon emissions. Emissions depend on token usage, context caching, and grid carbon intensity. Temporal and spatial variations in carbon intensity offer opportunities for carbon-aware execution, but require handling uncertain execution times and cache recomputation during shifting.

Method: Empirical characterization of OpenClaw/OpenClaw-like agent workloads using WildClawBench. Design of AgentDecarbonizer that, given a task prompt and a user-specified deadline, conservatively estimates task duration and selects deadline-feasible execution schedules while accounting for cache recomputation overhead during spatial shifting. The system runs alongside OpenClaw and orchestrates task scheduling across multiple grids.

Result: Evaluated on WildClawBench workloads with 60 agent tasks across four grids, showing substantial emissions reductions: up to 57.9% compared with a carbon-agnostic baseline and up to 37.5% compared with a baseline that selects the carbon-optimal grid at task start time.

Conclusion: Carbon-aware scheduling that leverages deadline flexibility and cache-aware optimizations can substantially lower emissions for AI agent workflows. Practical deployment must address uncertainties in task duration and cache recomputation overhead, and future work should enhance duration prediction, latency-energy tradeoffs, and scalability across more grids.

Abstract: AI agents extend large language models from single prompt-response interactions to long-running, goaldirected workflows that issue many model calls, invoke tools, and interact with external environments. These workflows enable tasks such as software repair, data analysis, and experiment management, but their repeated model invocations can incur substantial carbon emissions. This paper characterizes the carbon emissions of OpenClaw agent workloads using WildClawBench, and shows that emissions depend on token consumption, context cache reuse, and the carbon intensity of the grid. Our characterization identifies deadline flexibility as an opportunity for carbon-aware execution: agent tasks can wait for lower-carbon-intensity periods or shift to lower-carbon grids. However, doing so requires handling uncertain execution time for temporal shifting and cached context recomputation during spatial shifting. We present AgentDecarbonizer, a carbon optimizer for AI agents that runs alongside OpenClaw. Given a task prompt and user-specified deadline, AgentDecarbonizer conservatively estimates task duration and selects deadline-feasible execution schedules, while accounting for cache recomputation overhead during spatial shifting. Evaluated on WildClawBench workloads with 60 agent tasks across four grids, AgentDecarbonizer reduces carbon emissions by up to 57.9 % compared with a carbon-agnostic baseline and by up to 37.5 % compared with a baseline that selects the carbon-optimal grid at task start time.

</details>


### [100] [Faults That Fortify: CNN Adversarial Robustness via GPU Undervolting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20572)
*Behnam Omidi,Ahmad Tahmasivand,Husam Alsyouri,Saba Al-Sayouri,Chongzhou Fang,Ihsen Alouani,Khaled N. Khasawneh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Hardware-level defense via GPU undervolting during training improves adversarial robustness and reduces energy, with no algorithmic changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address high training cost and adversarial vulnerability of CNNs, particularly on energy-constrained edge devices.

Method: Characterize undervolting-induced faults at the bit level; train LeNet, VGG-6, MobileNetV3 on MNIST and CIFAR-10 under nominal and undervolted voltage; two training regimes (standard and adversarial); evaluate against adversarial attacks.

Result: Undervolted models show higher adversarial accuracy than nominal-voltage models in both training regimes; energy savings due to quadratic scaling of dynamic power with voltage.

Conclusion: Hardware faults from undervolting act as implicit regularization, enabling robust training with energy efficiency gains; a readily deployable defense not requiring algorithmic changes.

Abstract: Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) face a dual challenge: vulnerability to adversarial attacks and prohibitive training cost. Adversarial training is effective but expensive, a burden that grows as learning shifts to the energy-constrained edge. This paper addresses both through GPU undervolting during training. Reducing supply voltage introduces stochastic perturbations that act as implicit regularization, improving robustness while lowering power. We characterize undervolting-induced faults at the bit level, then train LeNet, VGG-6, and MobileNetV3 on MNIST and CIFAR-10 under two training regimes, standard and adversarial, each at nominal and undervolted voltage, and evaluate all models against adversarial attacks. In both regimes, the undervolted model consistently achieves higher adversarial accuracy than its nominal-voltage counterpart, showing that hardware-induced faults strengthen even adversarial training. Because dynamic power scales quadratically with supply voltage, these robustness gains arrive with substantial energy savings. GPU undervolting is therefore a readily deployable hardware-level defense requiring no algorithmic change, and opens a promising direction in which robustness and energy efficiency move together.

</details>


### [101] [Provable Edge-of-Stability for Adam on a One-Dimensional Quadratic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20638)
*Yiman Fong,Heng Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Uncorrected Adam on a 1D quadratic exhibits edge-of-stability dynamics; across parameter space it tends to restore to a frozen stability threshold 2(1+β1)/(η(1−β1)); but edge behavior can fail in certain subcritical or specially tuned trajectories; provides a dynamical explanation and highlights limitations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Isolate the optimizer-induced dynamics behind Adam's edge-of-stability by removing evolving loss geometry, using a simple quadratic to analyze the intrinsic dynamics.

Method: Dynamical-system analysis of the uncorrected Adam update on a one-dimensional quadratic; analytic characterization across parameter space; proving restoring tendency to the stability threshold; identifying regimes where edge-seeking breaks down (subcritical periodic orbits, tuned trajectories).

Result: In broad regimes, Adam exhibits a restoring tendency toward the frozen stability threshold; threshold value given; edge-seeking mechanisms can fail in subcritical periodic orbits and specially tuned convergent trajectories; these results explain EoS under simple loss geometry and delineate limitations.

Conclusion: The paper provides a concrete dynamical explanation for Adam's edge-of-stability in a geometry-free setting and delineates the limitations of EoS, informing understanding and potential mitigation strategies.

Abstract: The edge-of-stability (EoS) phenomenon of Adam has been widely observed, while its underlying dynamical mechanism is not yet fully understood. We study uncorrected Adam on a one-dimensional quadratic, a clean setting where constant curvature isolates the optimizer-induced dynamics behind the EoS. We characterize the resulting dynamics across the parameter space. In broad regimes, we prove that Adam exhibits a restoring tendency toward its frozen stability threshold $2(1+β_1)/[η(1-β_1)]$. We also identify settings in which this edge-seeking mechanism breaks down, including strictly subcritical periodic orbits and specially tuned trajectories that converge to the optimum while remaining uniformly supercritical. These results give a concrete dynamical explanation for Adam's EoS in a setting free of evolving loss geometry, while also exposing its limitations.

</details>


### [102] [Meta-clustering of milk mid-infrared spectra identifies dairy cow groups associated with negative energy balance in early lactation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20653)
*T. Touil,E. R. Paquet*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Eight MIR-spectrum clustering approaches converge on five meta-clusters in early-lactation cows, linked to NEB severity; PCA-based k-means with full spectrum suffices to recover these clusters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Identify latent cow groups directly from milk MIR spectra to relate to milk traits and health status (NEB), enabling monitoring and early intervention.

Method: Used 407,632 milk MIR records from 3,408 farms. Applied spectral filtering to select informative wavenumbers, two dimensionality reduction techniques (PCA and autoencoder), and two clustering algorithms (k-means and spectral clustering) for eight approaches. Meta-clusters were formed by regrouping similar clusters across methods.

Result: Identified five distinct meta-clusters significantly associated with milk traits. Despite methodological differences, the eight approaches converged on the same five meta-clusters. The PCA-based k-means on full spectrum recaptured these clusters. Meta-clusters map onto a gradient of NEB severity (severe, moderate, mild) and two recovery states.

Conclusion: Clustering MIR spectra provides robust, biologically meaningful cow groupings related to energy balance; simple, scalable PCA-kmeans with full spectrum performs as well as more complex methods; the approach has potential for monitoring at-risk cows in production settings.

Abstract: Clustering methods have been used to identify distinct groups of milk samples, cows, or herds. Fourier-transform infrared (FTIR) spectroscopy, particularly mid-infrared (MIR) spectroscopy, has been applied to individual cow milk samples to predict various milk traits. Applying clustering directly to MIR spectral data may reveal latent groups of cows associated with milk traits or health disorders and can help prevent these conditions or monitor at-risk animals. This study aimed to identify groups of individual dairy cows in early lactation directly from milk MIR spectra and to analyze their associations with milk traits. Using a dataset of 407,632 individual milk MIR records from 3,408 commercial farms, we combined (i) spectral filtering that selects informative wavenumbers, (ii) two dimensionality-reduction methods: principal component analysis (PCA) and an autoencoder, and (iii) two clustering algorithms: k-means and spectral clustering to yield eight different clustering approaches. We regrouped the assigned clusters into meta-clusters that encompassed the most similar ones identified by the eight approaches. Our results revealed five distinct meta-clusters of early-lactation individual dairy cows significantly associated with milk traits. Despite substantial differences, the eight approaches converged on the same five meta-clusters, and the classic, computationally efficient PCA-based k-means approach using the full spectrum recaptured clusters identified by more sophisticated, computationally intensive approaches. The five meta-clusters were strongly associated with DIM and appeared to reflect a gradient of negative energy balance (NEB) severity: severe, moderate, and possibly mild, while the remaining two likely represented cows recovering from NEB, one with rapid restoration of energy balance and one in early recovery.

</details>


### [103] [RiskTraf: Risk-Extrapolated Residual Learning for Multi-Variate Traffic Flow Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20656)
*Guangyu Wang,Zhidan Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PEMSB-3V provides a three-variable traffic benchmark (flow, speed, occupancy) and RiskTraf adds a model-agnostic risk-extrapolated residual head to improve flow forecasting without changing the backbone, yielding robust, horizon-wise corrections across regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard benchmarks often neglect raw speed and occupancy or use proxies, leading to biased forecasts. Relationships among flow, speed, and occupancy shift between free-flow and congested states, risking shortcut correlations if learned jointly. A dataset preserving all measurements and a risk-aware residual head aim to enable fair evaluation and robust predictions across regimes.

Method: PEMSB-3V preserves raw flow, speed, and occupancy from PeMS detectors. RiskTraf freezes a trained spatio-temporal backbone and couples it with a lightweight residual head that uses historical speed/occupancy to produce horizon-wise flow corrections. The residual head builds ordered traffic-risk environments and optimizes a risk-extrapolation objective (zero-start head) to mitigate regime-specific shortcuts without modifying the backbone.

Result: Empirical evaluation shows RiskTraf consistently improves diverse forecasting backbones and outperforms debiasing and distribution-shift adaptation methods. Code and benchmark are released at the provided URL.

Conclusion: RiskTraf provides a backbone-agnostic, risk-aware residual correction that enhances flow forecasting using all three traffic variables, addressing regime shifts without retraining core backbones and offering a robust benchmark for fair evaluation.

Abstract: Traffic sensors commonly record flow, speed, and occupancy, but standard traffic flow forecasting benchmarks and models rarely exploit all three raw measurements reliably. Although speed and occupancy provide sensor-native traffic-state information beyond flow alone, existing releases often omit these variables, replace them with proxies, or contain logically inconsistent records. Moreover, direct empirical risk minimization over three-variable inputs may exploit regime-dependent shortcuts, as the relationships among flow, speed, and occupancy vary substantially between free-flow and congested states. We introduce \textbf{PEMSB-3V}, a public benchmark suite that preserves raw flow, speed, and occupancy measurements from PeMS detectors for flow prediction. We also propose \textbf{RiskTraf}, a model-agnostic risk-extrapolated residual plug-in. For each trained spatio-temporal backbone, RiskTraf freezes the selected checkpoint and learns a lightweight zero-start residual head from historical speed and occupancy. The residual head constructs ordered traffic-risk environments and optimizes horizon-wise flow corrections with a risk extrapolation objective, thereby mitigating regime-specific shortcut correlations without modifying the backbone. Extensive experiments demonstrate that RiskTraf consistently improves diverse forecasting backbones and outperforms debiasing and distribution-shift adaptation methods. Our code and benchmark are available at https://github.com/Guangyu4/RiskTraf.

</details>


### [104] [C-Score: Beyond Accuracy for Robustness Assessment in Semi-Supervised Learning under Open-World Unlabeled Contamination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20667)
*Tsao-Lun Chen,Chi-Cheng Fu,Han-Yi E. Chou,Shun-Feng Su*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Open-world unlabeled contamination undermines SSL despite stable test accuracy; C-Score diagnoses hidden training collapse across prediction, representation, and optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Identify hidden degradation in pseudo-label SSL when unlabeled data include out-of-distribution samples; traditional accuracy metrics may mask issues.

Method: Propose C-Score framework evaluating three spaces: prediction (PLE, CCI), representation (Sem-Drift relative to labeled anchors), optimization (Grad-Align). Empirical evaluation on CIFAR-10/100 with SVHN contamination and near-OOD sources; four SSL algorithms.

Result: Internal metrics detect degradation not visible in clean accuracy: CCI can rise >280%, near-OOD can yield up to ~14.9% accuracy collapse; thus robust evaluation requires diagnostics.

Conclusion: Clean accuracy is insufficient for assessing SSL robustness in open-world settings; C-Score provides diagnostic signals to better evaluate and potentially improve SSL under unlabeled contamination.

Abstract: Pseudo-label-based semi-supervised learning has achieved strong performance due to its simplicity and scalability. However, it is typically developed under a closed-world assumption that unlabeled data are drawn from the same distribution as labeled data. In practical deployment, unlabeled data are often collected from open environments and may contain OOD samples. Under such contamination, OOD samples may still receive high-confidence predictions and be incorporated into training as if they were valid target examples. This creates an important evaluation problem: clean in-distribution test accuracy may appear stable even when the internal learning dynamics of SSL have already deteriorated. To address this issue, we study hidden collapse in pseudo-label-based SSL under open-world unlabeled contamination from a diagnostic evaluation perspective. We present C-Score, a compact framework that evaluates training behavior in three complementary spaces: prediction, feature representation, and optimization. C-Score includes PLE and CCI for unlabeled prediction behavior, Sem-Drift for deviation from labeled semantic anchors, and Grad-Align for the compatibility between labeled and unlabeled optimization. Experiments on CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100 with multiple OOD sources, varying contamination ratios, and four pseudo-label-based SSL algorithms show that C-Score metrics reveal hidden degradation that clean accuracy alone fails to detect: under SVHN contamination, CCI rises over 280% while best-accuracy remains within 3% of the uncontaminated baseline; near-OOD sources (CIFAR-100, STL-10) cause up to 14.9% accuracy collapse (FlexMatch, r=0.5). The results suggest that clean accuracy alone is insufficient for evaluating SSL robustness in open-world environments, and that internal diagnostic signals are necessary for more reliable robustness assessment under unlabeled contamination.

</details>


### [105] [Lightweight Adaptive ReduNet via Hyperspherical Manifold Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20668)
*Zhenglin Huang,Qifa Yan,Bin Dai,Xiaohu Tang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LA-ReduNet is a lightweight, adaptive extension of ReduNet that achieves similar MCR^2 performance with far fewer layers and much lower parameter storage by using hyperspherical manifold learning and adaptive steps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ReduNet's layer-wise MCR^2 optimization requires many layers, leading to large parameter storage. A more compact, efficient architecture is desired.

Method: Introduce LA-ReduNet, refine the layer update rule with hyperspherical manifold learning and adaptive step sizes, enabling discriminative features with an order-of-magnitude fewer unfolded layers; compare to ReduNet via simulations.

Result: Simulation results show comparable classification accuracy with significantly fewer layers for the MCR^2 objective to stabilize; parameter storage is reduced by approximately 29x under the reported settings.

Conclusion: Adaptive hyperspherical, lightweight updates yield an efficient, interpretable forward-only network for MCR^2-based feature learning, offering practical benefits in storage and computation while maintaining performance.

Abstract: In recent years, a white-box neural network called ReduNet has been proposed, which employs the maximal coding rate reduction (MCR$^2$) principle to transform raw data into low-dimensional discriminative features via a forward layer-wise construction process. Unlike traditional deep networks that rely on backpropagation, ReduNet explicitly derives the parameters of each layer from the features of its preceding layer, offering a mathematically interpretable paradigm. However, this layer-wise construction often requires a large number of layers for the MCR$^2$ objective to reach a stable value, which increases the parameter storage of the unfolded module. To address this issue, we propose LA-ReduNet, a lightweight adaptive architecture that refines the layer-wise update rule and enables discriminative feature representations to be obtained with substantially fewer unfolded layers. Specifically, LA-ReduNet employs hyperspherical manifold learning and adaptive step sizes, thereby reducing by an order of magnitude the number of layers required for the MCR$^2$ objective to reach a stable value. Simulation results demonstrate that, while maintaining comparable classification accuracy, LA-ReduNet requires significantly fewer layers for the MCR$^2$ objective to reach a stable value. Remarkably, under the considered experimental settings, LA-ReduNet requires only approximately $1/29$ of the parameter storage of the unfolded ReduNet module for the MCR$^2$ objective to reach a stable value.

</details>


### [106] [Reinforcement Learning for Continuous-Time Jump Markov Decision Processes with Applications to Network Dynamic Pricing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20680)
*Huiling Meng,Ningyuan Chen,Xuefeng Gao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Model-free q-learning for entropy-regularized Continuous-Time Jump MDPs (CTJMDPs) with general discrete state spaces; bridges theory from controlled diffusions to CTJMDPs; provides algorithms and empirical validation in network dynamic pricing showing near-optimal policies and scalability over discretization-based methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the gap where existing continuous-time RL methods rely on Euclidean state spaces and semimartingale theory, making them unsuitable for CTJMDPs with general discrete state spaces lacking vector-space structure.

Method: Develop theoretical foundations for q-learning in CTJMDPs, formulate entropy-regularized control with stochastic policies, derive model-free q-learning algorithms, and compare to naive time discretization and discrete-time MDP approximations; validate on network dynamic pricing problems.

Result: Proposed RL algorithm learns near-optimal policies and outperforms standard benchmarks, demonstrating robustness and scalability to large-scale network instances.

Conclusion: The work extends q-learning to CTJMDPs with general discrete state spaces, offering practical model-free methods and empirical efficacy over conventional discretization approaches in complex dynamic pricing settings.

Abstract: We study reinforcement learning (RL) in Continuous-Time Jump Markov Decision Processes (CTJMDPs) featuring general discrete state spaces (which need not possess a vector space structure) and continuous/discrete action spaces. The setup covers many well-known applications in operations such as multi-product dynamic pricing with capacitated resources (Gallego and van Ryzin 1997). To model the exploration-exploitation tradeoff, we formulate an entropy-regularized continuous-time control problem with stochastic policies. Recent continuous-time RL techniques such as $q$-learning for controlled diffusions in (Jia and Zhou 2023) focus on continuous state spaces $\mathbb{R}^d$ and rely heavily on semimartingale theory in $\mathbb{R}^d$ for their theoretical analysis. Consequently, their methods cannot be directly applied to CTJMDPs with general discrete state spaces, which may lack the algebraic addition and subtraction structures inherent to Euclidean spaces. To bridge this gap, we establish the theoretical foundations of $q$-learning for CTJMDPs and develop model-free $q$-learning algorithms. Compared to naïve time discretization and approximating CTJMDPs using discrete-time MDPs, our approach has several conceptual and empirical benefits. Numerical experiments in network dynamic pricing (Gallego and van Ryzin 1997) show that our proposed RL algorithm reliably learns near-optimal policies and consistently outperforms standard benchmark methods, demonstrating superior solution quality and effective scalability to large-scale network instances.

</details>


### [107] [Geometric Regularization for Long-Tailed Semi-Supervised Learning via Gaussian Feature Bridges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20710)
*Hongyang He,Xinyuan Song,Yan Zhong,Daizong Liu,Yanbin Li,Yang-fan He,Wenqiao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Gaussian Bridge Consistency (GBC) introduces a class-conditional Gaussian feature bridge and a dynamic Prototype Atlas to guide unlabeled samples toward reliable class prototypes, with BridgeMix for confident feature interpolation; demonstrates robust long-tailed SSL on CIFAR10-LT and ImageNet-LT with scalability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-tailed label distributions and noisy pseudo-labels in real-world SSL cause poor generalization and confirmation bias; need mechanisms to leverage prototypes and interpolate to reduce bias.

Method: Construct semantic interpolation paths in latent space between unlabeled samples and class anchors via a class-conditional Gaussian bridge; maintain a Prototype Atlas of labeled/pseudo-labeled exemplars; apply bridge consistency loss along the path; introduce BridgeMix to interpolate both sample and anchors with confidence-awareness.

Result: Empirically achieve improved performance on long-tail classes on CIFAR10-LT and ImageNet-LT, with robustness to noise and scalability.

Conclusion: GBC provides a principled, scalable framework for long-tail SSL, leveraging semantic interpolation and confidence-aware mixing to mitigate confirmation bias and improve generalization.

Abstract: Real-world semi-supervised learning (SSL) often encounters significant challenges with long-tailed label distributions and noisy pseudo-labels, which hinder generalization and amplify confirmation bias. In this work, we introduce a novel framework, Gaussian Bridge Consistency (GBC), to address these challenges by constructing semantic interpolation paths between unlabeled samples and high-quality class anchors. Our method maintains a dynamic Prototype Atlas that stores a diverse and evolving set of labeled and pseudo-labeled exemplars per class. For each unlabeled instance, GBC forms a class-conditional Gaussian Feature Bridge in the latent space, enabling the student model to traverse a smooth trajectory from uncertain predictions to reliable class prototypes. A bridge consistency loss is applied along this path to enforce alignment with a geometrically interpolated target distribution. Furthermore, we propose BridgeMix, a confidence-aware feature mixing strategy that interpolates both sample and anchor pairs to amplify cross-sample generalization. Extensive experiments on CIFAR10-LT and ImageNet-LT (USB benchmarks) validate the robustness and effectiveness of GBC under realistic long-tailed SSL settings, consistently improving long tail-class performance without sacrificing scalability.

</details>


### [108] [Decoupling Policy Extraction for Offline Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20909)
*Xuyao Lin,Yixiang Shan,Jinru Duan,Tao Yang,Xinyu Zhao,Runyu Lei,Yiming Zhao,Jiaxin Fan,Zongbao Feng,Peng Jia*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Decoupled policy extraction for offline RL: train actor to mimic behavior; perform policy improvement at inference by ranking multiple actor proposals with a separate critic, yielding better performance than BC and joint offline RL even with a simple Q-learning critic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the limitations of joint actor-critic training in offline RL, where fixed data leads to critic overestimation and a trade-off between suppressing OOD actions and exploiting data-supported high-value actions.

Method: Train the actor to model the behavior distribution. At inference, generate multiple action proposals and use a separately learned critic to rank and select the best action within this candidate set, effectively decoupling policy improvement from actor training.

Result: Empirical results show the decoupled paradigm outperforms both behavior cloning and jointly learned offline RL methods, and remains effective even with a naive Q-learning critic.

Conclusion: Decoupling policy improvement from actor training improves offline RL performance by focusing actor on behavior modeling while using a learned critic for value-based selection; the approach mitigates OOD action issues and critic overestimation without heavy regularization.

Abstract: Offline RL methods commonly jointly train the actor and critic, where the critic is used to guide the actor toward higher-value actions. This coupled learning process is well motivated in online RL, where an improved actor collects new data that can further update the actor and the critic. However, training data remains fixed in offline RL, making actor-side policy improvement unable to generate new data to validate or correct the critic. Moreover, retaining this coupled paradigm leads to two related challenges. Firstly, actor updates can drift toward high-valued but potentially out-of-distribution (OOD) actions and amplify critic overestimation. Secondly, conservative value estimation or behavior-cloning regularization creates a difficult trade-off between suppressing OOD actions and selecting high-value actions within the data-supported region. Motivated by this observation, we revisit the conventional offline RL paradigm and propose decoupling policy improvement from actor training. Specifically, we train the actor solely to model the behavior distribution and perform policy improvement at inference time by reranking multiple actor-generated proposals with a separately learned critic. We refer to this paradigm as the decoupled policy extraction paradigm. Under such paradigm, the actor provides behavior-supported action candidates, while the critic performs value-based selection within this candidate set. Extensive experiments show that the decoupled policy extraction paradigm outperforms both behavior cloning and jointly learned offline RL methods, while remaining effective even with a naive Q-learning critic.

</details>


### [109] [Hidden Axis of Uncertainty: Latent-Posterior Alignment in Graph Neural Networks with Bayesian Output Layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20758)
*Suk Hoon Choi,Damdae Park,Junhyuk Choi,Hyein Jung,Changsoo Kim,Ung Lee,Kyeongsu Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Latent-Posterior Alignment (LPA) in GNNs with Bayesian outputs reduces predictive uncertainty via alignment between latent representations and low-variance posterior directions, even without posterior variance contraction; Alignment-Guided Learning (AGL) promotes this alignment, reducing uncertainty while preserving accuracy and improving structural calibration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding why predictive uncertainty in Bayesian neural networks with Bayesian output layers behaves under deep architectures; posterior contraction may not occur, so mechanisms beyond posterior magnitude must explain uncertainty changes.

Method: Interventional experiments to identify LPA as a mechanism; development of Alignment-Guided Learning (AGL) objective to explicitly promote alignment between latent representations and posterior directions during training.

Result: Predictive uncertainty decreases due to latent-posterior alignment rather than posterior contraction; AGL reduces uncertainty, maintains accuracy, and improves structural calibration.

Conclusion: Uncertainty dynamics in GNNs with mean-field Bayesian outputs are governed by the geometric interplay between latent and parameter spaces; focus shifts from posterior magnitude to latent-posterior alignment.

Abstract: Bayesian Neural Networks (BNNs) with Bayesian output layers provide a principled and tractable framework for quantifying predictive uncertainty, yet the mechanisms shaping that uncertainty remain unclear. While conventional theory attributes uncertainty reduction to posterior contraction, the corresponding assumptions need not hold for deep models. In the Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) with Bayesian output layers studied here, we observe that predictive uncertainty decreases as latent representations shift toward lower-variance posterior directions, even though the posterior variance does not contract. We term this behavior Latent-Posterior Alignment (LPA) and conduct interventional experiments that support its functional role in shaping predictive uncertainty. Building on this insight, we propose Alignment-Guided Learning (AGL), which explicitly promotes this alignment during training. AGL effectively reduces predictive uncertainty while preserving accuracy and improves structural calibration, ensuring that the model confidence faithfully mirrors underlying data density. These findings provide a new perspective on uncertainty dynamics in GNNs with mean-field Bayesian output layers, shifting the focus from the magnitude of the posterior to the geometric interplay between latent and parameter spaces.

</details>


### [110] [Fuzzy-MoE: Interpretable Regime-Conditioned Expert Routing for Non-Stationary Multivariate Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20761)
*Lan Guo,Jie Xiao,Zhao Su,Jun Shen,Haoran Li,Weixia Ma,Qingguo Zhou,Binbin Yong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Fuzzy-MoE introduces a fuzzy-rule-based dynamic Mixture-of-Experts for non-stationary multivariate time series, enabling interpretable latent-state routing and per-variable expert activation, achieving improved forecasting accuracy on benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Non-stationary multivariate time series exhibit heterogeneous latent dynamics across variables and samples, which traditional end-to-end models compress into a single mapping, hindering modeling flexibility and interpretability of which forecasting mechanism is active under different latent states.

Method: A dual-view fuzzy router within a Mixture-of-Experts framework. Multiple parallel expert networks are guided by a router that leverages local convolutional features and global segmented statistics. The router uses learnable Gaussian membership functions to assign latent temporal states and compute soft expert activation strengths, yielding explicit IF-THEN rule-based expert selection. Different variables in the same sequence can activate different experts, enabling fine-grained routing.

Result: On public time series benchmarks, Fuzzy-MoE significantly outperforms mainstream forecasting methods in accuracy. The fuzzy memberships and rule activations provide interpretable routing diagnostics, demonstrating both performance gains and mechanism transparency compared to traditional black-box MoE routing.

Conclusion: The approach delivers both improved forecasting performance and interpretable, state-aware routing in time series forecasting, contributing to the development of transparent dynamic routing inMixture-of-Experts models.

Abstract: In non-stationary multivariate time series, different variables and samples often exhibit heterogeneous latent dynamic states, while existing deep forecasting models usually compress them into a unified end-to-end mapping, leading to suboptimal modeling of time-varying dynamics and limited interpretability regarding which forecasting mechanism is activated under different latent states. To overcome these limitations, we reformulate time series forecasting as a unified framework of latent temporal state identification and interpretable expert routing, and propose Fuzzy-MoE, a fuzzy logic-based dynamic Mixture-of-Experts model. Fuzzy-MoE consists of multiple parallel expert mapping networks and a dual-view fuzzy router. By jointly exploiting local convolutional dynamics and global segmented statistics, the router infers latent temporal states and computes expert activation strengths through learnable Gaussian membership functions, enabling explicit IF-THEN rule-based expert selection. This fine-grained routing strategy allows different variables within the same sequence to activate different experts, effectively capturing heterogeneous temporal dynamics while improving model interpretability. Experimental results on multiple public time series benchmark datasets show that Fuzzy-MoE significantly outperforms mainstream forecasting methods in forecasting accuracy. Moreover, fuzzy memberships and rule activations provide interpretable routing diagnostics, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed framework in both forecasting performance and mechanism transparency. Unlike traditional MoE models that use black-box routing, Fuzzy-MoE`s routing is based on clear, interpretable fuzzy rules. This makes the expert selection transparent and traceable.

</details>


### [111] [Resolution-Consistent Greedy Neural Approximation on Infinite-Dimensional Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20812)
*Pablo M. Berná,Antonio Falcó,Diego Mondéjar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces a parameter-normalized neural dictionary for shallow nets with infinite-dimensional inputs, yielding a clean split of approximation error into truncation and finite-width terms and providing dimension-free statistical guarantees via a greedy learning scheme; extends to Hilbert-valued outputs and is supported by synthetic experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To obtain constructive, finite-sample guarantees for learning with infinite-dimensional inputs, bridging universal approximation with statistical performance by isolating truncation error from finite-width effects and providing a dimension-free statistical framework.

Method: Define a parameter-normalized neural dictionary and a weighted variation class; decompose error into a distribution-dependent coordinate-truncation term plus a greedy finite-width term; use a fully-corrective greedy procedure for empirical regression; extend framework to Hilbert-valued responses; ground ideas in quasi-Polish constructions and illustrate via synthetic experiments.

Result: Population guarantees for empirical regression with a statistical complexity that remains uniform in retained input resolution; error separation clarifies how resolution, width, and sample size trade off; dimension-free statements are statistical (not computational) and remain subject to nonconvex parameter search when adding neurons; applicability to Hilbert-valued outputs without explicit output-dimension dependence.

Conclusion: The work provides a constructive, dimension-free statistical framework for shallow models with infinite-dimensional inputs, clarifying the roles of truncation and width errors and linking to recent infinite-dimensional universal approximation via quasi-Polish constructions; computational challenges persist, but the theory guides resolution/width/sample-size regimes and is supported by synthetic experiments.

Abstract: We develop constructive approximation and learning guarantees for shallow neural models with infinite-dimensional inputs observed through finitely many coordinates. The analysis is based on a parameter-normalized neural dictionary and its associated weighted variation class. Within this class, the approximation error separates into a distribution-dependent coordinate-truncation term and a greedy finite-width term. For empirical regression, a fully-corrective greedy procedure yields population guarantees whose statistical complexity is uniform in the retained input resolution. The same framework extends to Hilbert-valued responses without an explicit dependence on the output dimension. The dimension-free statements are statistical, not computational: selecting a new neuron still requires solving a nonconvex parameter-search problem. The quasi-Polish construction underlying recent infinite-dimensional universal approximation results provides a motivating example, and synthetic experiments illustrate the predicted resolution, width, and sample-size regimes.

</details>


### [112] [Scaling Muon for Diffusion Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20818)
*Chenghao Li,Xiao Han,Xinxin Huang,Wei Liu,Boyang Li,Bing Xiao,Heran Zhang,Juanma Perez Rua,Ke Xu,Kangning Liu,Linjun Kuang,Na Li,Tan Wang,Tian Xie,Wei Peng,Yang Pei,Yifan Xu,Yuanhao Zhai,Yuwei Lin,Zhe Wang,Zihao He,Daniel Li,Junbiao Tang,Ziyang Jiang,Dake Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Periodic Row-wise Muon offers a scalable, end-to-end efficient variant of Muon for large Diffusion Transformers by performing a full NS5 update only every K steps and using lightweight, momentum-guided row-wise updates in between, preserving Muon's generative quality gains while significantly reducing compute, communication, and end-to-end training time across 1.3B–15B parameter DiTs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Muon's advantages in optimization and generative quality persist at large model scales, but the full NS5 spectral update and complete momentum materialization introduce substantial overhead that can negate step-efficiency gains. A scalable, end-to-end efficient optimizer is needed for large DiTs.

Method: Introduce Periodic Row-wise Muon: perform a full NS5 spectral update once every K steps and apply low-compute, row-wise constrained updates based on current momentum on the remaining steps. Co-design distributed implementation operating on sharded momentum during non-refresh steps and accelerate spectral refreshes via bucketed all-gather and overlap of communication and computation. Evaluate across DiT scales (1.3B–15B).

Result: Across scales, Muon improves best observed generative quality over AdamW by 12.9–19.1%. Periodic Row-wise Muon remains within 0.5% of vanilla Muon up to 4B models and improves by 4.5% at 9B. It reduces optimizer time by 46.9–54.3%, end-to-end step time by 15.7–24.3%, and logical communication volume by 66.7%, while achieving best generative quality with 33.7–64.8% less active training time. Overall, Periodic Row-wise Muon preserves Muon's quality advantage while delivering end-to-end efficiency gains for large DiTs.

Conclusion: Periodic Row-wise Muon maintains Muon's generative quality benefits and translates them into substantial end-to-end efficiency for large Diffusion Transformers, enabling scalable optimization at 1.3B–15B parameter scales.

Abstract: The matrix-aware optimizer Muon improves large model training by balancing updates across singular directions, yet its scaling behavior and end-to-end efficiency on large Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) remain unclear. We first establish Muon's scaling behavior on DiTs from 1.3B to 15B parameters, showing that its optimization and generative quality advantages over AdamW persist across model scales. However, at scale, the 5-step Newton--Schulz iteration (NS5) performed at every optimization step, together with full-momentum materialization, introduces substantial computation and communication overhead that can offset Muon's step-efficiency advantage. We introduce \emph{Periodic Row-wise Muon}, which performs a full NS5 spectral update once every \(K\) steps and applies a low compute and communication cost row-wise constrained update based on the current momentum at the remaining steps. We further co-design a distributed implementation that operates directly on sharded momentum during non-refresh steps and accelerates spectral refreshes through bucketed all-gather and communication--computation overlap. Across all scales, Muon improves the best observed generative quality over AdamW by 12.9--19.1\%. Compared with vanilla Muon, Periodic Row-wise Muon remains within 0.5\% in best generative quality on the 1.3B--4B models and improves it by 4.5\% at 9B. It reduces optimizer time by 46.9--54.3\%, end-to-end step time by 15.7--24.3\%, and logical communication volume by 66.7\%, while reaching its respective best generative quality with 33.7--64.8\% less active training time. These results show that Periodic Row-wise Muon preserves Muon's generative quality advantage while translating it into end-to-end training efficiency for large DiTs.

</details>


### [113] [Nothing Changed but the Model: CellFill -- Bounded In-Cell Learning for Bit-Identical, Revocable Updates to Quantized LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20873)
*Zifeng Liu,Zhiyong Du,Yaxin Lu,Yiming Mao,Zhenhe Wang,Wenqi Shi,Zhengkun Jing*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A neuromorphic update method for 4‑bit quantized LMs writes new knowledge into per‑weight residuals inside each quantization cell, enabling bit‑identical re‑quantization, revocability by dropping residuals, and bounded drift. Invariance approaches (CellFill) near‑match unconstrained updates, reduce cross‑domain forgetting versus naive adapters, and scale to a 27B model with similar gains, albeit with costs tied to knowledge and rehearsal effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable durable, verifiably identical updates to deployed quantized language models by localizing changes within the quantization cells, ensuring revocability and bounded drift, while avoiding full fine-tuning or adapter deployments that alter released artifacts.

Method: Introduce per-weight residuals confined to the 4‑bit quantization decision cells; freeze codes and scales of the released release; write knowledge into residuals; re-quantize to obtain bit-identical artifacts; propose six propositions and three training paths, including CellFill (a bounded reparameterization enforcing invariance structurally); compare constrained invariance to unconstrained updates and to a natural null (unmerged adapters); evaluate on factual recall and cross-domain perplexity; extend to a 27B hybrid linear-attention model with bit-identical weights.

Result: Exact invariance is near‑free: constrained dense path nearly matches unconstrained reference (58.9% vs 59.3% factual recall; diff −0.5 with 95% CI [−5.0, +4.0]); better on held‑out cross‑domain perplexity. Projection into cells reduces cross‑domain forgetting when convergent; projection acts as a trust region. All methods incur a cost of knowledge; in-domain perplexity can improve due to rehearsal sharing. Across three seeds, performance orderings depend on knowledge per cross-domain perplexity. Transfers to a 27B model (2.4e10 constrained weights; bit-identical) with roughly half the cross‑domain perplexity cost at 1.7B.

Conclusion: It is feasible to achieve bit‑identical, revocable updates inside a 4‑bit quantization framework by embedding knowledge in cell-residuals; invariance constraints can be nearly as effective as unconstrained updates with minimal recall penalty and improved cross‑domain robustness, though gains depend on rehearsal and metric choices. The approach scales to larger models, with similar relative efficiency gains, and establishes a structured alternative to standard adapters or full fine-tuning for deployed LMs.

Abstract: Every way of teaching a deployed language model something new -- full fine-tuning, adapter merging, model editing -- replaces the released checkpoint, and with it every evaluation and cache that referred to those exact bits. We instead learn inside the dequantization gap: with the integer codes and scales of a 4-bit release frozen, new knowledge is written only into the per-weight residual that lives strictly inside each quantization decision cell. Re-quantization then returns the released artifact bit-for-bit, a machine-checkable guarantee; updates are exactly revocable by dropping the residual; and drift is bounded. We give six propositions and three training paths, including CellFill, a bounded reparameterization that makes invariance structural rather than enforced.
  Exact invariance turns out to be nearly free: across three paired seeds the constrained dense path matches an unconstrained reference whose weights provably escape the artifact (58.9 vs 59.3 percent fact recall; paired difference -0.5 points, 95% CI [-5.0,+4.0]), and is better on held-out cross-domain perplexity. Against the natural null hypothesis -- serving the same update as an unmerged adapter -- projecting into the cells reduces cross-domain forgetting in every run that converged, and a diverged control shows the boundary: projection is a trust region, not a repair. What no method escapes is the cost of knowledge itself, and the apparent free lunch of in-domain perplexity improving past the anchor is an artifact of rehearsal sharing a corpus with the metric. Methods differ threefold at matched rehearsal in knowledge bought per point of cross-domain perplexity, a ranking that is not the recall ranking. The method transfers to a 27B hybrid linear-attention model (2.4e10 constrained weights, verified bit-identical), where matched recall costs about half as much cross-domain perplexity as at 1.7B.

</details>


### [114] [Training, learning and inference: unified dynamics of neural systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20965)
*Mian Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Defines a Generation-Fact Graph (GFG) of atomic generation facts to preserve generation histories; demonstrates training-learning dynamics via nanoGPT, with inference as a frozen projection; achieves ~91% metrics on ResNet/CIFAR-100 and diffusion/CIFAR-10.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address provenance, reproducibility, and interpretability of AI-generated scientific outputs by providing an AI-native, compilable substrate that records generation histories and supports recursive analysis, intervention, replay, and validation.

Method: Formally define an atomic generation fact f=(u,τ,ω,z;ρ) and compile them into a Generation-Fact Graph (GFG). Establish a GFG-based recursive process (analysis, intervention, replay, validation). Use nanoGPT to realize unified training-learning dynamics, modeling training as an evolution of a parameter-optimizer system with state and memory; learning as reorganization of distributed support guided by target-specific states and update geometry. Introduce three coordinates (target-boundary state, target-specific update geometry, parameter-Adam receiving state) to build a second-order predictor. Treat inference as a frozen projection of training-learning dynamics. Include component gating and rollback, attention-driven organization, and controlled feedback. Validate on ResNet/CIFAR-100 and diffusion/CIFAR-10.

Result: On held-out runs, achieved 91.43% accuracy and 91.49% macro-averaged recall across four transitions. Observed receiving-state-conditioned responses, persistent support reorganization, and frozen inference projection beyond nanoGPT. Demonstrated causal recruitment via gating/rollback and generalized findings to ResNet/CIFAR-100 and diffusion/CIFAR-10.

Conclusion: The GFG framework provides an AI-native, compilable substrate for scientific generation histories, enabling analysis of training-learning dynamics and inference as a projection. The results reveal causal structure and potential double-edged reinforcement, with validation on standard benchmarks indicating robustness beyond nanoGPT.

Abstract: We define an atomic generation fact f=(u,tau,omega,z;rho), recording the origin, realized transformation, concrete occurrence, generated result and relation role. Compiled into a Generation-Fact Graph (GFG), these facts provide an AI-native, compilable scientific fact substrate preserving generation histories. We establish a GFG-based recursive scientific process in which analysis, intervention, replay and validation form facts for later cycles. Using nanoGPT, we establish unified training-learning dynamics. Training is the evolution of a parameter-optimizer system with state and memory: each actual training action enters the receiving state and produces a finite-amplitude nonlinear functional response conditioned by that state and target-specific update geometry. Learning is the persistent reorganization of distributed functional support by these responses; capability formation, maintenance, decline or recovery becomes observable when target-specific states are evaluated against their readout boundaries. Three primary coordinates - target-boundary state, target-specific update geometry and parameter-Adam receiving state - yield a second-order predictor operating before post-update outputs are read. On held-out runs, it achieved 91.43% accuracy and 91.49% macro-averaged recall across four transitions. We further establish inference as a frozen projection of training-learning dynamics. Component gating and rollback show causal recruitment and non-additive combination of query-conditioned support formed during training, deriving organizational conditions realized by Attention. Controlled feedback indicates possible double-edged reinforcement effects. ResNet/CIFAR-100 and diffusion/CIFAR-10 experiments confirm receiving-state-conditioned responses, persistent support reorganization and frozen inference projection beyond nanoGPT.

</details>


### [115] [A Critical Audit of Spatiotemporal Forecasting Benchmark Datasets and Baselines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20980)
*Kenneth Martin,Simon Heilig,Asja Fischer,Michel F. C. Haddad,Adam M. Sykulski,Moshe Eliasof*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Benchmarking bias in multivariate time-series GNNs: spatially-unaware linear models can rival GNNs on standard datasets; identifiable biases arise from first-order differencing; rigorous statistical evaluation and dataset diversification are recommended; results inform novel GNN architectures via a simple hybrid model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand why GNNs may not consistently outperform simple baselines on common benchmarks and to diagnose dataset biases that undermine the discriminative power of GNNs.

Method: Statistical evaluation of commonly used short-range forecasting datasets to quantify spatial and temporal correlations, assess the impact of first-order differencing, and identify structural biases; apply the insights to a simple hybrid model to illustrate how findings can guide GNN design.

Result: Demonstrates that spatially-unaware linear models are strong competitors on several benchmarks; uncovers significant spatial/temporal correlations and biases due to differencing; provides a toolkit for rigorous statistical evaluation and a pathway for integrating insights into model development.

Conclusion: Urges reduced reliance on current benchmark datasets for method comparison, advocates for rigorous statistical evaluation, and suggests that the insights can inform the design of more robust GNNs and more informative benchmarks.

Abstract: Graph neural networks (GNNs) are routinely employed for short-range forecasting on multivariate time series with a spatial graph structure. Despite the availability of many alternative datasets, method innovations within this domain are predominantly assessed against a rather limited set of benchmark datasets, most notably Chickenpox, PedalMe, WikiMaths, METR-LA, and PEMS-BAY. The evaluation protocols contain baselines spanning from historical averages to classical machine learning approaches. These baselines often show competitive performance compared to GNNs. In the present work, we take a step back and analyse the benchmark datasets via classical time series methods to uncover why spatially-unaware linear models pose a stronger competitor than previously reported, casting further doubt on the discriminative reliability of the aforementioned widely adopted datasets. Our statistical analysis provides a toolset for identifying significant spatial and temporal correlations, while revealing a structural bias introduced by first-order differenced datasets. We therefore recommend reducing the over-reliance on such datasets for method comparison, and instead advocate for more rigorous statistical evaluation. By applying the results of our analysis to a simple hybrid model, we show how our methodology can lead to novel ways of developing GNN models

</details>


### [116] [Jacobian-guided Noise Injection for Quantization Robustness in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20988)
*Deepanshu Pandey,Arnav Chavan,Nahush Lele,Sankalp Dayal,Deepak Gupta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces Jacobian-Guided Noise Injection to stabilize self-attention quantization by injecting noise into pre-attention logits with variance derived from the Jacobian Frobenius norm; yields improved robustness for low-bit quantized LLMs, with notable gains on vision and language benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantizing large language models is hampered by the sensitivity of the self-attention mechanism to discretization errors, especially due to the softmax bottleneck and outliers that lead to state-dependent Jacobians and unstable quantization.

Method: During training, inject zero-mean Gaussian noise into pre-attention logits. The variance of this noise is directly computed from the Frobenius norm of the Jacobian of the attention operation, aligning noise strength with local attention sensitivity. This is a principled alternative to heuristic or direct Jacobian penalties.

Result: Experiments on state-of-the-art LLMs show improved robustness over standard post-training quantization methods. Reported gains include up to 37% relative Top-1 accuracy improvement on ImageNet-1K for SigLIP and up to 40% relative perplexity improvement on WikiText for language models under low-bit quantization settings.

Conclusion: Suppressing attention Jacobian sensitivity via Jacobian-guided noise injection yields a principled, training-time strategy that enhances quantization stability and performance of LLMs under aggressive quantization, outperforming prior PTQ approaches.

Abstract: Quantization of Large Language Models (LLMs) is often hindered by the sensitivity of the self-attention mechanism to discretization errors. We identify the softmax operator as a bottleneck for quantization stability due to its sensitivity to outliers and state-dependent Jacobian. We theoretically establish that suppressing the norm of this Jacobian helps in bounding quantization-induced performance degradation. Based on this, we propose Jacobian-Guided Noise Injection, a training strategy that injects zero-mean Gaussian noise into pre-attention logits, with variance derived directly from the Jacobian Frobenius norm. Unlike prior approaches that rely on heuristic or penalise jacobian directly, our method provides a way to identify the optimal noise variance based on the local attention sensitivity. We evaluate the method on SOTA LLM architectures, where it demonstrates improved robustness over popular PTQ methods. Empirical analysis reveals that the proposed method gives up to +37% relative gains on Top-1 accuracy on ImageNet-1K for SigLIP and improves relative perplexity by upto 40% on WikiText for language models in low bit quantisation settings, proving the efficacy of the approach.

</details>


### [117] [Trojaning the Alignment: Stealthy Backdoor Attacks against Graph Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20991)
*Minhua Lin,Zhicheng Gao,Yilong Wang,Hanqing Lu,Xiang Zhang,Suhang Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: STAG is a stealthy backdoor attack on Graph Foundation Models (GFMs) for text-attributed graphs (TAGs) that aligns graph-language representations via a coupled graph-trigger generator and text-side soft prompt to steer both modalities to a target-text region; it achieves stealth by rendering trigger nodes as readable text and by regularizing the trigger subgraph to stay close to the original. Demonstrates effectiveness and stealth across multiple TAG datasets and GFMs; code is released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GFM backdoors on TAGs with cross-modal alignment are underexplored. Existing attacks target only one modality and fail to exploit the graph-language alignment constraint and stealth requirements inherent to TAGs.

Method: Joint optimization of a graph-trigger generator and a text-side soft prompt to produce triggers that shift both graph and text representations toward the same target-class text region. To enhance stealth, trigger nodes are rendered as readable text via candidate retrieval, and the attached subgraph is regularized to remain structurally close to the original subgraph.

Result: Extensive experiments on multiple TAG datasets and representative GFMs show effective backdoor performance and high stealth, with a publicly released implementation.

Conclusion: STAG demonstrates a coherent cross-modal backdoor vulnerability at the GFM-TAG interface, underscoring the need for defenses that consider joint graph-language trojans; it provides a practical attack pipeline and guidance for future defense research.

Abstract: Graph Foundation Models (GFMs) on text-attributed graphs (TAGs) align graph representations with language semantics to support transferable graph learning. Despite these advantages, the backdoor vulnerability of GFMs on TAGs remains insufficiently understood, especially under graph-language alignment, where graph and text representations are trained to constrain each other in a shared semantic space. Existing backdoor attacks mainly target either the graph side or the text side, treating the two modalities independently. This makes direct adaptation ineffective: graph-only triggers can be constrained by clean text semantics, while text-only triggers alter the language view but do not directly shift the graph representation being aligned and scored. TAGs also impose a stealth challenge because triggers are exposed as both node text and local graph structure, making incoherent trigger attributes or anomalous subgraphs easy to inspect or filter. In this paper, we propose STAG, a stealthy trojan attack framework designed for the graph-language alignment interface of GFMs on TAGs. STAG coordinates a graph-trigger generator with a text-side soft prompt so that trigger-attached graph representations and triggered text representations move toward the same target-class text region. To address TAG-specific stealthiness, STAG realizes trigger nodes as readable text through candidate retrieval and regularizes the trigger-attached subgraph so that its local structure remains close to the original subgraph. Extensive experiments on multiple TAG datasets and representative GFMs demonstrate the effectiveness and stealthiness of STAG. Our code is available at https://github.com/ventr1c/STAG.

</details>


### [118] [Free-Probability Kernels for Zero-Rollout Hyperparameter Selection in Reservoir Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20998)
*Sara Malacarne,Andrea Ceni,Claudio Gallicchio*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Deterministic, pilot-informed selection of leaky linear reservoirs using free-probability derived kernels to rank operating regimes from a short pilot, avoiding extensive rollouts while transferring across widths.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce hyperparameter search cost in reservoir computing by replacing costly, repeated rollouts with a deterministic surrogate that predicts regime stability and temporal processing behavior.

Method: Develop a pilot-informed selector for leaky linear RCs. Use free probability to compute cross-lag propagation coefficients that capture how inputs mix over time. In the large-width limit, these coefficients define a deterministic temporal kernel approximating the finite-reservoir feature geometry. Apply kernel ridge regression on a short labelled pilot sequence to rank candidate operating regimes, selecting a configuration that transfers across widths without instantiating or rolling out the reservoir.

Result: Zero-rollout selection almost matches exhaustive search in deployment score (0.772 vs 0.774) while avoiding extensive rollouts. With limited rollouts, the ranking dominates performance at all budgets and reaches exhaustive performance at 4.8% of rollout cost. On ETT datasets, five retained candidates recover the exhaustive point on three datasets; on multivariate cellular-traffic forecasting, 15 rollouts per cell match the exhaustive 462-rollout reference and outperform randomized or Bayesian methods at low budgets.

Conclusion: Free-probability kernels serve as deterministic surrogates for reservoir regime selection when validation rollouts are scarce, enabling efficient, portable hyperparameter tuning with performance close to exhaustive search and transferability across widths.

Abstract: Reservoir computing (RC) couples a fixed recurrent dynamical system with a trained lightweight readout, but this efficiency is partly lost during hyperparameter selection: the recurrent gain, input scale, and leakage rate determine the reservoir's stability and temporal processing regime and are usually tuned through many rollouts. We introduce a deterministic, pilot-informed selector for leaky linear reservoirs followed by coordinate-wise nonlinear features. Free probability yields cross-lag propagation coefficients that summarize how the reservoir mixes past inputs. In the large-width limit, these coefficients define a deterministic temporal kernel that approximates the finite-reservoir feature geometry. Kernel ridge regression on a short labelled pilot sequence therefore ranks candidate operating regimes without instantiating or rolling out a reservoir, and the selected configuration transfers across widths. Across ten synthetic temporal benchmarks, zero-rollout selection obtains a mean deployment score of $0.772$, compared with $0.774$ for exhaustive simulation-based search, while avoiding $156\,600$ selection rollouts. With a small rollout budget, the proposed ranking provides the strongest mean performance at every tested budget and reaches the exhaustive reference using $4.8\%$ of its rollout cost. On four public electricity-transformer-temperature (ETT) forecasting datasets, five retained candidates recover the exhaustive operating point on three datasets. On multivariate cellular-traffic forecasting, 15 rollouts per cell reach the 462-rollout exhaustive reference and outperform random search and Bayesian optimization at low budgets. These results position free-probability kernels as deterministic surrogates for selecting reservoir operating regimes when validation rollouts are scarce.

</details>


### [119] [RODE: A Radial-Orthogonal Decoupled Engine for Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21024)
*Guoxiang Xu,Bince Qu,Qi Sun,Cheng Zhuo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RODE decouples radial (norm) and directional updates for matrix optimization, improving performance and reducing full-model norms compared to Muon variants across language and vision tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Matrix-aware optimizers often couple weight norm dynamics with directional updates, causing norm growth that can alter later steps. There is a need for explicit, independent control of the Frobenius norm to stabilize and improve learning.

Method: RODE introduces two separate update channels: a scalar radial rule that updates the matrix Frobenius norm, and a directional channel that performs Newton–Schulz-conditioned updates in the tangent space of the parameter matrix.

Result: RODE outperforms Muon variants across two language-modeling and two image-classification tasks in direct comparisons, achieving lower full-model norms. In 1.5B GPT-scale experiments, it reduces loss from 4.145 to 3.346 and global norm from 11964 to 2183 relative to Muon RMS; fixed-radius RODE improves further. In Qwen3.5-9B full fine-tuning, RODE also dominates Muon variants on all four tasks, with top mean on GSM8K and MATH-500.

Conclusion: Decoupling radial and directional dynamics yields a more effective and controllable matrix optimization approach, offering better performance and norm control than prior Muon-based methods.

Abstract: Modern neural network training increasingly uses matrix-aware optimizers, yet their conditioned matrix step is typically added directly to the weight, jointly changing its norm and direction. This interaction matters because the current norm determines angular motion, while directional learning can drive norm growth and thereby alter later steps. We introduce RODE, which gives the radial and directional components separate update rules and step sizes. RODE explicitly updates the matrix Frobenius norm through a scalar radial rule, while its directional channel performs Newton--Schulz-conditioned updates in the tangent space. Controlled GPT-2 interventions show gains from both direct norm control and RODE's directional update. Across two language-modeling and two image-classification tasks, RODE outperforms both Muon variants in every direct comparison and ends with lower full-model norms. At 1.5B scale, using the learning rate transferred directly from the Qwen2-style LM sweep, RODE lowers loss from 4.145 to 3.346 and final global norm from 11964 to 2183 relative to Muon RMS, with fixed-radius RODE improving further. For Qwen3.5-9B full-parameter fine-tuning, all six optimizers use the same tuning budget and the same formal-training and evaluation settings; RODE outperforms both Muon variants on all four evaluation tasks and attains the highest mean on GSM8K and MATH-500. Thus, decoupling radial and directional dynamics offers a more effective and controllable approach to matrix optimization.

</details>


### [120] [Designing a Robust LLM-Based Evaluation System for Agentic AI in Drug Discovery Through Human Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21057)
*Emma Granqvist,Rocío Mercado,Samuel Genheden*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes an LLM-as-a-Judge framework to evaluate agentic drug-discovery outputs; defines four quality dimensions and Tool Call Correctness; validates alignment with experts; optimizes the judge via few-shot demonstrations; applies to 70 questions, revealing limitations and potential benefit from rewriting questions; offers a reusable evaluation template.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the bottleneck of evaluating open-ended, tool-augmented outputs in scientific domains, where traditional metrics fall short and expert evaluation is not scalable; require human-aligned, scalable judgments for drug-discovery assistants.

Method: Define four output-quality dimensions (Completeness, Relevancy, Structural Clarity, Scope Adherence) plus deterministic Tool Call Correctness checks; conduct human alignment study with five expert annotators comparing multiple candidate LLM judges (Gemini 3.1 Pro, Claude Opus 4.7, GPT-5, Llama 3.1 70B); select best-performing judge; improve alignment via few-shot demonstrations with human-annotated examples (boost from 0.80 to 0.86); apply optimized judge to 70 held-out questions; examine effects of informal phrasing and rewriting questions before querying the agent; provide a reusable template for human-aligned evaluation in scientific-agent systems.

Result: Alignment with human majority vote improved from 0.80 to 0.86 after few-shot optimization; 70 held-out questions reveal limitations and that rewriting questions may help more than harm; informal phrasing does not systematically degrade quality; framework yields practical, scalable evaluation of agentic systems in drug-discovery.

Conclusion: The framework offers a reusable, human-aligned evaluation template for agentic systems in scientific domains, enabling scalable assessment of open-ended outputs and guiding future improvements in LLM-based evaluation.

Abstract: Agentic large language model (LLM) systems are reshaping scientific workflows in chemistry and drug discovery, but evaluating their open-ended, tool-augmented outputs remains a fundamental bottleneck. Reference-based metrics such as BLEU and ROUGE fail to capture semantic correctness, while expert human evaluation does not scale to the iteration speed these systems demand. The LLM-as-a-Judge paradigm has emerged as a scalable alternative, but existing drug discovery benchmarks deploy LLM judges without validating their alignment with human experts. In this work, we present an LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation framework for ChatInvent, an agentic drug discovery assistant deployed at AstraZeneca, with four contributions. First, we define four output-quality evaluation dimensions---Completeness, Relevancy, Structural Clarity, and Scope Adherence---alongside deterministic Tool Call Correctness checks. Second, we validate the judge through a human alignment study with five expert annotators, comparing Gemini 3.1 Pro, Claude Opus 4.7, GPT-5, and Llama 3.1 70B as candidate judges. Third, we optimize the best-performing judge using few-shot demonstrations of human-annotated examples, improving alignment with the human majority vote from 0.80 to 0.86. Fourth, applying the optimized judge to 70 held-out questions, we surface concrete limitations and find that informal phrasings do not systematically degrade output quality; if anything, it is helpful to have the LLM rewrite the original question before querying the agent. Our framework provides a reusable template for human-aligned evaluation of agentic systems in scientific domains.

</details>


### [121] [TracingFlow: A Simulation-Free Trajectory Inference Framework Based on Second-Order Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21070)
*Yuhao Sun,Zekun Wu,Zixun Huang,Peijie Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces TracingFlow, a second-order flow-matching framework for DOAT that regresses neural-network-predicted acceleration fields to model momentum and delays in continuous system evolution from sparse snapshots, outperforming first-order OT in scRNA-seq data; shows improved trajectory fidelity and leverages lineage priors for biologically plausible dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: First-order OT with memoryless velocity fields cannot capture regulatory momentum or time-delayed responses in processes like cell differentiation; a more expressive, second-order dynamics framework is needed.

Method: Second-order dynamics via acceleration field regression using neural networks; solves the Dynamical Optimal Acceleration Transport (DOAT) in a simulation-free flow-matching setup; learns underlying force fields to model high-curvature transitions; integrates lineage tracing priors to constrain dynamics.

Result: Superior distributional reconstruction and trajectory faithfulness on complex synthetic and large-scale scRNA-seq datasets; capable of capturing nonlinear evolutions and high-curvature transitions; lineage priors help recover mathematically optimal yet biologically plausible dynamical structures.

Conclusion: Extending OT to second-order flow matching enhances expressiveness to model momentum and delays in biological processes, enabling more accurate trajectory inference in single-cell omics and related domains; potential for improved understanding of differentiation dynamics and regulatory networks.

Abstract: Inferring continuous system evolution from sparse temporal snapshots is a key challenge in generative modeling and single-cell omics. While Optimal Transport (OT) is popular, existing frameworks are largely restricted to first-order dynamics, assuming memoryless velocity fields. This limits expressiveness, as first-order systems fail to account for regulatory momentum and time-delayed responses inherent in processes like cell differentiation. Here, we introduce TracingFlow, a simulation-free Flow Matching framework generalizing to second-order dynamics. By using neural networks to regress the acceleration field, TracingFlow provides an exact, efficient solution to the Dynamical Optimal Acceleration Transport (DOAT) problem. Unlike first-order methods yielding over-smoothed trajectories, our second-order formulation captures high-curvature transitions and nonlinear evolutions by learning the underlying force fields. Evaluated on complex synthetic and large-scale scRNA-seq datasets, TracingFlow achieves superior accuracy in distributional reconstruction and trajectory faithfulness. Moreover, by integrating lineage tracing priors, it recovers dynamical structures that are both mathematically optimal and biologically plausible.

</details>


### [122] [Causal Modeling of Adverse Pregnancy Outcomes via Adaptive LLM Proposals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21079)
*Kavimayil P. Komarasamy,Saurabh Mathur,Ameet Soni,David M. Haas,Kristian Kersting,Sriraam Natarajan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A neurosymbolic framework combines LLM-guided hypothesis generation with data-driven empirical scoring to infer causal graphs for Adverse Pregnancy Outcomes (APOs). The LLM acts as an adaptive proposal distribution, proposing causal relations which are scored against real-world data; top-scoring graphs refine the LLM's context iteratively. The method is evaluated on a clinical APO dataset, reproducing expert-validated edges and uncovering additional plausible causal links.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Causal discovery in APOs is hampered by scarce data and incomplete domain knowledge. Pure data-driven methods underperform, and LLMs alone produce inconsistent results. A neurosymbolic approach leverages broad domain priors from LLMs while grounding them with empirical data to produce plausible, testable hypotheses.

Method: Iterative neurosymbolic loop: (1) the LLM generates a set of plausible causal hypotheses/graphs; (2) these graphs are scored against empirical APO data using predefined causal-scoring metrics; (3) high-scoring graphs update the LLM’s context (adaptive proposal distribution), guiding subsequent generations; (4) repeat until convergence or stopping criteria. Evaluation compares generated graphs to an expert-constructed causal graph on a real-world dataset, assessing recoveries of expert edges and discovery of novel plausible relations.

Result: The framework recovered all expert-validated edges and identified additional plausible causal relations not previously listed by experts, suggesting new potential intervention targets and insights.

Conclusion: Neurosymbolic integration of LLM priors with empirical scoring yields plausible, data-grounded causal hypotheses for APOs, achieving parity with expert graphs and extending them with novel, testable relations.

Abstract: Adverse Pregnancy Outcomes (APOs) such as preterm birth and gestational diabetes can have long-term consequences for both the mother and child, yet an understanding of their causes remains elusive. Causal discovery in this domain is especially challenging due to a paucity of data and incomplete domain knowledge. As a result, pure data-driven methods fail, and Large Language Model (LLM) outputs remain inconsistent or contradictory. We introduce a neurosymbolic framework for generating plausible causal hypotheses that iteratively combines the broad prior knowledge of LLMs with empirical scoring on data. Our method treats the LLM as an adaptive proposal distribution, generating hypotheses that are scored against empirical data; the resulting high-scoring graphs are then used to update the LLM's context, steering subsequent generations toward more promising regions of the hypothesis space. We evaluate our approach on a real-world clinical dataset for modeling APOs and their risk factors, comparing our results against an expert-constructed causal graph. Our method recovers all expert-validated edges and identifies additional plausible causal relations not previously listed by experts, potentially providing new insights for targeted interventions.

</details>


### [123] [FlatLand: Personalized Graph Federated Learning via Tailored Lorentz Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21096)
