*Jiahong Liu,Ram Samarth B B,Xinyu Fu,Menglin Yang,Weixi Zhang,Rex Ying,Irwin King*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FlatLand is a personalized federated learning method for graphs that embeds clients in a tailored Lorentz (hyperbolic) space, using decoupled time-like and space-like parameters to separately encode heterogeneity and shared knowledge, enabling direct aggregation without client similarity estimation; it shows superior performance, especially in low-dimensional settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Heterogeneous client graph data in federated learning remain challenging; existing PFL methods ignore intrinsic geometric properties of diverse graphs. Hyperbolic geometry naturally captures graphs' negative curvature, and a time-like dimension provides a principled way to encode client-specific heterogeneity.

Method: Embed each client's data into a tailored Lorentz space; implement a parameter decoupling strategy that separates heterogeneous information (time-like parameters) from common knowledge (space-like parameters); enable direct aggregation without client similarity estimation or extra modules.

Result: Empirical results on diverse federated graph learning tasks show that FlatLand achieves superior performance, particularly in low-dimensional settings.

Conclusion: Lorentz-space-based PFL effectively encodes graph heterogeneity and shared knowledge, enabling simple yet powerful aggregation; time-like dimensions encode heterogeneity, space-like dimensions capture common information, and low-dimensional embeddings perform well.

Abstract: Federated learning enables privacy-preserving collaborative training, but highly heterogeneous client data remain challenging, especially in graph federated learning where clients possess structurally diverse graphs. Existing personalized federated learning (PFL) methods ignore the intrinsic geometric properties of diverse graph structures. We propose FlatLand, a novel personalized federated learning method that embeds different clients' data in tailored Lorentz space of hyperbolic geometry. Our key insight is that hyperbolic geometry naturally accommodates the intrinsic negative curvature prevalent in real-world graphs, while the time-like dimension in Lorentz space provides a principled way to encode client-specific heterogeneity. We develop a parameter decoupling strategy that separates heterogeneous information (captured in time-like parameters) from common knowledge (preserved in space-like parameters), enabling direct aggregation without requiring client similarity estimation and extra calculation modules. Empirical results on diverse federated graph learning tasks demonstrate that FlatLand achieves superior performance, particularly in low-dimensional settings.

</details>


### [124] [BackDFL: A Unified Benchmark For Backdoor Attacks and Defenses In Decentralized Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21137)
*Mouhamed Amine Bouchiha,Gregory Blanc,Yufei Han*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BackDFL introduces a unified benchmark to evaluate decentralized federated learning under adaptive backdoor attacks and shows that state-of-the-art defenses often fail at modest malicious participation, with robustness highly sensitive to data heterogeneity and communication topology.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DFL replaces centralized aggregation, which alters the threat landscape. Prior robustness work relies on narrow threat models and fragmented evaluations, likely overestimating security. A realistic benchmark is needed to reveal true failure modes.

Method: Proposes BackDFL benchmark; conducts extensive experiments with adaptive attackers across multiple communication graphs and data heterogeneity settings; evaluates Byzantine-robust DFL methods and adapted FL backdoor defenses.

Result: BackDFL exposes critical failure modes: defenses fail at malicious participation rates as low as 15%, especially under data heterogeneity; robustness varies significantly with communication topology.

Conclusion: Standardized, realistic evaluation frameworks for DFL are essential; defenses must account for graph topology and non-iid data; results caution against assuming FL-style defenses transfer directly to DFL; more robust and topology-aware solutions are needed.

Abstract: Decentralized Federated Learning (DFL) promises trust-free collaborative learning by replacing the centralized parameter server with peer-to-peer model exchange. However, this architectural shift fundamentally reshapes the threat landscape. Without globally coordinated aggregation, DFL becomes particularly susceptible to backdoor attacks, in which malicious participants implant persistent hidden behaviors while maintaining high clean-task performance. In this paper, we argue that the robustness of DFL has been significantly overestimated. Existing studies rely on simplified threat models, non-adaptive adversaries, fragmented evaluation protocols, inconsistent communication topologies, and ad hoc training configurations, leading to an incomplete understanding of DFL security. To address these limitations, we present BackDFL, a unified benchmark for systematically evaluating DFL under realistic and adaptive backdoor attacks. Through extensive experiments, BackDFL exposes critical failure modes of decentralized learning. Our results demonstrate that both state-of-the-art Byzantine-robust DFL methods and adapted FL backdoor defenses fail under modest malicious participation rates (as low as 15%), especially in heterogeneous settings, while their robustness varies substantially across communication graph topologies.

</details>


### [125] [COEC: Calibrated Orthogonal-Equivalence Compensation for Structured Pruning of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21142)
*Peiqi Yu,Nam Ling,Wei Wang,Wei Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A training-free compensation framework for structured pruning in LLMs, COEC, uses alternating left-right orthogonal rotations on retained weights, with right rotation on a reduced Stiefel manifold and singular-value scaling via generalized cross-validation. It tempers the calibration Gram matrix and adds an alignment penalty to preserve geometry between adjacent attention projections, relying on second-order statistics from a small calibration set. It is model- and pruning-criterion-agnostic, requiring no backpropagation or retraining, and improves perplexity and zero-shot accuracy, especially at higher sparsity, across Llama-3, Llama-3.1, and Qwen2.5.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Structured pruning reduces model size and inference cost but introduces output error. Existing training-free compensations (additive bias or a single orthogonal rotation) leave the input singular frame unchanged, limiting how the retained weights can adapt after column removal.

Method: Alternating left and right orthogonal rotations on the retained weight. The right rotation is optimized on a reduced Stiefel manifold; singular values are rescaled using generalized cross-validation to adapt regularization per layer. Introduces calibration Gram-matrix tempering to reduce dominance of high-energy activation directions and an alignment penalty to preserve the geometric relation between adjacent attention projections. All components rely on second-order statistics from a small calibration set and require no backpropagation or retraining. Independent of the column pruning criterion, applicable across multiple structured pruning methods.

Result: Empirical evaluation on Llama-3, Llama-3.1, and Qwen2.5 across various structured sparsity levels shows COEC improves perplexity and zero-shot accuracy relative to existing compensation methods, with larger gains at higher sparsity.

Conclusion: Post-pruning compensation can recover part of the performance lost to column removal; COEC provides a training-free, model-agnostic approach that improves key metrics across diverse models and pruning schemes.

Abstract: Structured pruning reduces the size and inference cost of large language models (LLMs) by removing weight columns, but the resulting output error can degrade accuracy. Existing training-free compensation methods use an additive bias or a single orthogonal rotation on the output side of the retained weight. These corrections leave its input singular frame unchanged and therefore limit how the retained weight can adapt after column removal. We propose COEC (Calibrated Orthogonal-Equivalence Compensation), a training-free compensation framework that applies alternating left and right orthogonal rotations to the retained weight. The right rotation is optimized on a reduced Stiefel manifold, while singular values are rescaled using generalized cross-validation to select the regularization strength for each layer. COEC further tempers the calibration Gram matrix to reduce the dominance of high-energy activation directions and introduces an alignment penalty that preserves the geometric relation between adjacent attention projections.All components use second-order statistics from a small calibration set and require neither backpropagation through the LLM nor retraining of the model parameters. COEC is independent of the column pruning criterion and can be applied to multiple structured pruning methods. Experiments on the Llama-3, Llama-3.1, and Qwen2.5 model families across multiple structured sparsity levels show that COEC improves perplexity on every model and zero-shot accuracy in most settings over existing compensation methods, with larger gains at higher sparsity. These results show that post-pruning compensation can recover part of the performance lost to column removal.

</details>


### [126] [Capturing Cardiac Cyclicity through Phase-Equivariant Self-Supervised Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21147)
*Blaise Delaney,Dominic Dootson,Juan Jose Juan Castella,Salil Patel,Andrew Pfaff,Yuji Xing,Jonny Hancox,Karin Sevegnani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Phase-equivariant self-supervised learning for ECG-like signals. Winder uses a fixed transport operator to separate phase-invariant and phase-rotating components, achieving competitive accuracy with ~1M parameters on PTB-XL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Leverage cyclic physiological structure (cardiac cycle) to design a phase-equivariant objective and an efficient joint-embedding architecture.

Method: Winder architecture with phase-invariant coordinates and phase-rotating harmonic subspaces; a fixed, closed-form transport operator derived from the cycle geometry (no learned parameters); evaluation on PTB-XL with a frozen linear probe.

Result: Diagnostic accuracy comparable to state-of-the-art SSL methods at around 1M parameters; latent space exhibits phase-equivariant geometry and is interpretable.

Conclusion: Explicitly encoding cardiac-phase symmetry preserves diagnostically useful information while producing a parameter-efficient latent representation directly linked to measurable physiology.

Abstract: The cyclic structure of physiological processes offers a natural prior for self-supervised representation learning, and the cardiac cycle provides a particularly well-defined setting in which to exploit it. We derive a phase-equivariant self-supervised objective and introduce Winder, a joint-embedding architecture that organises representations into phase-invariant coordinates and phase-rotating harmonic subspaces. Its transport operator is fixed and closed-form, derived from the cycle's geometry rather than learned, and adds no parameters. Evaluated on PTB-XL under a frozen linear-probe protocol, Winder attains diagnostic accuracy within the range reported by state-of-the-art self-supervised methods at a ~1 M parameter footprint, while exhibiting phase-equivariant latent geometry. These findings demonstrate that explicitly encoding cardiac-phase symmetry can preserve diagnostically useful information while yielding a latent geometry that is legible, parameter-efficient, and directly tied to a measurable physiological quantity.

</details>


### [127] [Thermo-FL: Thermal-Aware Robust Federated Fine-Tuning of Large Language Models for Edge AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21172)
*Shiva Shrestha,Kazi Shaharair Sharif,Zongxing Xie,Jiajing Huang,Anhao Xiang,Honghui Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Thermo-FL fuses thermal-aware client control with a robust sparse-aggregation pipeline (TERRA) to securely and efficiently fine-tune LLMs via federated LoRA on edge devices, addressing thermal throttling and adversarial update threats.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated fine-tuning on edge devices faces hardware instability (thermal throttling) and adversarial updates (Byzantine/MITM). A unified approach is needed to regulate compute, manage sparse communications, and ensure robust aggregation.

Method: Client-side: use device temperature to adjust active LoRA-layer fraction and update-density; Server-side: TERRA pipeline with norm filtering, mask-aware directional validation, adaptive active-coordinate clipping, and mask-aware aggregation for dynamically sparse updates; evaluation on a large-scale emulator and a Jetson-based physical testbed, with bitmap sparse encoding to reduce upload size.

Result: Emulator: improved robustness under adversarial sparse aggregation and strongest BoolQ accuracy across clean/attack settings; GSM8K competitive. Physical testbed: stabilized temperature, reduced upload size via bitmap sparse encoding, preserved GSM8K utility under sign-flip/scale and MITM perturbations.

Conclusion: Secure edge LLM adaptation should jointly address hardware behavior, workload regulation, sparse communication, and aggregation robustness to sustain performance under adversarial and environmental stresses.

Abstract: Federated fine-tuning enables large language models to adapt on edge devices without centralizing private data, but practical deployments must address hardware instability and adversarial update corruption together. Thermally constrained clients may throttle, slow local training, or delay synchronous aggregation, while Byzantine clients and communication-layer adversaries can corrupt the updates used to form the global model. To address these challenges, we present Thermo-FL, a thermal-aware federated LoRA fine-tuning framework that uses device temperature as an active control signal for local adapter training and sparse update transmission. On the client side, Thermo-FL adjusts the active LoRA-layer fraction and transmitted update density as devices heat or cool, reducing workload under thermal stress. On the server side, Thermo-FL introduces TERRA, a robust aggregation pipeline for dynamically sparse LoRA updates that combines norm filtering, mask-aware directional validation, adaptive active-coordinate clipping, and mask-aware aggregation. We evaluate Thermo-FL using both a large-scale emulator and a Jetson-based physical testbed. In the emulator, Thermo-FL improves robustness under adversarial sparse aggregation and achieves the strongest BoolQ accuracy across clean and attack settings while remaining competitive on GSM8K. In the physical prototype, Thermo-FL stabilizes device temperature, reduces compressed upload size through bitmap sparse encoding, and preserves GSM8K utility under sign-flip/scale and MITM perturbations. These results show that secure edge LLM adaptation should jointly consider hardware behavior, workload regulation, sparse communication, and aggregation robustness.

</details>


### [128] [A Neurosymbolic Approach for Constructing Planning Domain Models from Clinical Narratives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21186)
*Ranveer Singh,Saurabh Mathur,Michael Skinner,Prasad Tadepalli,Kristian Kersting,Sriraam Natarajan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NSPIN is a neurosymbolic framework that learns probabilistic planning models from unstructured surgical narratives by extracting events with a pretrained LLM, inducing a PPDDL model, and refining it with LLM-driven revisions, achieving generalization to unseen notes and alignment with surgical practice.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Formalizing surgical workflows for decision support is hard due to sparse structured data and implicit actions in clinical narratives. A neurosymbolic approach that combines LLM-driven extraction with symbolic planning can produce actionable, generalizable models.

Method: 1) Use a pretrained LLM to extract/impute structured event sequences from raw clinical notes. 2) Induce a probabilistic planning domain model (PPDDL) from these sequences. 3) Propose LLM-driven revisions to preconditions and refine them, guided by empirical validation. 4) Evaluate on a corpus of laparoscopic appendectomy notes from multiple surgeons.

Result: NSPIN yields probabilistic planning models that generalize to unseen notes. Expert clinical review finds the induced knowledge largely consistent with actual surgical practice.

Conclusion: A neurosymbolic pipeline can effectively derive actionable probabilistic planning models from unstructured clinical narratives, enabling better decision support in complex surgical workflows with demonstrated generalization and clinical alignment.

Abstract: Surgical procedures such as laparoscopic appendectomy are complex, high-stakes processes, yet formalizing their workflows for decision support remains a significant challenge. Inducing probabilistic planning domain models in this setting is particularly difficult due to the lack of structured event data and the prevalence of implicit actions in clinical narratives, which neither empirical symbolic methods nor Large Language Models (LLMs) can adequately address on their own. We introduce NSPIN, a neurosymbolic framework for inducing probabilistic planning domain models from unstructured clinical narratives. Our method extracts and imputes structured event sequences from raw text using a pretrained LLM, then induces a PPDDL model and refines its preconditions with LLM-proposed revisions, guided by empirical validation. We evaluate the approach on 2,660 laparoscopic appendectomy notes written by 9 surgeons. NSPIN yields models that generalize to unseen notes, and expert clinical review indicates its induced knowledge is largely consistent with surgical practice.

</details>


### [129] [Tydra: An Efficient Hybrid Model for Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21199)
*Mieszko Komisarczyk,Saurabh Mathur,Maurice Kraus,Sriraam Natarajan,Kristian Kersting*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A hybrid Transformer-SSM model (Tydra) for tabular in-context learning reduces inference cost while maintaining accuracy, outperforming a larger Hydra model on 30 OpenML datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To combine the high accuracy of transformer-based tabular models (e.g., TabPFN) with the efficiency of subquadratic SSM-based approaches (e.g., Hydra), addressing quadratic cost in context length.

Method: Introduce Tydra, a hybrid architecture interleaving attention (Transformer) and State Space Model (SSM) layers for tabular in-context learning. Evaluate on 30 OpenML datasets against TabPFN and Hydra, comparing inference time and predictive performance.

Result: Inference time reduced by ~30% relative to TabPFN while preserving much of TabPFN’s predictive performance; Tydra outperforms Hydra, which is about ten times larger, while offering faster inference.

Conclusion: Hybrid Transformer-SSM architectures are a promising direction for tabular foundation models, achieving a favorable trade-off between accuracy and efficiency.

Abstract: Transformer-based tabular foundation models such as TabPFN achieve strong predictive performance but incur quadratic computational cost with context length. On the other hand, subquadratic SSM-based alternatives such as Hydra trade away accuracy for efficiency. To balance both, we introduce Tydra, a hybrid Transformer-State Space Model (SSM) architecture for tabular in-context learning that interleaves attention and SSM layers. Across 30 OpenML datasets, Tydra reduces inference time by 30% relative to TabPFN while retaining much of its predictive performance. Tydra also outperforms an approximately ten-times-larger Hydra model while providing faster inference. The results indicate that hybrid architectures are a promising direction for tabular foundation models.

</details>


### [130] [Curriculum-Aware Interpolate-then-Refine: Learned Physiological Time-Series Imputation under Realistic Missingness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21207)
*Yu-Chao Huang,Haochen Zhang,Nicholas Konz,Tianlong Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A two-stage, curriculum-aware imputation framework (CAIR) improves physiological time-series imputation by first predicting a coarse base curve with a bidirectional-GRU interpolator and then refining it via a Transformer across three passes, trained under a random-gap curriculum to reflect realistic physiologic missingness and extreme events.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Physiological signals exhibit missingness tied to clinically extreme values and highly variable gap lengths. Generic imputers fail to capture these patterns, leading to poor practical performance and misleading reconstructions that do not preserve clinically relevant burden metrics.

Method: Two-stage architecture: (1) bidirectional-GRU interpolator to generate a base curve; (2) Transformer refiner that iteratively corrects its estimate over three passes. Trained jointly under a broad, signal-agnostic random-gap curriculum. Evaluated by stratifying imputers by gap length and missingness mechanism (MCAR, MAR, NMAR) on AI-READI (continuous glucose) and MIMIC-III arterial pressure datasets.

Result: CAIR achieves the highest accuracy across all missingness mechanisms, with margins over the strongest baselines increasing with difficulty (9% under MCAR to 19% under value-dependent dropout). It outperforms baselines on both datasets; also shows that low reconstruction error does not guarantee preservation of clinically relevant burden metrics, and methods matching CAIR’s error fail to preserve those metrics.

Conclusion: A curriculum-aware, multi-pass imputation framework that learns a coarse base curve and iteratively refines it yields robust, clinically meaningful imputation for physiological time series, outperforming generic imputers across diverse missingness mechanisms and gap lengths; evaluation should consider task-relevant burden metrics in addition to traditional reconstruction error.

Abstract: Imputing physiological time series (arterial blood pressure, blood glucose, etc.) is essential for addressing the missingness that pervades clinical data. Yet modern imputation methods perform poorly in this domain: a recent benchmark found that simple linear interpolation outperformed every learned imputer on real-world clinical signals with realistic gaps. We show that this reflects two properties of physiological missingness that generic imputers ignore: gaps may occur when the signal is clinically extreme rather than typical, and gap lengths can easily span orders of magnitude. To this end, we introduce Curriculum-Aware Interpolate-then-Refine (CAIR), a two-stage framework for physiological time-series imputation. Our key motivation is to learn a coarse base curve and then repeatedly correct it toward physiological realism, rather than predict a gap in a single pass. Consequently, CAIR couples a bidirectional-GRU interpolator with a Transformer refiner that corrects its own estimate over three successive passes, trained jointly under a broad, signal-agnostic random-gap curriculum. We evaluate imputers stratified by gap length and missingness mechanism (MCAR, MAR, NMAR) rather than by a single average, and CAIR is the most accurate under every mechanism on continuous glucose monitoring (AI-READI) and arterial pressure in intensive care (MIMIC-III). Its margin over the strongest baseline grows with difficulty, from 9% under MCAR to 19% under value-dependent dropout, where generic learned imputers are weakest. We further show low reconstruction error alone does not recover the burden metrics clinicians act on: interpolants matching CAIR's error fail to preserve those metrics, imputers that recover them are far less accurate, and CAIR alone ranks among the best on both axes.

</details>


### [131] [TRACE-C: Rank-Calibrated Relational Anomaly Detection for Multi-Stream Operational Telemetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21251)
*Matthew Faucher*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TRACE-C is a strictly-prior rank-calibrated detector for aligned multi-stream telemetry; it fuses three window channels (local max-normalized sum, Gaussian-copula-form dependence on robust-z residuals, and AR(1) innovation) and Fisher-aggregates channel ranks to detect joint anomalies; evaluation on Great Britain grid data shows mixed results and highlights interpretive limits: p-values are selection quantities, copula channel is not a literal copula, and results are diagnostics rather than formal inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To detect joint anomalies in multi-stream telemetry that are not apparent when examining streams individually, with auditable, priors-based calibration and thorough interpretability controls.

Method: Three-channel fusion detector TRACE-C using same-regime rolling median/MAD residuals, three channels (max normalized local sum, copula-like dependence contrast on robust-z residuals, worst standardized AR(1) innovation); channel ranks are Fisher-aggregated and ranked against earlier aggregates; evaluated on six GB grid streams with 2019 fit, 2019 development, 2020 hold-out; ablation experiments show copula channel effects; results and interpretability notes.

Result: In 2019, TRACE-C ranks Storm Atiyah first overall, but ablation reveals this is driven by the local channel; copula-only ranks Atiyah 59th. The 9 Aug event is ranked 143 by fused detector vs 40 by temporal channel alone; reconstruction baselines rank it first. In 2020, no window selected; highest-ranked frozen window later linked to Storm Ellen.

Conclusion: Three interpretive limits persist: p-values are selection quantities, not event probabilities; copula-form channel is not a literal copula density; empirical ranks are diagnostics, not coverage or FDR proofs; all outputs are derived from committed machine-readable reports.

Abstract: Operational telemetry can be jointly anomalous while every individual stream stays inside its familiar range. TRACE-C is an auditable strictly-prior rank-calibrated detector for aligned multi-stream telemetry: same-regime rolling median/MAD residuals feed three window channels -- a maximum normalized local sum, a Gaussian copula-form dependence contrast on robust-z residuals, and a worst standardized AR(1) innovation -- whose channel ranks are Fisher-aggregated and ranked against earlier aggregates.
  We evaluate six Great Britain grid streams with a January-April 2019 fit, July-December 2019 development evidence, and a 2020 hold-out frozen before inspection. TRACE-C ranks Storm Atiyah first among 2019 test windows, but a disclosed channel ablation attributes that rank to the local channel, not the copula-form channel: copula-only ranks Atiyah 59th. The short 9 August frequency event is ranked far lower by the fused detector (143) than by the temporal channel alone (40), and reconstruction baselines rank it first. In 2020 no window is selected, which is consistent with record-rule saturation rather than an uneventful year; the highest-ranked frozen window was later interpreted as Storm Ellen.
  Three interpretive limits carry throughout. The resulting p-values are selection quantities, not event probabilities. The copula-form channel is not a literal copula density: the method applies no probability-integral or normal-score transform. Empirical rank counts are diagnostics, not coverage or false-discovery proofs. Every table and figure in this paper is generated from committed machine-readable reports.

</details>


### [132] [ConceptTS: LLM-Guided Concept Bottlenecks for Interpretable Multivariate Time-Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21277)
*Yichen Jiang,Yueqiao Chen,Dongyu Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces ConceptTS, an interpretable multivariate time-series forecasting framework that leverages a large language model to propose task-relevant concepts and executable labeling rules, organizing predictions around historical context, local forecast intervals, and the full horizon; it achieves competitive accuracy with black-box baselines while yielding semantically meaningful concept activations on air-quality data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the opacity of state-of-the-art multivariate forecasters by injecting interpretability through human-readable concepts, derived with minimal manual labeling using an LLM.

Method: Use a large language model to propose task-relevant concepts and generate executable labeling rules, translating domain knowledge into supervision. Organize concepts into three bottlenecks (historical context, local forecast intervals, full horizon). Employ a shared decoder that combines predicted activations to form the forecast, enabling concept-level interventions.

Result: Empirical evaluation on the Beijing Multi-Site Air Quality dataset shows ConceptTS achieves accuracy competitive with strong black-box baselines while producing semantically meaningful concept activations.

Conclusion: ConceptTS demonstrates that interpretable, concept-driven forecasting can match black-box performance and provide transparent, controllable predictions via concept activations.

Abstract: State-of-the-art multivariate time-series forecasters can model complex temporal and cross-variable dependencies, yet their opaque representations provide limited insight into why a particular forecast is produced. This lack of transparency restricts their use in settings where practitioners must understand and assess the factors underlying a prediction. We introduce ConceptTS, an interpretable forecasting framework that organizes its predictions around named, human-readable concepts. ConceptTS uses a large language model to propose task-relevant concepts and generate executable labeling rules, translating the language model's domain knowledge into direct supervision without costly manual concept annotation. The proposed concepts are organized into three complementary bottlenecks that describe the historical context, local forecast intervals, and the full forecast horizon. A shared decoder combines representations derived from their predicted activations to construct the forecast, making the model's decision process explicit and supporting direct concept-level interventions. Experiments on the Beijing Multi-Site Air Quality dataset show that ConceptTS achieves accuracy competitive with strong black-box baselines while producing semantically meaningful concept activations.

</details>


### [133] [SPARCL: Spectral Partitioned Analytic Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21307)
*James Hartley,Zeropy Surio,Daniel Whitmore,Hannah Clarke,Thomas Reed*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SPARCL provides a spectral-partitioned analytic continual learner that freezes a high-energy core of the running autocorrelation and updates only the residual via recursive least squares, yielding invariant core logits and closing much of the gap to representation-based methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Analytic continual learning avoids gradient-based optimization but still experiences forgetting. The shared inverse autocorrelation across tasks causes spectral interference; old-class logits drift when new task samples load onto dominant eigen-directions.

Method: Decompose the running autocorrelation into a high-energy core and a residual; freeze core classifier components for old classes; update the residual block with recursive least squares; optionally expand the residual with a random projection; yields a closed-form update with an invariance guarantee for the core contribution.

Result: Empirical evaluation on CIFAR-100, CUB-200, ImageNet-R, and ImageNet-A under a frozen ViT-B/16 backbone shows SPARCL substantially narrows the gap to strong representation-matching learners and complements sparse decorrelation approaches like Fly-CL.

Conclusion: Spectral partitioning provides a simple, provably invariant update for analytic continual learning, enabling effective retention of old-class information while maintaining online adaptability.

Abstract: Analytic continual learning has emerged as a strong exemplar-free alternative to gradient-based class-incremental learning because it replaces iterative optimization with closed-form ridge updates. Yet the usual forgetting narrative, centered on stochastic gradient overwriting, does not explain why analytic methods still drift on old classes despite exact recursive solvers. We identify the culprit as spectral interference: the joint ridge classifier for all tasks shares the inverse autocorrelation operator $(R+λI)^{-1}$, so incoming task samples that load onto old dominant eigendirections dilute the spectrum and perturb old-class logits even when old labels are never revisited. Based on this view, we propose SPARCL, a spectral partitioned analytic continual learner that decomposes the running autocorrelation into a high-energy core and a residual complement, freezes old-class classifier components in the core subspace, and updates only the residual block through recursive least squares with an optional residual random-projection expansion. This yields a simple closed-form update with a provable invariance guarantee for the core contribution of old logits. Across CIFAR-100, CUB-200, ImageNet-R, and ImageNet-A under a frozen ViT-B/16 protocol, SPARCL closes most of the gap from classical analytic learners to strong representation matchers, while remaining complementary to sparse feature-decorrelation approaches such as Fly-CL.

</details>


### [134] [Rethinking Expressivity and Efficiency in Test-Time Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21308)
*Zeyun Zhong,Joya Chen,Manuel Martin,Frederik Diederichs,Juergen Gall,Juergen Beyerer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: E^2-TTT introduces a closed-form state transition for test-time training that preserves per-token update dynamics while enabling fully parallel chunk-wise training, achieving strong length extrapolation and competitive throughput; code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reconcile the expressive power of per-token update dynamics in test-time training with the hardware efficiency of chunk-wise inference, which current methods struggle to balance.

Method: Derives a closed-form state transition by assuming gradients are evaluated at chunk-start weights, enabling exact reproduction of the chunk-end fast-weight and momentum states of the per-token recurrence; enables fully parallelized chunk-level training while preserving temporal update structure.

Result: Empirically trains models up to 1.3B parameters from scratch. On language modeling, matches prior TTT and hybrid-attention baselines; outperforms them on in-context retrieval. Shows pronounced gains in length extrapolation, retaining >90% accuracy at 8x context length on Needle in a Haystack. Throughput matches efficient chunk-wise methods.

Conclusion: E^2-TTT effectively bridges expressivity and efficiency in test-time training, enabling expressive per-token update dynamics with chunk-wise parallelism, and is validated by large-scale experiments and an open-source implementation.

Abstract: Test-Time Training (TTT) enables long-context processing via continuous weight updates during inference, but current methods struggle to balance the expressivity of per-token update dynamics with the hardware efficiency of chunk-wise approximations. We propose E$^2$-TTT (Expressive and Efficient TTT) to bridge this gap. Under the standard approximation of taking gradients at the chunk-start weights, we derive a closed-form state transition that exactly reproduces the chunk-end fast-weight and momentum states of the per-token recurrence. This enables fully parallelized chunk-level training while preserving the temporal structure of the update rule that prior chunk-wise methods discard. We validate E$^2$-TTT by training models up to 1.3B parameters from scratch. It performs on par with previous TTT and hybrid attention baselines in language modeling while outperforming them on in-context retrieval. Its advantage is most pronounced in length extrapolation: on the standard ``Needle in a Haystack'' passkey test, it retains over 90% accuracy at $8\times$ the training context length. Meanwhile, E$^2$-TTT can match the training throughput of efficient chunk-wise methods, demonstrating that it effectively reconciles expressivity with efficiency. The code is available at https://github.com/zeyun-zhong/E2-TTT.

</details>


### [135] [Time-Aware Tranformer-Based Prediction Model for AECOPD](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21324)
*Weihao Qu,Ling Zheng,Dongyang Wang,Jiacun Wang,Haowen Pan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A Time-Aware Transformer is proposed to predict AECOPD from daily ventilator respiratory data to enable timely, low-latency detection, outperforming traditional methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While AECOPD requires rapid detection, conventional ML models rely on clinical/lab data causing latency; home-monitoring ventilator data offers timely signals.

Method: Introduce a Time-Aware Transformer to extract meaningful patient representations from time-stamped ventilator data and capture temporal symptom progression for classification tasks.

Result: The proposed model outperforms traditional methods on multiple classification tasks, demonstrating improved AECOPD prediction accuracy in a home monitoring setting.

Conclusion: Time-Aware Transformer-based approaches can enhance timely AECOPD prediction using home ventilator data, potentially reducing latency in early detection.

Abstract: The rapid symptom change of Acute exacerbation of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (AECOPD) makes it critical to have time-sensitive prediction models. However, most current machine learning models studying AECOPD use clinical and laboratory data, which will inevitably cause latency. To ensure timely detection of AECOPD and minimize latency, this paper focuses on home monitoring scenarios where only respiratory data from daily-use ventilators is available. We introduce a Time-Aware transformer-based AECOPD prediction model, which generates meaningful patient representations using the Time-Aware transformer to capture the symptoms and their temporal progression in ventilator data. Our experimental results demonstrate that our Time-Aware transformer-based approach outperforms traditional methods in multiple classification tasks, highlighting its potential to enhance AECOPD prediction accuracy.

</details>


### [136] [Across-Design Uncertainty in Short Pricing Panels: Evidence from Simulated Price Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21334)
*Pedro Cadahia Delgado*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Sparse-price observational panels have most estimation error due to across-design variation rather than realized price trajectories; inference benefits from independent variation across units rather than fixed passive panels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand how inference is affected when panels contain many observations but few distinct price movements and how within-panel resampling misses across-design variation.

Method: Synthetic data-generating process calibrated to a sparse pricing regime; decompose estimation error into within-trajectory uncertainty and across-trajectory design variation; baseline simulations with gradient-boosted models; analyze dispersion across designs; examine regions sharing price paths; apply Paule-Mandel variance component.

Result: Across-design variation dominates estimation error (97.6% for gradient-boosted spec). Empirical dispersion follows sigma_hat ≈ 0.182 V^(-0.271), with V = moves × magnitude^2. Regions with common price paths reduce noise but do not yield independent trajectories; averaging across units with independent design-specific errors achieves dispersion reduction at the standard sqrt(rate). Paule-Mandel variance component across independent units substantially increases empirical coverage (0.469→0.931) in homogeneous simulations.

Conclusion: Design data-generating processes that create independent identifying variation is crucial; relying on fixed passive panels underestimates dispersion and overstates inference precision. Inference should account for across-design heterogeneity, and methods that leverage independent design variation improve coverage.

Abstract: Short observational pricing panels can contain many observations while offering only a small number of distinct price movements. This paper studies the inferential consequences of that distinction in a synthetic data-generating process calibrated to a sparse pricing regime. We separate uncertainty conditional on a realised price trajectory from variation in estimation error across alternative trajectories generated by the same pricing process. In the baseline simulations, the latter component accounts for 97.6% of the variance of estimation error for the gradient-boosted specification. Within-panel resampling procedures use the information of one realised trajectory and do not identify this across-design component.
  Three results organise the analysis. First, across-design dispersion is well described by the empirical relation sigma_hat approx 0.182 V^(-0.271), where V equals moves times magnitude squared. Second, adding regions sharing a common price path reduces outcome noise but does not create independent price trajectories; conversely, averaging across units with independent design-specific errors reduces dispersion at the standard square root rate. Third, a Paule-Mandel variance component estimated across independently priced units substantially increases empirical coverage in homogeneous simulations, from 0.469 to 0.931. The broader implication is a shift toward designing data-generating processes that create independent identifying variation rather than relying solely on fixed passive panels.

</details>


### [137] [Asymmetric Capacity Allocation in Self-Refinement Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21345)
*Zhuoyi Yang,Ian G. Harris,Salar Hashemitaheri,Cassie Huang,Yuangang Li,Hyunwoo Oh,Paul Dourish,Tony Givargis,Mohsen Imani,Li Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Stage-wise model-size analysis of self-refinement pipelines shows non-uniform capacity is beneficial: larger generators and refiners help, critics’ size is less critical but any critic outperforms no critique; undersized refiner can hurt.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how model size affects generation, critique, and revision within self-refinement, enabling more resource-efficient design.

Method: Empirical study across 5 benchmarks with 6 sizes of Qwen3 and 4 sizes of Gemma3, evaluating each stage (generation, critique, revision) in the self-refinement pipeline.

Result: Larger generators and refiners improve the pipeline; an undersized refiner can harm performance; critic size has little impact, though including a small critic consistently helps; performance scaling differs by stage.

Conclusion: Model capacity should be allocated non-uniformly across stages; design guidelines can reduce computational cost while maintaining or improving performance.

Abstract: Self-refinement, typically structured as generation, critique, and revision, is a widely adopted paradigm for improving LLM generation and serves as a core mechanism in many LLM agents. While the three stages involve different cognitive demands, most existing approaches conveniently treat the model size as an implementation detail rather than a subject of study, which may lead to a waste of resources. Little work has systematically examined how model size affects each stage or whether effective self-refinement requires equally capable models for generation, critique, and revision. We present the first stage-wise model size study of the self-refinement pipeline on 5 benchmarks from different domains using 6 model sizes of Qwen3 and 4 model sizes of Gemma 3. We conclude that larger generators and refiners generally improve the pipeline, whereas an undersized refiner can even harm performance. Second, performance is highly insensitive to the size of the critic, although including even a small critic consistently outperforms omitting critique altogether. Our findings demonstrate that model capacity should not be allocated uniformly across self-refinement pipelines. Instead, different stages exhibit distinct size scaling characteristics, providing practical guidance for designing more computationally efficient multi-stage language model systems.

</details>


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)

### [138] [Humanoid Musical Robots as Experimental Interfaces for Music-Evoked Emotion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20433)
*Vincent K. M. Cheung,Jia-Yeu Lin*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Proposes musical humanoid robots as experimental interfaces for controlled study of music-evoked emotions, enabled by parametric control over acoustic and multimodal variables; validated via WAS-5 case study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current emotion research relies on pre-recorded stimuli and single-modality paradigms; need embodied, interactive, and multimodal investigation of music-emotion coupling.

Method: Theoretical framework grounded in music cognition/emotion; identify mechanisms requiring controlled manipulation of acoustic/non-acoustic variables; argue robot platforms allow decoupled, reproducible control of auditory, visual, and interactive components; illustrate feasibility with WAS-5 case study.

Result: Demonstrates reproducible control of acoustic and interaction variables; supports feasibility of using musical humanoid robots for controlled music-emotion experiments.

Conclusion: Positions musical humanoid robots as a methodological platform enabling future controlled investigations of music-evoked emotions.

Abstract: Advances in technology have led to increasingly sophisticated musical humanoid robots. However, their use has largely been limited to performance and related research in human-robot interaction. In this position paper, we propose a novel perspective: musical humanoid robots as experimental interfaces for investigating music-evoked emotions. We argue that current research is constrained by paradigms relying on pre-recorded auditory stimuli, which fail to capture the multimodal, embodied, and interactive nature of real-world musical experience. Building on existing theories of music cognition and emotion, we identify mechanisms that require controlled manipulation of both acoustic and non-acoustic variables. We show that humanoid robots are well-suited as they enable parametric control of performance variables, reproducibility across trials, and the decoupling and recombination of auditory, visual, and interactive components. We illustrate the technical feasibility of this perspective through a case study of the WAseda Saxophonist Robot 5 (WAS-5), demonstrating reproducible control of acoustic and interaction variables that are prerequisites for future music-emotion experiments. Our work positions musical humanoid robots as a methodological platform that enables future controlled investigations of music-evoked emotions.

</details>


### [139] [EndoLIFT: Language-Disambiguated Latent-Conditioned Rectified Flow for Bidirectional Endoscopic Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20478)
*Chi Kit Ng,Yidong Zhang,Lui Siu Hing,Jinsong Lin,Tianchun Wu,Ho Yin Chim,Zhiqing Tang,Tao Yang,Huxin Gao,Trevor Yeung,Raymond Shing-Yan Tang,Hongliang Ren*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: EndoLIFT is a vision-language-action policy for bidirectional endoscopy that resolves intent aliasing by conditioning actions on both explicit language intent and a latent trajectory; it yields substantial improvements in directional accuracy, reduces wrong-direction advances, and generalizes across seen/unseen phantoms and ex-vivo trials.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bidirectional endoscopy creates intent aliasing: similar observations can require opposite actions depending on the intended phase. There is a need to separate language-driven intent selection from trajectory execution to improve safety, reliability, and generalization.

Method: A multimodal policy named EndoLIFT that ingests RGB frames, a natural-language instruction, and the previous-action state, and uses a 32-D variational trajectory latent to condition continuous action-chunk generation via a rectified-flow action expert. Comparisons include a latent-conditioned baseline and an architecture-controlled 1-bit mode flag reference. Evaluation includes closed-loop phantom studies (colon phantom, unseen lung/stomach phantoms) and 10/10 ex-vivo porcine-trachea trials.

Result: EndoLIFT improves navigation-direction accuracy by 11.1 percentage points and reduces wrong-direction advance by 83%. It achieves 82.8% intent-following accuracy across 44 linguistic variants. In closed-loop evaluation, overall success improves by 30 percentage points over EndoLIFT without the latent-trajectory conditioning (VTL) on seen colon phantom and unseen lung/stomach phantoms, and completes 10/10 ex-vivo porcine-trachea trials.

Conclusion: The work disentangles language-based intent selection from trajectory latent contributions to directional correctness and robust retraction, demonstrating strong generalization and robustness across modalities and unseen phantoms.

Abstract: Routine gastrointestinal endoscopy is intrinsically bidirectional: the instrument is advanced to reach target anatomy and later withdrawn or retroflexed for inspection, while an external cue may require earlier reversal. When the requested phase changes before the visual scene does, nearly identical observations can require opposite axial actions. We identify and formalize this ambiguity in bidirectional endoscopic control as intent aliasing. We propose EndoLIFT (Endoscopic Language-Instruction Flow with Trajectory Latents), a vision-language-action policy that combines explicit language-based intent conditioning with a latent-conditioned rectified-flow action expert. The policy receives RGB, a language instruction, and the previous-action state; a 32-D variational trajectory latent stochastically conditions continuous action-chunk generation. Controlled same-observation instruction swaps establish that language selects the axial mode, independently of whether the trajectory latent is present. Relative to the matched model without latent conditioning, EndoLIFT improves navigation-direction accuracy by 11.1 percentage points and reduces wrong-direction advance by 83\%. An architecture-controlled 1-bit mode-flag reference exhibits weaker canonical-anchor switching, while EndoLIFT retains 82.8\% intent-following accuracy across 44 held-out linguistic variants. In closed-loop evaluation, EndoLIFT improves overall success by 30 percentage points over EndoLIFT w/o VTL on both the seen colon phantom and the unseen lung and stomach phantoms, and completes 10/10 ex-vivo porcine-trachea trials. These results separate language-based intent selection from the trajectory latent's contribution to directional correctness and robust retraction.

</details>


### [140] [Koala Gripper: Co-designing Robotic Grippers and Data-Capture Devices for Scaling Dexterous Manipulation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20546)
*Amar Hajj-Ahmad,Zubin Kremer Guha,Tim Fofonoff,Zhi Ern Teoh,Ciarán T. O'Neill,Ben Thacher,Igor Fala,Vidullan Surendran,Murphy Wonsick,Peter Whitney,David Watkins*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Co-design framework yielding Koala Gripper—a data-collection-oriented, ergonomic, backdrivable robotic gripper platform with a force-optimized finger/trigger linkage and dual-thumb design, enabling better dexterity and data-for-learning pipelines than parallel-jaw grippers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Demand for large manipulation datasets; current data-collection devices sacrifice ergonomics and manipulation performance by mirroring gripper morphologies; need integrated design aligning data collection and execution hardware.

Method: Propose a co-design workflow weaving platform constraints into development; implement Koala Gripper with novel linkage, monolithic dual-thumb, ergonomic design; backdrivable actuated fingers with low effective mass; deploy with end-to-end data collection and policy execution pipeline; validate on object grasp, tool use, singulation; compare to baseline.

Result: Improved dexterity and grasp capability versus parallel jaw grippers; secure grasps across diverse objects; capable of forceful tool use and precise singulation; scalable and user-friendly; end-to-end data collection and learning-from-demonstration workflow demonstrated.

Conclusion: Koala Gripper enables better data collection and policy learning; co-design approach yields ergonomic, high-performance platform; suitable for large manipulation datasets and learning frameworks.

Abstract: As the demand for larger manipulation datasets grows, handheld robotic gripper data collection and the associated gripper designs become more vital. Current data collection device designs trend towards matching the morphologies of existing robotic grippers, sacrificing ergonomics and manipulation performance. In this paper, we propose a co-design framework that guides the simultaneous development of both data collection and robotic execution devices by weaving both platform constraints into the design process. Through this workflow, we present the Koala Gripper system, a data capture device and robotic gripper platform that improves dexterity and grasp capability compared to parallel jaw grippers while preserving scalability and ease-of-use. The design introduces a novel force-optimized finger/trigger linkage mechanism with directional reflected mass characteristics, a unique monolithic dual-thumb, and user-centered ergonomic design. The design's actuated robotic fingers are backdrivable, with effective mass on the order of tens of grams. We show that these grippers are capable of secure grasps over a wide range of objects, forceful tool use, and precise singulation. We further validate the platform by deploying it with an end-to-end data collection and policy execution pipeline that highlights its capabilities through learning from demonstration. More information available at http://koalagripper.rai-inst.com

</details>


### [141] [Logic-VLA: A Temporal Logic Conditioned Vision-Language-Action Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20556)
*Celina Shiyu Wang,Yiqi Zhao,Junjie Ye,Yue Wang,Jyotirmoy V. Deshmukh*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A formal-requirement-aware Vision-Language-Action model, Logic-VLA, conditions on Signal Temporal Logic (STL) specs at inference. It uses a syntax-graph STL encoder and a two-stage policy adaptation (STL-conditioned supervised fine-tuning on satisfying demos; trajectory-level preference optimization with a flow-matching surrogate). It achieves substantial STL-satisfaction gains in quadcopter navigation with minimal NL-task degradation and generalizes to unseen STL formulas.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: NL-grounded VLA systems can follow natural-language instructions but may neglect safety-critical, spatiotemporal constraints. There is a need to integrate formal, temporal requirements into VLA policies without sacrificing flexibility or requiring separate policies for each specification.

Method: Propose Logic-VLA with: (1) a syntax-graph-based STL encoder pretrained to capture temporal logic semantics; (2) STL-conditioned policy adaptation in two stages: (a) STL-conditioned supervised fine-tuning on demonstrations that satisfy the STL, and (b) trajectory-level preference optimization over matched satisfying-violating rollout pairs using a flow-matching surrogate for Identity Preference Optimization; (3) inference-time conditioning on STL specs to steer behavior while preserving nominal NL tasks.

Result: Evaluated in closed-loop quadcopter navigation in randomized photorealistic environments and tested on unseen STL formulas. Logic-VLA improves STL satisfaction rate by 24.8–40.7 percentage points over an STL-blind base policy, while reducing nominal NL task success by at most 1.8 percentage points. Demonstrates that a single VLA can adapt to varying formal requirements without a separate policy per specification and generalizes to unseen STL specifications.

Conclusion: Logic-VLA effectively integrates formal temporal requirements into a VLA framework with minimal impact on nominal NL task performance and demonstrated generalization, enabling a single policy to adapt to multiple formal requirements via inference-time STL conditioning.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models can follow natural-language (NL) task instructions, but such instructions may not precisely specify safety-critical or spatiotemporal requirements on the resulting behavior. We introduce Logic-VLA, a formal-requirement-aware VLA that conditions on Signal Temporal Logic (STL) specifications supplied at inference time. Logic-VLA uses a syntax-graph-based STL encoder pre-trained to capture temporal logic semantics. Policy adaptation proceeds in two stages: STL-conditioned supervised fine-tuning on satisfying demonstrations is followed by trajectory-level preference optimization over matched satisfying-violating rollout pairs using a flow-matching surrogate for Identity Preference Optimization. This formulation improves formal requirement satisfaction while preserving the nominal NL task. We evaluate Logic-VLA in closed-loop quadcopter navigation simulation across randomized photorealistic environments and test generalization to STL formulas unseen during training. Across the evaluation benchmarks, Logic-VLA improves STL satisfaction rate over an STL-blind base policy by 24.8 to 40.7 percentage points (pp) while reducing nominal NL task success by at most 1.8 pp, showing that a single VLA can adapt its behavior to varying formal requirements without requiring a separate policy for each specification.

</details>


### [142] [Nonlinear Model Predictive Control for Trajectory Tracking of Differentially Flat Fixed-Wing Aerial Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20655)
*Nishanth Bobbili,Pratyaksh Rao,Luca Morando,Luca Masci,Giuseppe Loianno*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Unified framework coupling differential flatness trajectory generation with NMPC, enhanced by wind-aware sampling, for predictive, constrained, robust fixed-wing UAV control; validated in simulations and real flights with improved tracking under wind.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Differential flatness provides fast, feasible trajectories but is often paired with model-free controllers lacking prediction; fixed-wing UAVs face aerodynamic limits and environmental disturbances (notably wind); there is a need for a planning-control loop that is predictive and constraint-aware.

Method: Integrate differential flatness-based trajectory generation with nonlinear model predictive control (NMPC); embed a wind-aware sampling strategy within NMPC to anticipate wind disturbances and generate dynamically feasible reference trajectories; enforce aerodynamic and input constraints; maintain computational efficiency through flatness-based planning.

Result: Extensive simulations and real-world flight experiments showing improved tracking accuracy and robustness, especially with wind-aware sampling under strong wind conditions.

Conclusion: The framework provides a predictive, constraint-aware control approach for fixed-wing UAVs, improving robustness to wind disturbances and enabling complex trajectories; wind-aware sampling within NMPC is a beneficial component for robust operation.

Abstract: Planning and control of fixed-wing Unmanned Aerial Vehicles (UAVs) are challenging due to nonlinear dynamics, aerodynamic limits, and environmental disturbances. Differential flatness offers a principled way to generate fast, feasible trajectories, but its use has largely been confined to model-free controllers, which lack predictive capabilities and demand tuning. In this paper, we propose a unified framework that integrates differential flatness-based trajectory generation with Nonlinear Model Predictive Control (NMPC), combining computationally efficient planning with predictive, constraint-aware control. To further improve robustness, we introduce a wind-aware sampling strategy embedded within the NMPC framework, enabling the generation of dynamically feasible reference trajectories that proactively account for wind disturbances while strictly enforcing aerodynamic and control input constraints. We validate the proposed framework through extensive simulations and real-world flight experiments, demonstrating improved tracking accuracy and robustness for complex trajectories, particularly when using the proposed wind-aware sampling strategy under strong wind conditions.

</details>


### [143] [Rethinking Demonstration Unlearning in Imitation Learning for Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20784)
*Jiazhuo Li,Yu Zhang,Yiming Fei,Kangkang Dong,Xiaojun Zhu,Houde Liu,Jinze Tao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A retrain-calibrated audit assesses demonstration edits in imitation learning along two axes—behavior (how close the edited policy is to a policy retrained without removed demos) and evidence (detectability by a membership attack)—and combines them via a conformal test to evaluate joint retrain-consistency across many independent retrains. Findings show edits can improve task behavior while evading retraining evidence or reduce evidence while drifting from retraining; demonstrated on real-robot and simulation domains, e.g., redirect edit on ACT arm restoring 18/20 trials.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Demonstration unlearning in imitation learning is costly when retraining from scratch. There is a need for cheaper policy edits, but such edits must be validated to ensure they effectively remove the targeted demonstrations in closed-loop behavior and are not merely masking retraining signals. Existing unlearning metrics (forgetting loss, single membership attacks) do not characterize joint retrain-consistency in closed-loop policy execution.

Method: Introduce a retrain-calibrated audit with two axes: (1) behavior axis: action divergence to a retrain-at-matched-states, calibrated by a floor derived from independent retrains, ensuring the floor is a baseline of retrain similarity; (2) evidence axis: per-demonstration membership attack against a retrain null, reporting rank and absolute member-loss; combine with a conformal test across a fleet of independent retrains large enough to reject at conventional significance. Apply to five preregistered conditions on three real-robot policies and two simulation suites.

Result: Across the tested conditions, the behavior and evidence axes can dissociate: edits may repair task behavior while leaving evidence unchanged, or reduce evidence while moving behavior away from retraining. In the ACT arm, a redirect edit restored blind-scored robot success to 18 of 20 trials.

Conclusion: The retrain-calibrated audit provides a joint, calibrated assessment of policy edits in imitation learning, revealing potential trade-offs between improving closed-loop task performance and evading retraining detection. It enables cheaper yet verifiable unlearning, with demonstrated sensitivity to different edit types and contexts.

Abstract: Imitation learning for robotics depends on human demonstrations, some of which people may later ask to remove. Retraining without them is the natural reference, but its cost grows with policy and dataset scale, motivating cheaper operators that edit a trained policy. Metrics inherited from machine unlearning, such as forgetting loss or a single membership attack, do not establish what an edit removed from a policy acting in closed loop. We therefore introduce a retrain-calibrated audit that reads demonstration unlearning along two axes: behavior, whether the edited policy acts like one retrained without the removed demonstrations, and evidence, whether an auditor can still detect it was trained on them. The behavior axis measures action divergence to that retrain at matched states, calibrated by a floor built from independent retrains, so a policy at the floor is as close to a retrain as retrains are to each other. The evidence axis applies a per-demonstration membership attack against a retrain null, reporting both its rank and its absolute member-loss level, since rank alone accepts operators that inflate member losses past the null. A conformal test then combines both axes into one hypothesis of joint retrain consistency, against a fleet of independent retrains large enough to reject at conventional significance. Across five preregistered conditions on three real-robot policy classes and two simulation suites, the axes dissociate in both directions on one checkpoint, as an edit may repair task behavior while leaving evidence unchanged, or reduce evidence while moving behavior away from retraining. On the ACT arm, a redirect edit restores blind-scored robot success to 18 of 20 trials.

</details>


### [144] [Natural Sit-to-Stand Motion Synthesis For Humanoids via Guided Assistance Curricula and Staged Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20823)
*Meet Pal Singh,Vyankatesh Ashtekar,Ashish Dutta*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A PPO-based reinforcement learning framework learns natural sit-to-stand (STS) motions for a humanoid from scratch, using a three-part strategy: a curriculum with pelvis-assisted force and chair-height progression, broad initial/target pose sampling via inverse kinematics across eight chair heights, and biomechanics-inspired rewards. It achieves high reliability (≈97% success) across chair heights and generalizes to deeper postures, outperforming prior work.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: STS is a difficult control problem with infinitely many valid trajectories; achieving robust, human-like standing motions without demonstrations or reference trajectories is challenging, especially across varying chair heights and postures.

Method: A single Proximal Policy Optimization policy is trained with three components: (i) a coupled force/chair-height curriculum using a vertical pelvis-assist force that decays over time and progressively unlocks taller chairs to prevent premature distribution shift; (ii) robustness via random sampling of a large set of IK-generated initial/target poses spanning eight chair heights; (iii) a reward structure inspired by biomechanics and optimal control to shape angular momentum for seat-off and to use a center-of-pressure attraction function for stable transitions with low energy.

Result: On a deterministic force-free evaluator, the policy achieves more than 97% balanced-standing success across eight chair heights, generalizes smooth motion across heights, and can rise from substantially deep-seated postures, outperforming the state of the art.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that RTS-like humanoid STS can be learned from scratch with a well-designed curriculum, broad pose diversity, and biomechanics-informed rewards, yielding robust, human-like, and generalizable stand-up behaviors across varying chair heights.

Abstract: A humanoid has infinitely many ways to stand up from sitting while maintaining balance, making sit-to-stand (STS) a challenging control problem. We synthesise natural humanoid STS motion from scratch using reinforcement learning, without demonstrations or reference trajectories. A single Proximal Policy Optimisation policy learns smooth, human-like rising driven by three complementary components. (i) A coupled force/chair-height curriculum is used. A vertical pelvis-assist force aids early trajectory exploration and decays over training. Taller chairs are unlocked with decaying assisting force. This ensures that the policy masters a viable STS trajectory at each chair height before being exposed to harder ones, avoiding the premature distribution shift that otherwise collapses generalisation. (ii) Motion robustness is achieved by randomly sampling from a large number of inverse kinematics-generated initial and target poses spanning over eight chair heights. (iii) A set of rewards is defined inspired from biomechanics and optimal control studies. They shape the robot's angular momentum for seat-off, and enable support-region transition via centre of pressure attraction function to ensure smooth low-effort actuation. On a deterministic force-free evaluator, the policy attains more than 97% balanced-standing success across eight chair heights. The policy generalises smooth motion across chair heights and enables the robot to rise from substantially deep-seated postures as compared to the state of the art.

</details>


### [145] [Demonstration-Guided Humanoid Stand-Up on an Emulated Deformable Surface](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20852)
*Aniruddh Kushwah,Vyankatesh Ashtekar,Ashish Dutta*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A reference-guided RL approach for stand-up of a 29-DOF humanoid on deformable soft ground, using a human demo on hard ground. Terrain compliance is modeled with MuJoCo solref/solimp. Training is staged: first on hard ground with reference tracking and recovery rewards; then terrain softness is introduced by adjusting solref/solimp to broaden penetration, followed by retraining to maintain the demonstration pattern. The policy achieves fallen-to-standing in simulation with about 40 mm contact penetration, across two sequences and on both hard and soft ground. An ablation shows tracking alone is insufficient and explicit recovery rewards are essential.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome the challenge of robust stand-up on deformable terrain and improve sim-to-real transfer by leveraging reference demonstrations and terrain-aware rewards; address how contact softness affects support force generation and motion generation.

Method: Two-stage RL training: (1) learn on hard ground with reference tracking (residual joint-position control) and explicit recovery rewards (pelvis height, torso uprightness, final posture); (2) lower terrain stiffness by tuning solref and expand solimp penetration zone to allow more surface penetration, then retrain to adapt to delayed support forces while preserving the demonstration pattern. The policy operates within MuJoCo’s rigid-body soft-contact model and uses a reference-guided objective.

Result: Policy completes fallen-to-standing tasks in simulation with pelvis height and uprightness targets met; max penetration ≈ 40 mm during contact-intensive phases; success on two stand-up sequences; works on both hard and soft ground; ablation indicates reference tracking alone fails, explicit recovery rewards are crucial.

Conclusion: Demonstrates that a reference-guided RL framework with terrain-aware rewards can adapt stand-up motions to soft ground while preserving demonstration patterns, highlighting the importance of explicit recovery objectives for reliable performance under deformable-contact conditions.

Abstract: This paper presents a reference-guided reinforcement learning framework to generate stand-up motion for a 29-DOF Unitree G1 humanoid on deformable soft ground, using a human demonstration recorded on hard ground. The terrain compliance is modelled using solref and solimp parameters from MuJoCo's rigid body soft-contact model. The rewards consists of (i) reference motion tracking through residual joint-position control and (ii) explicit recovery objectives such as pelvis height, torso uprightness, and the final posture. First, the policy is trained with the specified rewards considering hard ground. Next, the terrain stiffness is lowered by updating solref and the nominal surface penetration zone is expanded using solimp. Subsequent training enables the policy to adapt to the delayed support force generation due to significant surface penetration during contact-intensive phases while preserving the original demonstration pattern. The learned policy successfully completes the fallen-to-standing task in simulation, reaching the targeted pelvis height and uprightness, with a maximum contact penetration of approximately 40 mm during the process. The proposed method is demonstrated on two stand-up sequences and successfully achieves the final recovery objective on both hard and soft ground. Ablation studies show that reference tracking alone is insufficient for successful stand-up, and that explicit recovery rewards are essential.

</details>


### [146] [IMU-Free Body-Frame State Estimation with Sparse Scene Flow for Quadcopters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20891)
*Daniel Grønhaug,Sofie Markeset,Mathias Kolberg*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A vision-only EKF-based state estimator for X-configuration quadcopters using a canonical stereo pair with no IMU/GPS. It operates entirely in the body frame, fusing stereo and temporal information through a continuous-discrete EKF on a composite manifold; stationary scene points serve as inertial references. A 4-view bundle adjustment produces a sparse, covarianced 3D point cloud; feature-derived information influences the pose prior but not the EKF state directly in the solver. The system yields body pose, velocity, angular velocity, gravity, disturbances, sparse scene flow, and adaptive, focus-driven point density. It can serve as a measurement source for a downstream world model without world-frame infrastructure, while remaining extensible to include IMU/GPS in the future.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable robust 6-DOF state estimation for quadrotors in GPS-denied environments using only vision, removing the need for inertial sensors while maintaining real-time capable fusion and a reusable world-frame reference via stationary scene points.

Method: Continuous-discrete extended Kalman filter on a composite manifold ⟨SE(3), R^3, ...⟩ estimating body pose, velocity, angular velocity, gravity, and disturbances. Features are detected (FAST, Shi–Tomasi), tracked (SSD, Lucas–Kanade), and matched across stereo cameras (NCC) with search regions predicted from the filter pose and point uncertainty. Chi-squared gating retains only stationary points. A 4-view full bundle adjustment (two stereo pairs at two timestamps) jointly estimates position and velocity from stereo disparity and temporal parallax, using the filter-derived pose as a prior. EKF features influence only via the pose prior; the point cloud carries per-point position, velocity, and covariance. Point density is adaptively allocated with a focus point.

Result: Provides a body-frame state estimate, a calibrated pose change, and sparse scene flow. Outputs include a sparse 3D point cloud with per-point statistics. The estimator is self-contained (no GPS/IMU) yet designed for later integration of world-frame sensors; the point cloud is suited for downstream world-model construction.

Conclusion: A self-contained, vision-only state estimation pipeline for X-quadrotors that leverages stationary scene references and a hybrid EKF–BA structure to deliver robust body-frame estimates and scene geometry, with adaptable focus-driven density and potential for future world-frame integration.

Abstract: We present a vision-only state estimation system for X-configuration quadcopters equipped with a canonical stereo camera pair and no inertial sensors. The system operates entirely in the body frame, requiring only synchronised stereo images and motor thrust commands. A continuous-discrete extended Kalman filter on a composite manifold state $\langle SE(3), \mathbb{R}^3, \ldots \rangle$ maintains estimates of body-frame pose, velocity, angular velocity, gravity, and disturbances, using stationary scene points as implicit inertial references. Feature points are detected (FAST, Shi-Tomasi), tracked temporally (SSD, Lucas-Kanade) and matched across cameras (NCC), with search regions predicted from filter-derived pose and point uncertainty. Chi-squared gating on the normalised innovation admits only stationary points to the filter. The system also produces a sparse 3D point cloud carrying per-point position, velocity and joint covariance. These come from a 4-view (two stereo pairs at two timestamps) full bundle adjustment that jointly estimates position and velocity from stereo disparity and temporal parallax, with the filter-derived relative pose as a prior. Feature points in the EKF do not enter the solver; their information is reflected through the pose prior. Point cloud density is spatially adaptive: an external focus point directs allocation, producing dense coverage in the region of attention and sparse coverage elsewhere. The output is a body-frame state estimate, a calibrated pose change, and a sparse scene flow. It is intended as a measurement source for a downstream world model anchored in the current body frame, without dependence on GPS, IMU, or any world-frame infrastructure, though the architecture accommodates their future integration.

</details>


### [147] [Scalable Distributed Simulation-Based Testing for Automated Driving Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20904)
*Christian Geller,Benedikt Haas,Lutz Eckstein*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: End-to-end, DevOps-driven framework for scalable CARLA ADS testing on Kubernetes, enabling automated build/deploy/distributed execution via Helm/Helmfile and Argo Workflows; achieves ~8× speedup on 200 scenarios; supports traceable safety evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scale thousands of scenario tests for automated driving systems and ITS while reducing manual effort and increasing reproducibility.

Method: Package ROS 2 as Helm charts; declaratively compose simulation environments with Helmfile; deploy and orchestrate tests with Argo Workflows on a lightweight Kubernetes cluster (K3s); aggregate OpenSCENARIO cases; run in parallel; collect logs and metrics; validate with real-world ADS test app.

Result: Significant end-to-end time reductions (8×); analysis of parallelism vs orchestration overhead and cluster stability; demonstration with scenario sources and downstream evaluation modules; evidence support for safety arguments.

Conclusion: The framework provides a scalable, traceable foundation for simulation-based ADS testing and can support safety argumentation through reproducible evidence.

Abstract: Virtual scenario-based testing is a key enabler for validating automated driving systems (ADS) and intelligent transport systems (ITS). However, executing large-scale test suites involving possibly thousands of scenarios remains labor-intensive and difficult to scale. This paper presents an end-to-end, DevOps-driven framework that automates build, deployment, and distributed execution of CARLA-based scenario tests of an ADS on a lightweight Kubernetes cluster.
