ROS 2 applications are packaged as standardized Kubernetes Helm charts generated from repository specifications, while entire simulation environments are composed declaratively via dynamic Helmfile manifests. The paper describes how a distributed testing workflow can be implemented in Argo Workflows to provision environments, aggregate and batch OpenSCENARIO test cases from configurable sources, execute scenarios in parallel across cluster nodes, and collect logs and resource metrics. In an evaluation on a multi-node K3s cluster running 200 scenarios, the best configuration speeds up end-to-end workflow time by more than a factor of eight compared to a sequential baseline. The results demonstrate significant gains in end-to-end execution time and quantify trade-offs between parallelism, orchestration overhead, and cluster stability. The framework is further demonstrated in a real-world ADS test application with connections to scenario sources and downstream evaluation modules. This demonstrates that the approach provides a strong foundation not only for scalable simulation testing, but also for generating traceable evidence that can support safety arguments.

</details>


### [148] [Fast Coordinated Bimanual Motion Planning With Hard Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20946)
*Borna Paro,Luka Petrović,Ivan Marković*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A fast, constraint-enforcing bimanual motion planner using a leader–follower IK parameterization that fixes the end-effector relative transform, yielding up to 19.4× speedups and continuous constraint satisfaction across simulations and real Kinova Gen3 experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rigid bimanual manipulation imposes a nonlinear equality constraint that fixes the relative pose between the two end-effectors, restricting feasible configurations to a measure-zero manifold and complicating motion planning.

Method: Adopts a leader–follower parameterization wherein the leader’s configuration is free and the follower’s is determined via inverse kinematics to satisfy the fixed relative transform continuously along the entire path; extensive simulation evaluation across diverse environments, constraints, and bimanual platforms, followed by real‑world validation on a Kinova Gen3 with tray transport and elongated-object manipulation.

Result: Plan generation is 19.4× faster than prior work while guaranteeing continuous satisfaction of the transformation constraint along the trajectory; simulation studies and hardware experiments demonstrate successful transfer of planned trajectories to real bimanual hardware.

Conclusion: The proposed pipeline delivers fast, constraint-consistent bimanual planning that generalizes across environments and hardware, with empirical validation on both simulation and real Kinova Gen3 platforms; it effectively addresses the nonlinear constraint challenge inherent in bimanual manipulation.

Abstract: Bimanual manipulation enables complex tasks but introduces added complexity from the high number of degrees of freedom involved. When handling rigid objects, the relative transformation between the two end effectors must remain fixed throughout the motion, manifesting as a nonlinear equality constraint that confines the feasible configuration space to a measure-zero manifold and challenges conventional motion planners. We propose a fast bimanual motion planning pipeline that enforces this hard transformation constraint continuously along the entire path, using a leader-follower parameterization: the leader's configuration is treated as a free variable, while the follower's is determined via inverse kinematics to satisfy the constraint. We extensively evaluate the method in simulation across diverse environments, constraints and bimanual platforms, achieving 19.4x faster planning than prior work while guaranteeing continuous constraint satisfaction. Real-world experiments on a bimanual Kinova Gen3 setup, involving tray transport and elongated-object manipulation, validate direct transfer of planned trajectories to physical hardware.

</details>


### [149] [Neural-Primitive: An Efficient End-to-end Local Planner with Primitive-based Imitation Learning for Autonomous Flight](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20948)
*Zhitao Liu,Guangtong Xu,Zihan Wang,Jialiang Hou,Chao Xu,Fei Gao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A fast, end-to-end imitation-learning local planner for UAVs in unknown clutter, mapping sensor input directly to polynomial trajectory coefficients to generate safe, smooth, dynamically feasible trajectories in real time with minimal onboard memory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the computation-quality-memory trilemma in onboard trajectory generation for autonomous flight in unknown cluttered environments by delivering a lightweight, real-time planning approach.

Method: An offline primitive-based dataset collection framework to produce safe trajectory primitives in non-convex environments, and a compact neural network that maps sensory inputs to polynomial coefficients encoding higher-order dynamics. The planner operates end-to-end without back-end optimization, enabling real-time trajectory generation.

Result: Ultra-fast computation speeds (below 1 ms on a desktop, ~3.68 ms onboard) and memory footprint under 1.5 MiB; extensive simulations show improved planning latency and target-reaching quality; zero-shot sim-to-real transfer demonstrated in real-world experiments.

Conclusion: The approach delivers a memory- and computation-efficient, real-time local planner that generates smooth, collision-free, dynamically feasible trajectories, with strong sim-to-real transfer performance.

Abstract: Autonomous flight in unknown cluttered environments is hindered by the computation-quality-memory trilemma of onboard trajectory generation. In this paper, we propose an efficient end-to-end local planner via imitation learning. A lightweight offline-primitive-based dataset collection framework is designed to produce safe and high-quality trajectory primitives in non-convex environments. A compact neural network directly maps sensory inputs to polynomial coefficients that inherently encode higher-order dynamical information. The learned policy generates smooth, empirically collision-free and dynamically feasible trajectories in real time without back-end solving. It achieves ultra-fast computation (below 1ms on a standard desktop and average 3.68ms during onboard flight), while maintaining low onboard memory requirements (less than 1.5MiB). Extensive simulation benchmarks demonstrate superiority in both planning latency and target-reaching progress quality. Zero-shot deployment in real-world experiments further validates the robust sim-to-real transfer capability of the proposed method.

</details>


### [150] [Hybrid Roller-Jamming Gripper for Object Acquisition and Retention Under Pose Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20962)
*Yijie Ren,Guillaume Gourmelen,Hiroyasu Iwata*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Hybrid roller-jamming gripper combines inward rollers for object intake with vacuum-granular jamming for post-capture stability, showing high success across 840 grasp trials and ablation tests that separate roller and jam contributions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses pose uncertainty in household manipulation where roller-only grippers struggle with post-capture stability and granular-jamming grippers require sufficient contact before jamming.

Method: Integrates inward roller rotation to draw the object toward the gripper center, followed by vacuum-induced granular jamming to stiffen rollers and stabilize the grasp. Includes simplified geometric analysis, bench-level force characterization, and a 7-DoF arm demonstration with eight objects. Evaluation includes 840 grasp trials with planar and orientation offsets, plus ablation tests (roller-only and jamming-only).

Result: Overall success: 812/840. Planar-offset: 215/216. Orientation-offset: 597/624. Ablation: roller-only 54/81; jamming-only 24/81. Demonstrates that combining rollers and jamming yields higher acquisition and retention than either technique alone.

Conclusion: Hybrid roller-jamming is a promising strategy for robust acquisition and retention under imperfect first contact, validated by substantial trial data and ablation studies.

Abstract: In household manipulation, pose uncertainty often results in off-centre or partial initial contact, making reliable object acquisition difficult. Roller-based grippers can actively draw objects inward but often provide limited post-capture stability, whereas granular-jamming grippers require sufficient contact before jamming to achieve strong retention. This paper presents a hybrid roller-jamming gripper that integrates active object intake and post-capture retention within a single gripper. The proposed gripper uses inward roller rotation to increase contact and draw the object toward the gripper centre, followed by vacuum-induced granular jamming to stiffen the rollers and stabilise the grasp. The paper also presents a simplified geometric analysis of the gripper and a bench-level characterisation of the prototype's force capability. The gripper prototype was mounted on a 7-DoF robotic arm and evaluated using eight test objects. Furthermore, controlled planar position and orientation offsets were applied, with each condition repeated three times. The main evaluation comprised 840 grasp trials, including 216 planar-offset trials and 624 orientation-offset trials. Overall, the gripper succeeded in 812/840 trials: 215/216 planar-offset trials and 597/624 orientation-offset trials. The ablation evaluation comprised 162 trials on three objects. The roller-only and jamming-only conditions achieved 54/81 and 24/81 successes, respectively, showing their different contributions. These results provide initial mechanism-level evidence that hybrid roller-jamming is a promising strategy for improving acquisition and retention after imperfect first contact.

</details>


### [151] [PhysCaP: Grounding Code-as-Policy Agent with Physics-Informed Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21031)
*Chen-Yu Lin,Jing-Wen Chen,Hsueh-En Chang,Hung-An Chen,Sheng-Hsun Chang,Chi-Pin Huang,Fu-En Yang,Min-Hung Chen,Yi-Ting Chen,Yu-Chiang Frank Wang,Shao-Hua Sun*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PhysCaP introduces a physics-informed, active-exploration policy that explicitly seeks physical property information to improve manipulation when properties are hidden, using training-free property extractors and a dual-agent planner/prioritizer for efficient exploration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Passive vision-language-action policies struggle to infer latent physical properties essential for manipulation; there is a need for active, information-seeking behavior and physical-property estimation without extra sensing.

Method: A Physics-Informed Code-as-Policy framework with: (1) training-free modules estimating object mass and stiffness from proprioception; (2) a dual-agent design: Planner decides exploration vs stopping; Prioritizer filters implausible interactions and ranks remaining interactions with a heuristic priority score; (3) an exploration layer integrated into a code-as-policy setting to guide targeted, cost-efficient interactions.

Result: Empirical evaluation on real tabletop tasks (hidden-object search, empty-can detection, avocado ripeness) and a LIBERO simulation shows PhysCaP matches or outperforms naive baselines with fewer interactions and shorter execution time; ablations demonstrate the value of the physical-property extraction modules.

Conclusion: Integrating physics-informed exploration into code-based policies with training-free physical-property extractors and a dual-agent decision loop yields more efficient active perception in manipulation, particularly when latent properties are concealed.

Abstract: We present PhysCaP, a Physics-Informed Code-as-Policy agent for active perception in robotic manipulation. While vision-language-action policies excel at imitating demonstrations, they rely on passive observation and fail to infer latent physical properties critical for manipulation. PhysCaP augments code-as-policy frameworks with a physics-informed exploration layer that enables explicit information-seeking through interaction. It introduces training-free physical property extraction modules that estimate object mass and stiffness from robot proprioception without additional sensors. To balance exploration costs and the efficiency of information obtained, PhysCaP employs a dual-agent design: a Planner that decides when to explore and when to stop, and a Prioritizer that filters implausible interactions and ranks the remainder using a heuristic priority score, enabling efficient, targeted exploration. We evaluate PhysCaP on real-world tabletop manipulation tasks (searching for hidden objects, detecting empty cans, and finding ripe avocados) and a simulated task in LIBERO. The results show that existing passive and naive interactive baselines either fail when physical properties are hidden or over-explore, whereas PhysCaP achieves comparable performance with fewer interactions and reduced execution time. Ablation studies further validate the effectiveness of the proposed physical property extraction modules. Project page: https://physcap.github.io

</details>


### [152] [Roadside-Cooperative Autonomous Driving: From Data Platform to Vision-Language End-to-End Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21032)
*Yitao Xu,Tong Wu,Yiyan Wu,Guoji Xu,Yanbo Jiang,Jiahao Wang,Zehong Ke,Junkai Jiang,Fang Zhang,Jianqiang Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Proposes V2XBench and Chat-V2XBench benchmarks and AURORA framework; achieves high route completion and driving score with a VLM-based, cross-view aligned, end-to-end cooperative driving pipeline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current V2X benchmarks lack closed-loop evaluation and language-grounded supervision, hindering development of vision-language models for cooperative driving.

Method: V2XBench provides synchronized ego-roadside sensing with closed-loop evaluation; Chat-V2XBench is a progressive VQA dataset for cooperative reasoning; AURORA uses a dual-view perception architecture with a Cross-View Query Alignment and Fusion (CQAF) module to unify ego and roadside tokens, followed by a LoRA-adapted VLM to connect semantic reasoning with trajectory planning.

Result: In closed-loop V2XBench evaluations, AURORA attains state-of-the-art performance, including Route Completion 98.21% and Driving Score 76.02, particularly robust under heavy occlusion, with low roadside communication bandwidth.

Conclusion: This work pioneers an extensible V2X–VLM paradigm, enabling next-generation cooperative autonomous driving.

Abstract: Vehicle-to-Everything (V2X) cooperation enables beyond-line-of-sight perception, mitigating occlusions in single-vehicle sensing. However, existing V2X benchmarks provide limited support for closed-loop evaluation and language-grounded supervision, hindering the development of vision-language models (VLMs) for end-to-end cooperative driving. To address these limitations, we introduce V2XBench, a simulation platform featuring synchronized ego--roadside sensing and closed-loop evaluation, together with Chat-V2XBench, a progressively structured VQA dataset for cooperative reasoning. Building upon this benchmark infrastructure, we propose AURORA, an end-to-end cooperative driving framework. Equipped with a dual-view perception architecture, AURORA mitigates spatial and semantic discrepancies across ego and roadside viewpoints through a query-level Cross-View Query Alignment and Fusion (CQAF) module. Leveraging the resulting unified tokens, a LoRA-adapted VLM bridges semantic reasoning and generative trajectory planning. Extensive closed-loop evaluations on V2XBench demonstrate that AURORA achieves state-of-the-art performance in heavily occluded scenarios, with a Route Completion rate of 98.21% and a Driving Score of 76.02, while requiring low roadside communication bandwidth. Ultimately, this work pioneers an extensible V2X--VLM paradigm, paving the way for next-generation cooperative autonomous driving.

</details>


### [153] [TaPeR: Probabilistic Recovery of Sparse Task Precedence Graphs from a Handful of Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21035)
*Adrian Röfer,Karla Stepanova,Abhinav Valada*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Learns long-horizon task dependencies from demonstrations without symbolic predicates by using simple kinematic graphs and pose distributions to estimate pairwise dependencies, initializes a probabilistic precedence graph, and filters it into a final task-dependency graph. It achieves more accurate structures from fewer demos than baselines and supports multiple valid execution orders.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable flexible ordering of subtasks in long-horizon manipulation by extracting task structure from demonstrations without requiring explicit symbolic grounding.

Method: From demonstrations, construct simple kinematic graphs and distributions over relative object poses; estimate pairwise task-step dependency probabilities; initialize edge weights of a precedence graph with these probabilities; apply a filtering pipeline to convert this probabilistic graph into a final task dependency graph.

Result: The method recovers more accurate task structures from fewer demonstrations than baselines and enables generation of multiple valid execution orders for a given task; evaluated on an existing benchmark and a new longer-task dataset with more complex dependencies.

Conclusion: Demonstrating that task structure can be inferred from motion/pose data alone, enabling flexible planning and execution orders without explicit symbolic grounding, with potential applicability to more complex, real-world tasks.

Abstract: Long-horizon manipulation tasks are often only partially ordered. For example, when assembling an electronic device, the battery and circuit board may be installed in either order, but both must be in place before the enclosure is closed. Recovering such dependencies enables robots to flexibly reorder subtasks while preserving task validity. Existing approaches typically infer task structure from human demonstrations using both temporal and symbolic supervision. However, symbolic predicates require explicit grounding, which is difficult to obtain in realistic settings. In this work, we present an approach for extracting task dependency structures from demonstrations using only simple kinematic graphs and distributions over relative object poses. From these representations, our method estimates pairwise task-step-dependency probabilities and uses them to initialize the edge weights of a precedence graph. We then introduce a filtering pipeline that converts this graph of probability estimates into the final task dependency graph. We evaluate our approach on an existing benchmark and on a new dataset comprising longer tasks with more complex dependencies. We find that our method recovers more accurate task structures from fewer demonstrations than the baselines. Finally, we demonstrate that the inferred graphs can be used to generate multiple valid robotic execution orders for the same task.

</details>


### [154] [FF-MPCC: High-speed Agile Formation Flight with Model Predictive Contouring Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21056)
*Aditya Dandwate,Vit Kratky,Parakh M. Gupta,Martin Saska,Robert Penicka*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A decentralized MPCC-based method for agile UAV formation along a path, with reparametrization/synchronization for dynamic formation geometries; shows 65% improvement in formation maintenance at speeds up to 21 m/s, validated in simulation and real flights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Maintain formation integrity on demanding trajectories under individual UAV dynamic constraints; address limitations of time-parameterized trajectory tracking.

Method: Introduce reparametrization and synchronization for dynamic formation geometries; decentralized algorithm to determine UAV desired positions; integrate with MPCC to couple formation maintenance with path following; validation via simulations and flight tests.

Result: 65% improvement in formation maintenance vs time-parameterized trajectory tracking; capable at 21 m/s; comparable time to goal; works across varying path complexities and on-the-fly formation shape changes.

Conclusion: Feasible and effective approach for high-speed coordinated formation flight; preserves formation while adhering to constraints; supports dynamic formation adaptations.

Abstract: Flying in a prescribed formation in an agile manner remains a challenging problem in the field of UAVs, particularly when following highly-demanding trajectories that require flight at platform limits. We address this problem by proposing a novel decentralized approach to formation flight along a given path that integrates formation maintenance into the MPCC framework, allowing UAVs to adapt their progression along complex paths while respecting individual dynamic constraints and maintaining the desired formation. To this end, we introduce a novel reparametrization and synchronization method for dynamic formation geometries together with a decentralized approach to determine the desired positions for the individual UAVs. The proposed approach allows the formation to coordinate high-speed path following without compromising formation integrity. The proposed approach is validated through extensive simulation and real-world experiments involving scenarios with varying complexity of paths and changes of required formation shape on the fly. In comparison to time-parameterized trajectory tracking, we demonstrate improved formation maintenance by 65% in high-speed flight with velocities up to 21 m/s, while achieving comparable times required to reach the goal.

</details>


### [155] [Teaching is a Process: The TOSS Framework for Modeling Human Teaching Decisions in Human-Interactive Robot Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21083)
*Bernhard Hilpert,Kim Baraka,Joost Broekens*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Introduces the TOSS framework for Human-Robot teaching by analyzing intuitive human teaching decisions in RL contexts; posits a Trigger–Signal–Objective–Strategy network forming a procedural loop between robot behavior and teaching actions; provides a public dataset and theoretical basis for advancing human-centered teaching and novel learning algorithms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand and model the cognitive logic humans use when teaching autonomous agents, with the aim of aligning human teaching intent with robot processing, and to extend robot learning beyond current RL constraints.

Method: An exploratory, bottom-up study with 34 participants observing two RL scenarios; coding 204 intuitive teaching responses across early, middle, and late learning phases; qualitative analysis to identify a network structure (Triggers, Objectives, Signals, Strategies) and to derive the TOSS framework; sharing an openly accessible dataset.

Result: Finds that teaching decisions form an interconnected network of Triggers, Objectives, Signals, and Strategies; teachers assume diverse roles (coaches, engineers, designers); identifies a procedural loop between robot behavior and human actions; introduces the TOSS framework and provides a dataset to support future research.

Conclusion: The TOSS framework offers a theoretical and practical foundation for understanding teaching decisions, simulating realistic oracles, and designing human-centered teaching environments and novel robot learning algorithms beyond existing limitations.

Abstract: Successful Human-Robot Teaching assumes alignment between robot processing needs and human teaching intent. To better understand this alignment, this work seeks to uncover the underlying logic that humans intuitively apply when teaching. Through an exploratory, bottom-up study with N=34, participants observing two distinct robot Reinforcement Learning (RL) scenarios, we analyze 204 intuitive teaching responses across early, middle, and late learning phases. Results reveal that teaching decisions consist of a nuanced, interconnected network of Triggers (situational catalysts), Objectives (subjective teaching targets), Signals (communicative acts), and Strategies (high-level governance) in which teachers spontaneously adopt diverse roles, acting as coaches, engineers, or designers and prioritize different objectives. Based on these results, we introduce the TOSS Framework, which conceptualizes Human-Robot teaching as a procedural loop between robot behavior and human teaching actions, in which human teaching decisions are modeled as Trigger-Signal responses modulated by teaching Objectives and Strategies. It provides future research with an openly accessible dataset and a theoretical foundation for a) understanding teaching decisions and b) simulating realistic oracles as well as c) designing human-centered teaching settings and novel robot learning algorithms that go beyond the constraints of current robot learning settings.

</details>


### [156] [SRL-MPC: Shape-Aware Reinforcement Learned Model Predictive Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21175)
*Ruihua Han,Rui Gao,Zhe Liu,Xinyi Wang,Chang Chen,Shuai Wang,Qi Hao,Jia Pan,Hengshuang Zhao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Shape-Aware Reinforcement Learned MPC (SRL-MPC) combines high-order control barrier functions with a neural policy to adapt MPC parameters for safe, efficient navigation in crowds of heterogeneous shapes, outperforming baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing safe navigation methods rely on homogeneity assumptions, geometry simplifications, or offline optimization, hindering performance in dense crowds with varied robot shapes; a method is needed to preserve MPC safety while enabling shape-aware adaptability.

Method: Derive HOCBF constraints from geometric separation features via a support-function-based transformation; train an RL policy that consumes GSFs to output real-time MPC parameter updates, enabling SRL-MPC to adapt MPC to neighboring crowd geometries without geometry simplification.

Result: Experimental evaluation in randomized crowds with arbitrarily shaped robot fleets shows that SRL-MPC improves safety and adaptability and scales robustly, outperforming representative baselines.

Conclusion: SRL-MPC maintains MPC's safety structure and generalizability while incorporating RL-driven adaptability for shape-aware, heterogeneous crowd navigation; the approach is effective and scalable.

Abstract: Safe and efficient shape-aware navigation in heterogeneous crowds and robot fleets remains challenging. Traditional approaches often assume homogeneous robots, sparse workspaces, simplified geometry, offline computation, or handcrafted parameters to make the problem tractable, which limits their deployment in dense crowd scenarios. Toward this end, we propose Shape-Aware Reinforcement Learned Model Predictive Control (SRL-MPC), a method for safe, efficient, and adaptive navigation in crowds with heterogeneous shapes without geometry simplification. To encode shape-aware safety, we formulate high-order control barrier function (HOCBF) constraints from geometric separation features (GSFs) based on support function transformation. A reinforcement learning (RL) framework then learns a neural policy that reads GSFs and outputs real-time MPC parameter updates, enabling the MPC solver to adapt to neighboring crowd geometries. The key advantage of SRL-MPC is that it preserves the safety structure and generalizability of MPC while integrating the adaptability and intelligence of RL. Experiments in randomized crowd scenarios with arbitrary shaped robot fleets demonstrate the effectiveness, scalability, and robustness of SRL-MPC. The results show that SRL-MPC substantially outperforms representative baselines in safety and adaptability. Project website: https://hanruihua.github.io/srl_mpc_project/

</details>


### [157] [Beyond Imitation: Self-Improving Robot Policies via Off-Policy Q-Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21204)
*Varun Giridhar,Anant Khandelwal,Jeremy A. Collins,Ignat Georgiev,Animesh Garg*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Q-Planning augments a large visuomotor behavior cloning (BC) policy with a small off-policy Q-function to enable value-guided inference and online self-improvement, allowing learning from both successes and failures without retraining the BC network.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: BC lacks self-improvement since failures cannot be learned from without extra demonstrations; RL fine-tuning scales poorly to multi-billion-parameter policies. A light-weight critic can drive improvements by exploiting deployment experiences without modifying the BC weights.

Method: Integrate a compact off-policy Q-function with a large BC policy. Inference uses a one-step Q-weighted average over BC samples. Online self-improvement updates only the Q-function using both successful demonstrations and failed deployment rollouts, leaving BC weights frozen. Evaluations compare LIBERO, RoboTwin, and real-robot tasks to baselines like SFT, IBRL, DSRL, and DAWR under identical budgets.

Result: Ten iterations of self-improvement yield large gains (LIBERO-10: 93%→99%; RoboTwin: 83.8%→91.4%). Episode efficiency improves on near-ceiling tasks. In contact-rich real-robot tasks (stack-cups, insert-wallet), five iterations improve performance from 40%→90% and 25%→80%, respectively, while SFT on successful rollouts stalls (55%, 30%). With identical online budgets, Q-Planning uniquely improves from failures without training an auxiliary actor and outperforms Best-of-N, filtered SFT, IBRL, DSRL, and DAWR.

Conclusion: Q-Planning shows that a small off-policy Q-function suffices to unlock self-improvement for large visuomotor BC policies by leveraging both successes and failures, achieving stable, scalable gains across simulation and real-robot benchmarks without updating the BC policy itself.

Abstract: Behaviour Cloning (BC) has driven remarkable progress in robot manipulation, yet it is fundamentally limited by its inability to self-improve: a policy that fails cannot learn from that failure without additional human demonstrations. Reinforcement Learning fine-tuning offers a path to self-improvement but has proven difficult to scale to the multi-billion-parameter models underpinning modern robot policies. We propose Q-Planning, which equips a large visuomotor BC policy with a small off-policy Q-function. Because a Q-function estimates value rather than imitates actions, it can be trained on the same successful demonstrations as the BC policy and later absorb both successful and failed deployment rollouts, an asymmetry BC does not have. We exploit this asymmetry to enable value-guided action selection at inference (a single-step Q-weighted average over BC draws) and online self-improvement that fine-tunes only the Q-function, leaving the BC weights untouched. On LIBERO and bimanual RoboTwin, ten iterations of self-improvement lift every benchmark score we tested (LIBERO-10 93% to 99%, RoboTwin 83.8% to 91.4%) and shorten successful episodes on the near-ceiling suites (LIBERO-Object, LIBERO-Goal). On two contact-rich bimanual real-robot tasks, the same loop (BC frozen, no human intervention) improves purely from its own deployment rollouts: stack-cups 40% to 90% and insert-wallet 25% to 80% in five iterations, whereas SFT on successful rollouts alone stalls at 55% and 30%. Under an identical online budget Q-Planning is the only method, among Best-of-N, filtered SFT, IBRL, DSRL, and DAWR, that improves stably from failures without training an auxiliary actor.

</details>


### [158] [The Coastline as a Structural Constraint: Harnessing Scene Geometry for Autonomous Surface Vessel Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21276)
*Derek R. Benham,Joshua G. Mangelson*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Two GPS-denied coastal localization pipelines that exploit shoreline and water-surface geometry: (1) LiDAR-based water-surface observations to estimate roll/pitch/heave and align shoreline against a satellite coastline map for global pose; (2) monocular passive imagery to detect shoreline and horizon, infer shoreline distance, accumulate local submaps, and fuse via a hierarchical factor graph against the same coastline map. Zero-shot foundation models can extract shoreline observations across diverse coasts. LiDAR improves trajectory accuracy; monocular pipeline bounds long-term drift; collectively, coastal geometry yields globally referenced localization cues in GPS-denied maritime environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable globally referenced, GPS-denied localization for autonomous surface vessels in coastal regions by exploiting natural coastal geometry and satellite coastline maps as a persistent reference frame.

Method: 1) LiDAR pipeline: uses LiDAR observations of the water surface to estimate roll, pitch, and heave, and directly registers shoreline observations to a satellite-derived coastline map to recover global position and heading. 2) Monocular pipeline: uses passive imagery to semantically segment shoreline and horizon, infers shoreline distance from monocular cues, accumulates shoreline observations into short local submaps, registers them to the satellite coastline map, and fuses within a hierarchical factor graph.

Result: Across three real-world coastal datasets, the LiDAR framework consistently improves trajectory accuracy against standard baselines, while the monocular framework maintains bounded long-term drift. Zero-shot foundation models can reliably extract shoreline observations across diverse coastal environments.

Conclusion: Coastal geometry provides a powerful, globally referenced information source for GPS-denied maritime localization. The two complementary frameworks offer robust localization—LiDAR for precise global pose and orientation, and monocular imagery for drift-bounded tracking—demonstrated on real-world data and enhanced by foundation-model shoreline extraction.

Abstract: Coastal environments contain rich, largely unexploited geometric structure capable of providing globally referenced localization cues. In this work, we present two complementary localization frameworks that exploit shoreline and water-surface geometry for GPS-denied autonomous surface vessel localization. The first framework leverages LiDAR observations of the water surface to estimate roll, pitch, and heave (vertical motion), while recovering global position and heading through direct registration of shoreline observations against a satellite-derived coastline map. The second framework relies solely on passive imagery to detect the shoreline and horizon through semantic segmentation. Using the proposed coastal scene geometry, shoreline distance is inferred from monocular imagery. Shoreline observations are accumulated into short-duration local submaps, registered against the same satellite-derived coastline map, and fused within a hierarchical factor graph. Evaluated across three real-world coastal datasets, the LiDAR pipeline consistently improves trajectory accuracy over standard baselines, while the monocular architecture maintains bounded long-term drift. In addition, we establish that modern zero-shot foundation models can reliably extract shoreline observations across diverse coastal environments. Together, these results demonstrate that coastal geometry provides a powerful and dependable source of globally referenced information for GPS-denied maritime localization.

</details>


### [159] [VT-MUSE: Multimodal Unified Sequential Visuotactile Representation Learning for Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21290)
*Congsheng Xu,Qiaochu Yang,Fangyuan Shi,Yifan Han,Baijun Chen,Yiming Wang,Haonan Zhao,Daolin Ma,Xiaokang Yang,Hesheng Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Two-stage multimodal representation learning for visuotactile manipulation (VT-MUSE): Stage I aligns and fuses modalities with temporal consistency; Stage II uses a conditional variational latent model to capture temporal dynamics, aided by decoders, feeding a Transformer policy. Achieves +11 percentage points over baselines on simulation and shows real-world gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current visuotactile methods encode visual and tactile data separately, missing fine-grained cross-modal and temporal dependencies, especially the evolution of contact over time. A unified sequential representation that jointly models visual context and tactile dynamics is needed for robust manipulation.

Method: Stage I: modality-specific encoders are jointly adapted via cross-modal temporal alignment and masked-view consistency. Stage II: a conditional variational latent model processes masked visual sequences with full tactile histories. Auxiliary decoders reconstruct masked visuals and predict tactile depth changes, encouraging the latent to capture global context and local dynamics. The learned representation feeds a lightweight Transformer policy with gated cross-attention.

Result: In simulation, VT-MUSE outperforms the strongest baseline by 11 percentage points on all tasks and achieves substantial improvements in real-world experiments.

Conclusion: A unified visuotactile sequential representation with cross-modal temporal alignment and predictive decoding improves manipulation performance; the two-stage framework effectively captures both global visual context and local contact dynamics, enhancing policy performance and transfer to real-world settings.

Abstract: We propose VT-MUSE, a Multimodal Unified SEquential representation learning framework for visuotactilemanipulation. Existing approaches often encode visual and tactile observations independently before fusion, limiting their ability to capture fine-grained cross-modal dependencies. Moreover, most methods focus on observations at the current time step and overlook the temporal evolution of contact. VT-MUSE addresses both limitations through a two-stage representation learning framework. In Stage I, modality specific encoders are jointly adapted via cross-modal temporal alignment and masked-view consistency. In Stage II, a conditional variational latent model processes masked visual sequences together with full tactile histories. Auxiliary decoders reconstruct the masked recent visual observations and predict tactile depth changes, encouraging the latent representation to retain both global visual context and local contact dynamics. The learned representation is subsequently integrated into a lightweight Transformer policy through gated cross-attention. On the simulation benchmark, VT-MUSE outperforms the strongest baseline evaluated on all tasks by 11 percentage points and also achieves substantial improvements in real-world experiments.

</details>


### [160] [NeSAM: Neuro-Symbolic Kinodynamics with Soil Adaptation for Off-Road Mobility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21330)
*Chenhui Pan,Tong Xu,Francesco Cancelliere,Xuesu Xiao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: NeSAM combines differentiable Bekker-Wong terramechanics, learned terrain representations, and a Transformer-based residual dynamics model to predict 6-DoF kinodynamics over deformable terrain, with online EKF-based soil parameter updates; validated in simulation and on hardware, showing notable accuracy and trajectory-tracking gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Off-road vehicle motion prediction on deformable terrain is challenged by soil-dependent sinkage, slip, and traction; existing learning-based kinodynamics lack explicit soil mechanics and interpretability, motivating a physics-informed, adaptive approach.

Method: Integrate differentiable Bekker-Wong terramechanics for soil forces with learned terrain encodings; apply a Transformer-based residual dynamics model for long-horizon corrections; online soil-parameter estimation from terrain observations via extended Kalman filter; evaluation in Verti-Bench (Chrono-based) and on a real Verti-4-Wheeler; demonstrated improvement under closed-loop control with online soil adaptation.

Result: Prediction accuracy improved up to 30% in simulation and 29% on real data relative to strong baselines. In closed-loop navigation, traversal success increases with online soil adaptation and the Hausdorff distance to the reference trajectory decreases by 69.4%.

Conclusion: NeSAM provides a physically-informed, interpretable, and adaptive framework for soil–vehicle dynamics, achieving robust performance in both simulation and real-world tests, with implications for broader terrain and platform generalization.

Abstract: Accurate prediction of off-road vehicle motion over deformable terrain remains challenging because sinkage, slip, and traction vary with local soil conditions. Existing learning-based kinodynamic models directly approximate vehicle-terrain interactions from data but do not explicitly represent soil mechanics and offer limited physical interpretability. To address these limitations, we present NeSAM, a neuro-symbolic framework that combines differentiable Bekker-Wong terramechanics with learned terrain representations and a Transformer-based residual dynamics model for long-horizon, six degree-of-freedom kinodynamic prediction. The terramechanics component models soil-dependent interaction forces, while the residual model corrects discrepancies between the analytical prediction and the observed vehicle dynamics. NeSAM further estimates physically meaningful soil parameters from terrain observations and updates them online using an extended Kalman filter. We evaluate NeSAM in Verti-Bench, a simulator built on the Chrono multiphysics engine, and validate its performance on a physical Verti-4-Wheeler platform. NeSAM improves prediction accuracy by up to 30% in simulation and 29% on real-world data relative to the strongest compared baselines. When integrated with a close-loop navigation controller, NeSAM further improves traversal success rate through online soil adaptation while reduces Hausdorff distance to the reference trajectory by 69.4%, indicating improved trajectory tracking accuracy.

</details>


### [161] [ViTacPhys: Physical Property-Aware Grasping from Human Visual-Tactile Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21355)
*Yiwen Liu,Yujun Zhu,Kui Jia,Zhao Liao,Yangwei You,Shuaijun Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ViTacPhys combines visual and tactile signals to estimate object mass, friction, and stiffness from demonstrations and uses these properties to condition a grasping policy, achieving strong in-distribution and notable out-of-distribution performance and successful cross-domain transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve manipulation policies by explicitly leveraging object physical properties predicted from demonstrations, addressing the gap in vision-only models.

Method: Temporal visual-tactile modeling with cross-attention multimodal fusion; semantic priors from a vision-language model; supervised mass and friction classification, stiffness regression; 60 objects; cross-domain transfer via limited robot data, robot-style video augmentation, and matched demonstrations; online adaptive grasping.

Result: On seen objects: mass 97.2% accuracy, friction 98.8% accuracy, stiffness MAPE 5.51%. On held-out from known categories: mass 87.5% accuracy, friction 97.5% accuracy, stiffness MAPE 9.08%. Grasping success: 95.0% in-distribution, 83.4% out-of-distribution. For out-of-distribution grasps, force profiles align more with human teleoperation than ACT.

Conclusion: Explicitly estimating and conditioning on object physical properties enables adaptive grasping; ViTacPhys demonstrates effective cross-domain transfer with limited robot data and augmentation.

Abstract: Recent vision-based action models have demonstrated strong capabilities in complex manipulation, but they rarely leverage explicit object physical properties to adapt their policies. We introduce ViTacPhys, a visual-tactile framework and data acquisition system that estimates object mass and friction-coefficient classes, together with continuous stiffness, from human manipulation demonstrations. Trained on data from 60 rigid and deformable objects, ViTacPhys combines temporal visual-tactile modeling, cross-attention multimodal fusion, and a semantic prior derived from a vision-language model. On seen objects, it achieves 97.2% mass classification accuracy, 98.8% friction-coefficient classification accuracy, and a stiffness mean absolute percentage error (MAPE) of 5.51%. On held-out objects from known categories, it achieves 87.5% mass accuracy, 97.5% friction-coefficient accuracy, and a stiffness MAPE of 9.08%. We transfer ViTacPhys from the human domain to the robot domain using limited robot teleoperation data, robot-style video augmentation, and human demonstrations with matched actions, and deploy it as an online module for adaptive grasping. The resulting physical-property-conditioned policy achieves total grasping success rates of 95.0% on in-distribution objects and 83.4% on out-of-distribution objects. For out-of-distribution objects successfully grasped by both methods, its force profiles are more consistent with human teleoperation than those produced by ACT. These results demonstrate the feasibility of explicitly estimating and conditioning on object physical properties for real-world adaptive grasping.

</details>


### [162] [Mining beyond Earth with Space Robots: Exploration, Sampling, and Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21358)
*Dong Li,Dujun Nie,Xiaotong Zhang,Ruilin Wang,Yuchen Li,Chang Ge,Chao Xiong,Kaichang Di,Andreas Nüchter,Levente Kovács,Qingquan Li,Shirong Ge,Fei-Yue Wang,Long Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Survey of space mining robotics; proposes a six-stage autonomous architecture and roadmap, plus curated datasets and simulation resources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable sustainable off-world exploration and commercial development by overcoming harsh conditions, communication delays, and high launch/operational costs through autonomous robotics.

Method: Comprehensive literature review; policy and stakeholder context; development of a six-stage space mining architecture; curation of mission data, terrestrial analogs, and high-fidelity simulations; identification of challenges and a strategic research roadmap; ongoing updates via a project page.

Result: Defines a six-stage pipeline: (1) remote-sensing-driven exploration, (2) precise in-situ robotic detection, (3) single-robot small-scale sampling, (4) multi-robot large-scale excavation, (5) autonomous resource extraction, (6) final integration into in-situ construction or terrestrial transport. Reviews and curates available data resources and simulation environments; identifies open challenges and outlines a strategic roadmap; maintains an updated project page.

Conclusion: Autonomous space mining is essential for a sustainable off-world economy; the paper provides a structured framework, supporting resources, and a roadmap to guide research and development.

Abstract: Space resource acquisition and utilization, commonly referred to as Space Mining, represent critical pathways for enabling sustained human exploration and unlocking commercial opportunities in space. These resources mainly include helium-3, water, mineral resources on the Moon and Mars, and abundant mineral deposits on asteroids. Due to the harsh conditions of space, communication delays, and high launch costs, the development of autonomous robotic systems is critical to achieving efficient, cost-effective space mining. This paper provides a comprehensive overview of space mining robotics and associated technologies. First, we review the background of space mining, including international policies, commercial entities, and recent advancements. We define a systematic six-stage architecture for space mining: Exploration is initiated by (1) remote sensing for target identification and (2) precise in situ robotic detection; Sampling progresses from (3) single-robot small-scale sampling to (4) multi-robot large-scale excavation; and Extraction integrates (5) autonomous resource extraction and (6) final integration into in situ construction or terrestrial transport. Additionally, we review and curate existing resources for space mining research, including real-world mission data, terrestrial analog datasets, and high-fidelity simulation environments. Finally, we identify critical open challenges in autonomous space mining and delineate a strategic research roadmap to bridge current technological gaps, fostering the transition toward a sustainable off-world economy. To track ongoing developments in space mining, we maintain an updated project page: https://github.com/OpenSpace-Lab/Space-Mining-with-Robotics-List.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [163] [Generalizing Soft Tissue Deformation and Force Prediction Across Material Stiffness and Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20967)
*Madina Kojanazarova,Sidaty El Hadramy,Philippe C. Cattin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Calibrated hyperelastic constitutive models in SOFA train a softness-conditioned equivariant graph neural network (SC-EGNN) to predict deformation and forces across tissues and unseen geometries, achieving sub-millimeter deformation accuracy at 0.01s inference; force accuracy hinges on calibration consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need reliable model selection across tissue stiffness levels and generalization to varying tissue geometries for real-time soft tissue simulation in surgical contexts. Current methods lack systematic guidance for constitutive model choice and cross-domain generalization.

Method: Calibrate hyperelastic constitutive models in the SOFA Framework using gravity-loaded silicone beams with different stiffnesses. Generate training data from calibrated simulations. Train a softness-conditioned equivariant graph neural network to predict deformations and forces across multiple tissue types and unseen geometries, using gravity and geometry as inputs.

Result: The model achieves sub-millimeter mean deformation accuracy with 0.010 s inference time. Force prediction quality improves with calibration consistency, with a direct link to upstream calibration quality. Demonstrates generalization across stiffness levels and unseen geometries.

Conclusion: Systematic calibration of constitutive models is crucial for reliable data-driven surrogates. The softness-conditioned equivariant GNN can generalize across tissue types and unseen geometries, provided the calibration data is consistent. Physical realism (calibrated forces) depends on upstream model calibration.

Abstract: Accurate soft tissue simulation is essential for surgical training, pre-operative planning, and haptic feedback systems. While learning-based surrogate models trained on data using the finite element method (FEM) offer a promising path to real-time inference, their reliability depends on well-calibrated constitutive models. Existing approaches neither provide systematic guidance on model selection across stiffness levels, nor generalize across different tissue stiffnesses or geometries. We perform a comprehensive calibration of hyperelastic constitutive models in the SOFA Framework using gravity-loaded silicone beams with different stiffnesses. Using calibrated simulations as training data, we use a softness conditioned equivariant graph neural network, enabling deformation and force prediction across multiple tissue types and unseen geometries. Our model achieves sub-millimeter mean deformation accuracy at 0.010s inference time, while showing that force prediction quality is directly tied to upstream calibration consistency.

</details>


### [164] [SDAD: Spec-Driven Agentic Development for the AI-Native SDLC](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20341)
*Vu Hung Nguyen,Thanh Nguyen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes Spec-Driven Agentic Development (SDAD): AI agents with large context drive SDLC via formalized specifications, agentic synthesis, and multi-agent verification, governed by explicit metrics; speed is achieved through stronger upfront discipline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve AI-augmented, high-velocity software delivery while maintaining discipline, provenance, and governance; address the Waterfall–Agile pendulum by embedding AI-code as a fourth production paradigm and elevating specification quality as execution fuel.

Method: Define the SDAD framework: (1) intent capture; (2) machine-readable specification; (3) agentic synthesis; (4) independent multi-agent verification with human sign-off. Extend the model to team-role metamorphosis (engineer, QA, platform, product), and introduce quantitative governance metrics (Ambiguity Tax, Spec Fidelity, SER, TCI_agentic with repair multiplier phi); propose a staged-migration blueprint and hybrid estimation for adoption. Integrate industrial/research evidence on AI-augmented testing to motivate separation between synthesis and release authority.

Result: A conceptual framework and governance model for AI-augmented SDLC that argues agentic speed hinges on upstream discipline; anticipates improvements in specification quality, auditable provenance, governance, and clear gates, with a staged path to adoption.

Conclusion: Agentic speed does not remove engineering discipline; it relocates discipline upstream into specification precision, explicit gates, and auditable provenance. AI-code is presented as a fourth production paradigm alongside Waterfall, Agile, and human-guided processes.

Abstract: Frontier coding agents backed by large language models with context windows from hundreds of thousands to millions of tokens are restructuring the Software Development Life Cycle (SDLC). Rich context handling and multi-step reasoning now allow substantial Functional Requirement Documents (FRDs) and repository context to be ingested in a single workflow, making specification quality the execution fuel for autonomous delivery. This report formalises Spec-Driven Agentic Development (SDAD) as a synthesis of disciplined up-front formalisation and high-velocity implementation: intent capture, machine-readable specification, agentic synthesis, and independent multi-agent verification under human sign-off. We revisit the historical pendulum between Waterfall and Agile, introduce AI-code as a fourth production paradigm, and compare Human-Agile (circa 2020) with Agentic-SDAD (circa 2026) across artefacts, cadence, accountability, and security posture. Beyond process description, we extend the model to team role metamorphosis (engineer, QA, platform, and product functions), quantitative governance (Ambiguity Tax, Spec Fidelity, SER, and TCI_agentic with repair multiplier phi), and pragmatic adoption via hybrid estimation and a staged migration blueprint. Industrial and research evidence on AI-augmented testing and verification is integrated to motivate separation between synthesis and release authority. Overall, the paper argues that agentic speed does not eliminate engineering discipline; it relocates discipline upstream into specification precision, explicit gates, and auditable provenance.

</details>


### [165] [PrimeAgentOrchestrator: Memory-Primed Agent Spawning for Personal AI Infrastructure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20342)
*Myron Koch*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PAO preloads Claude Code with memories from two backends, injects briefing via filesystem, and manages lifecycle; an experience report over four months with three context-delivery iterations and tradeoffs of bridging heterogeneous memory systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address LLM coding agents losing accumulated context by provisioning new agent instances with user-specific memories from existing databases.

Method: PAO queries two memory backends in parallel (PostgreSQL observation DB and Cloudflare Worker semantic index), fuses results with backend-specific retrieval strategies, and delivers a briefing via filesystem injection exploiting host auto-read behavior; manages trust seeding, readiness polling with error detection, and adaptive terminal injection across agent lifecycles.

Result: An experience report documenting four months of deployment, detailing three generations of context delivery mechanisms, failure modes driving redesigns, and the engineering tradeoffs of bridging heterogeneous memory systems rather than building a single unified memory backend.

Conclusion: Demonstrates the feasibility of context preloading for coding agents and highlights practical design tradeoffs and failure modes when integrating heterogeneous memory backends, informing future work on memory provisioning for LLM agents.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) coding agents start each session with an empty context window, discarding accumulated knowledge from prior work. We present PrimeAgentOrchestrator (PAO), a system that spawns new instances of Claude Code -- Anthropic's terminal-based coding agent -- pre-loaded with relevant memories compiled from the user's existing personal databases. At spawn time, PAO queries two independently-operated memory backends in parallel (a PostgreSQL entity-observation database and a Cloudflare Worker semantic search index), fuses results using backend-specific retrieval strategies, and delivers the compiled briefing via filesystem injection that exploits the host agent's configuration auto-read behavior. PAO manages the full agent lifecycle including trust pre-seeding, readiness polling with error detection, and adaptive terminal text injection. We report on four months of regular deployment (December 2025 through March 2026) as an experience report, documenting three generations of context delivery mechanisms, the failure modes that motivated each redesign, and the engineering tradeoffs of bridging heterogeneous memory systems rather than building a unified one.

</details>


### [166] [Truth Lies Deep: Countering Semantic Camouflage via Latent Intent Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20378)
*Md. Hasib Ur Rahman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes Latent Intent Verification (LIV) to detect adversarial semantic camouflage by exploiting an 'Intent Horizon' in early model layers; shows 20–50% improvement over guardrails on PKU-SafeRLHF without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current safety guardrails are brittle, often relying on late-stage refusals and not erasing harmful concepts from pretraining. Semantic camouflage hides harmful intent in benign narratives, exploiting the disconnect between pretraining knowledge and post-hoc safety gating.

Method: Analyze latent activation trajectories across three SLM families (Phi-3, Qwen2.5, Gemma-2b) under adversarial stress to identify a universal Intent Horizon (~15–20% depth) where harmful intent collapses into a safe narrative. Propose Latent Intent Verification (LIV) as a lightweight probing defense and evaluate on the PKU-SafeRLHF dataset, comparing to standard guardrails across architectures without retraining.

Result: Identified a depth at which harmful intent becomes indistinguishable in late layers but remains detectable in early layers. LIV outperforms standard guardrails by 20–50% across Phi-3, Qwen2.5, and Gemma-2b, effectively neutralizing zero-day semantic attacks without model retraining.

Conclusion: Early-layer latent representations preserve a detectable ‘harm signature’ that can be leveraged by LIV. This approach provides a lightweight, architecture-agnostic defense that complements existing guardrails and suggests a shift toward latent representation monitoring in safety pipelines.

Abstract: Safety alignment in Large Language Models (LLMs) is often superficial, relying on refusal mechanisms that trigger only at the final stages of generation without erasing the foundational knowledge of harmful concepts acquired during pretraining. This study demonstrates that this architectural disconnect leaves models vulnerable to Semantic Camouflage -- adversarial attacks that wrap harmful intent in benign narrative contexts (e.g., creative writing), effectively bypassing standard input and output guardrails. By analyzing the latent activation trajectories of three distinct Small Language Model (SLM) families (Phi-3, Qwen2.5, and Gemma-2b) under adversarial stress, this research identifies a universal ``Intent Horizon'' -- a critical depth (typically 15--20\% of total layers) where the model's distinct, pre-trained representation of harmful intent collapses as it contextualizes the query into a ``safe'' narrative. Results indicate that while late-layer representations of camouflaged attacks are mathematically indistinguishable from safe queries (Detection Rate $< 20\%$), early-layer representations retain a distinct, detectable ``harm signature.'' Leveraging this insight, this paper proposes Latent Intent Verification (LIV), a lightweight probing defense. Experiments on the PKU-SafeRLHF dataset demonstrate that LIV outperforms standard guardrails by a margin of 20--50\% across all tested architectures, effectively neutralizing zero-day semantic attacks without requiring model retraining.

</details>


### [167] [A Survey on Foundations and Frontiers of Multimodal Agentic Frameworks: Techniques and Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20379)
*Neel Mokaria,Rishie Raj,Dheeraj Baiju,Xiaoqian Shen,Shraman Pramanick,Kevin Qinghong Lin,Arda Senocak,Mike Zheng Shou,Philip Torr,Mohamed Elhoseiny,Yapeng Tian,Ruohan Gao,Salman Khan,Sayan Nag,Sanjoy Chowdhury,Dinesh Manocha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A survey analyzing how multimodality shapes agentic systems across core modules, proposing a modality-centric taxonomy, and evaluating architectures, domains, and scalability to chart a path for robust multimodal agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With advances in LLMs and LMMs, there is a need to systematically understand how multimodal inputs influence perception, reasoning, planning, memory, and action in autonomous agents, and how these modalities are integrated architecturally.

Method: Literature review and synthesis of multimodal agentic work, organized by a modality-centric taxonomy (perception, reasoning, planning, memory, action) and fusion type (delegated, late-fusion, early-fusion); cross-domain analysis of Robotics, GUI/Web, Multimedia editing/generation, and Video understanding/retrieval; evaluation of performance and efficiency trade-offs.

Result: Proposes a taxonomy linking architectural choices to agent capabilities, traces evolution from text-centric to multimodal agents, analyzes grounded perception and multimodal reasoning for emergent agentic behaviors, and evaluates efficiency-scalability trade-offs across domains; highlights gaps and prospects for robust, general-purpose multimodal agents.

Conclusion: Multimodality is pivotal for grounded, capable agentic systems. The work maps design space and performance trends, identifies gaps, and outlines a roadmap toward robust, general-purpose multimodal agents, emphasizing efficiency, scalability, and deployment considerations.

Abstract: Advances in large language models (LLMs) have fueled a wave of research into agency: the ability to reason, plan, and act. This effort has produced agentic frameworks that orchestrate perception, memory, and decision-making around powerful LLM backbones. With the advent of large multimodal models (LMMs), these systems can process and integrate diverse modalities, including images, audio, and video, thereby improving their real-world applicability. Yet, while surveys of LLM-based agents exist, the role of multimodality in shaping agency has not been systematically examined in recent years. This survey fills the gap by analyzing the impact of multimodality across the core functional modules of the agentic framework: perception, reasoning, planning, memory, and action. Using this lens, we trace the evolution from text-centric agents to multimodal frameworks, examine how modalities are integrated through delegated, late-fusion, and early-fusion architectures, and assess the emergence of agentic behaviors enabled by grounded perception and multimodal reasoning. We organize existing work through a modality-centric taxonomy that links architectural design choices to agent capabilities. Moreover, we review multimodal agentic systems across various application domains, including Robotics, GUI & Web Navigation, Multimedia Content Generation & Editing, and Long-form Video Understanding & Retrieval. Beyond capabilities, we analyze performance across these settings and discuss efficiency-scalability trade-offs, including training and inference costs, latency, and deployment constraints. By focusing on the impact of multimodality in agentic design, we aim to identify key gaps and chart a roadmap toward robust and general-purpose intelligent systems.

</details>


### [168] [Interpretable Multimodal Classification with Linear Discriminant Tree Ensembles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20384)
*Mojtaba Moattari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Tree-based ensembles balance accuracy and interpretability for multimodal affective classification, outperforming a Multimodal Transformer and an interpretable baseline, with improved human-annotator agreement on feature importance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need trustworthy multimodal affect classifiers that provide interpretable cues. Transformers offer strong accuracy but limited interpretability; there is a gap for models delivering both.

Method: Encode modalities into tokens, cluster concepts to reduce dimensionality, route fused modalities through tree-based ensemble classifiers (LDT, LDF, LDAB), and apply a novel modified feature importance metric that downweights the negative class. Evaluation compares against Multimodal Transformer and Interpretable Multimodal Routing (IMR); metrics include F1-mod, accuracy, and human-annotator agreement on salient features (IEMOCAP, CMU-MOSI).

Result: F1-mod gains of 4.3 percentage points over the Multimodal Transformer; accuracy gains of 3.0 percentage points over IMR. Feature-importance-based salience shows higher human-annotator agreement (62.2% vs. 43.2% on IEMOCAP; 46.7% vs. 32.1% on CMU-MOSI).

Conclusion: Tree-based ensembles offer competitive accuracy with enhanced interpretability for multimodal affective tasks. The modified feature importance improves marker detection and cross-modal concept explanations, presenting a viable alternative to opaque deep transformers in trust-sensitive settings.

Abstract: Multimodal affect and behaviour classifiers that fuse heterogeneous text, audio, and visual streams must simultaneously achieve competitive accuracy and produce human-understandable explanations of the cues driving their decisions -- a dual objective that current high-capacity models, notably Transformers, only partially address. While Transformers attain strong predictive performance, their distributed representations and deep nonlinearity make it difficult to assign meaningful importance weights to individual multimodal features, limiting their use in trust-sensitive applications such as clinical affect monitoring and educational assessment. We address this gap by developing a framework based on tree-based ensembles that balances accuracy and interpretability. The framework encodes each modality into tokens, extracts and clusters concepts to reduce dimensionality, routes the fused modalities through tree-based ensemble classifiers, and interprets trends using a novel modified feature importance metric. The modified importance reduces the influence of the negative class in binary classification tasks, thereby improving indicator or marker detection. The proposed tree-based ensembles -- Linear Discriminant Tree (LDT), Linear Discriminant Forest (LDF), and Linear Discriminant AdaBoost (LDAB) -- achieve F1-mod gains of 4.3\% over the Multimodal Transformer and accuracy gains of 3.0\% over the primary interpretable multimodal baseline, Interpretable Multimodal Routing (IMR). The proposed multimodal feature importance extracts salient inter-modal concepts with substantially higher human-annotator agreement scores than default feature importance (62.2\% vs.\ 43.2\% on IEMOCAP; 46.7\% vs.\ 32.1\% on CMU-MOSI).

</details>


### [169] [Representation Affects Retrieval: A Case Study of Skill Discovery and Routing in a Multimodal Agent Harness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20389)
*Kevin Dela Rosa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: In-prompt exposure of skills in Tinycloud is non-monotonic: full autoload of skills ensures correct selection across tasks, while partial exposure can cause lexical competition that misroutes the planner; the work is a small-N case study linking to retrieval-based skill routing at scale rather than a benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate how small-scale, in-prompt exposure of available skills affects the planner's selection, and what this implies for embedding-based retrieval approaches at scale.

Method: Six-task ablation across three exposure regimes (all-on, default, all-off) evaluating two skill representations (tool-skills and workflow-skills) exposed via two system-prompt surfaces (inlined-body autoloaded vs. one-line on-demand) within the Tinycloud production harness.

Result: Full autoload exposure selects the gold skill on every task; all-off slows execution and causes hard discovery failures; default exposure misroutes a task due to lexical signal competition between an autoloaded tool-skill and a listed workflow-skill. Overall, in-prompt exposure is not monotonically helpful; partial exposure can hinder correct selection.

Conclusion: The findings illustrate non-monotonic effects of in-prompt skill exposure in small-scale settings and motivate careful design; the work serves as a case study guiding how in-context prompts relate to large-scale retrieval-based skill routing, rather than a benchmark.

Abstract: A production agent harness must discover and rank, from a growing library of skills, the one most appropriate for a user's task. At small scale this selection happens in context: the LLM planner chooses among skill representations exposed in its system prompt, without an explicit embedding-based retrieval step. We treat this in-context selection as the small-N counterpart to embedding-based skill retrieval at scale, and present a case study of how Tinycloud, a production multimodal video agent harness, represents its skills for the planner. The harness ships skills under two recurring representations: tool-skills that wrap a single external API or system tool and serve as primitive vocabulary, and workflow-skills that orchestrate tool-skill calls plus a template render to produce one named deliverable. The harness exposes them via two surfaces in the system prompt: an inlined-body surface (full instructions, scripts, templates) for autoloaded skills, and a one-line listing for on-demand skills. A six-task selection ablation across three exposure regimes (all-on, default, all-off) shows that full autoload selects the gold skill on every task; all-off slows execution and produces hard discovery failures; and the production default misroutes one task because its lexical signal collides with an autoloaded tool-skill that pulls planner attention away from a listed workflow-skill. The headline finding is that in-prompt exposure of skills is not monotonically helpful: partial exposure can create lexical competition that suppresses correct selection. We connect this small-N observation to recent retrieval-based skill-routing work at large scale, and frame this contribution as a case study rather than a benchmark.

</details>


### [170] [Nexus: Depth-Adaptive KV-Cache Splicing and Retrieval-Decoupled Tool Routing for Agentic LLMs on Unified Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20397)
*Mustafa Arslan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Nexus decouples tool routing from schema prefill using an 8-bit semantic lookaside buffer and compressed textual signatures, achieving depth-independent routing accuracy (~89% up to 250 tools) and TTFT speedups (1.1–1.7x) with fidelity guarantees, at the cost of RoPE drift limitations and a poorly predictive drift gate; results are model-tuple specific.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quadratic prefill costs from re-encoding verbose tool schemas per turn dominate time-to-first-token as tool registries scale; need scalable routing that doesn't depend on continually re-prefilling full schemas.

Method: Use an INT8 SLB with a cross-encoder margin gate to select tools by retrieval; generate arguments from compressed textual signatures (~19 tokens) rather than spliced KV; compare to full-schema prefill and concatenate-all-schemas baselines; optionally transplant a compiled schema KV block into live context with RoPE-aware redecode; evaluate on Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct Q4_K_M on Apple-silicon unified memory.

Result: Routing accuracy remains ~89% as registry scales to 250 tools; full-schema prefill baseline overflows the context window; first-argument token arrives 1.66x sooner than full prefill; ~80% main-context token saving. Secondary path yields 1.1–1.7x TTFT speedup at moderate depth; never-regress fidelity (top-1, D_KL ~0); latency can dip to 0.98x before converging to parity. RoPE phase drift limits: beyond P=256 anchored splice requires depth-adaptive suffix redecode; two negative results: off-anchor RoPE fidelity boundary and drift gate failure (Spearman rho = 0.193). Measurements from a single model tuple (Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct Q4_K_M) on Apple-silicon unified memory; qualitative boundaries generalize; quantitative envelope is tuple-specific.

Conclusion: Nexus offers a scalable, retrieval-based routing approach that decouples routing from schema prefill and delivers depth-independent routing accuracy and latency improvements with fidelity guarantees, but faces RoPE-drift constraints and drift-prediction challenges; results are model-tuple specific and further work is needed to broaden generalization and address the drift gate weakness.

Abstract: Agentic large language models (LLMs) on the Model Context Protocol (MCP) re-encode verbose tool schemas every turn, so prefill - quadratic in sequence length - dominates time-to-first-token (TTFT) as the tool registry grows. Nexus's primary lever is to decouple routing from the schema-prefill cost: an INT8 semantic lookaside buffer (SLB) with a calibrated cross-encoder margin gate selects tools by retrieval, and arguments are generated over a compressed textual signature (median 19 tokens) rather than over spliced key/value (KV) cache. This path is depth-independent: routing accuracy stays near 89% as the registry scales to 250 tools - where a concatenate-all-schemas baseline overflows the context window entirely - and it reaches a first-argument token 1.66x sooner than a full-schema re-prefill at a ~80% main-context token saving. As a secondary, bounded lever we transplant a compiled schema KV block directly into the live context. This is fundamentally limited by rotary position embedding (RoPE) phase drift: an anchored splice is output-exact, but off-anchor placement corrupts attention, so beyond a threshold P=256 Nexus repairs the seam with a depth-adaptive suffix redecode that escalates to a full re-prefill. The resulting never-regress property is a guarantee on output fidelity (top-1 agreement, D_KL approx. 0) - not on latency, which can dip to 0.98x before converging to parity - alongside a 1.1-1.7x TTFT speedup at moderate depth that narrows to parity at deep context. Two negative results bound the design: the off-anchor RoPE fidelity boundary, and the failure of a reference-free drift gate to predict drift (Spearman rho = 0.193). All measurements are from one model tuple (Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct Q4_K_M) on Apple-silicon unified memory; the qualitative boundaries generalize, while the quantitative envelope is tuple-specific.

</details>


### [171] [Environmental Slow AI: Design Principles for Generative Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20398)
*Vanessa Utz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes Slow AI-inspired design principles to reframe generative AI around environmental sustainability, detailing five principles (restraint, sufficiency, selectivity over retention, material visibility, friction as affordance) with dual design and interpretive layers to enhance reflection and user agency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the embedding of maximalist cultural values in generative AI systems by redirecting design priorities toward environmental sustainability through an environmental humanities lens.

Method: Conceptual analysis within environmental humanities; derivation of five design principles under Slow AI; illustrative critique against widely deployed AI systems; dual-layer framing (design implementation + interpretive layer).

Result: Five design principles articulated with dual levels: implementation and interpretive prompts; each illustrated against current systems; collective aim to expand human agency by reintroducing deliberate choice through friction and visibility.

Conclusion: Reorienting AI design around sustainability via Slow AI can foster reflective engagement and preserve/develop human agency in the face of frictionless defaults.

Abstract: Generative AI (genAI) systems produce cultural artefacts at scale, but they also reflect embedded cultural values through their design. Once identified, these values become open to deliberate reshaping. This position paper examines the maximalist values of current generative AI through an environmental humanities tradition and proposes design principles in which environmental sustainability serves as the core value instead. The principles are developed under the umbrella of Slow AI, a term that already circulates across several distinct research and practice programs. Five design principles are articulated (restraint, sufficiency, selectivity over retention, material visibility, and friction as affordance), each of them illustrated against the current design of widely deployed systems. Each principle operates at two levels: a design implementation, and an interpretive layer at which users and developers are prompted toward reflective engagement with the system. Together these principles extend human agency by restoring decisions that frictionless defaults have silently removed and do so by building interpretive reflection into design.

</details>


### [172] [When Retrieval Fails Before It Begins: Structurally Indirect Prerequisite Eviction as a Retention Failure in Agentic Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20400)
*Minkyu Song*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Identifies and mitigates a pre-retrieval eviction failure in budgeted agentic memory using a one-hop graph-aware DSGC rule, achieving large retention gains and providing a reproducible benchmark and diagnostics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
