Motivation: Move beyond retrieval-centric models to study pre-retrieval failures caused by budget eviction of upstream blocks, offering operational definitions, benchmarks, and diagnostics; introduce DSGC to mitigate such failures.

Method: Define pre-retrieval failure; develop deterministic benchmark; provide per-seed trace diagnostics; implement DSGC, a one-hop graph-aware garbage collection; evaluate with lexical and sentence encoders; perform robustness analyses across budget and scaling; release pipeline and failure postmortem.

Result: Retention improved: lexical encoder 0.90 vs 0.03; sentence encoder 1.00 vs 0.23; robustness mapping; pipeline released; postmortem supports mechanistic insights.

Conclusion: Pre-retrieval eviction constitutes a distinct failure boundary; graph-aware one-hop DSGC effectively mitigates it under certain budgets/scales; framework enables reproducible mechanistic analysis.

Abstract: Agentic memory under a fixed budget involves two stages: retention and retrieval. Existing retrieval-centered paradigms implicitly assume necessary evidence survives eviction, but we challenge this by isolating a pre-retrieval failure mode: structurally indirect prerequisite eviction, in which upstream blocks weakly aligned with the query are discarded under budget pressure. We provide an operational definition of this failure, a reproducible deterministic benchmark, and per-seed trace diagnostics. Finally, we evaluate Dependency-aware Semantic Garbage Collection (DSGC), a one-hop graph-aware rule. In our main suite, DSGC improves full-chain retention from 0.03 to 0.90 under a lexical encoder and from 0.23 to 1.00 under a sentence encoder. Robustness checks then identify the budget and scaling regimes where the one-hop rule holds or degrades. Our released pipeline and failure postmortem support mechanistic analysis of retention before retrieval as a distinct failure boundary.

</details>


### [173] [World models of environment, agent and joint agent-environment systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20401)
*Manuel Baltieri,Filippo Torresan,Yivan Zhang,Alexander Boyd,Fernando E. Rosas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Defines three predictive channels for world models in model-based RL (environment O|A, agent A|O, and the joint (A,O)) and builds canonical predictive models via ε-transducers/ε-machines. Shows that environment models factor through joint causal states under closed-loop coupling (dually for agent) and provides a POMDP example with a finite canonical model despite an unrestricted environment model. Clarifies what is being modeled and how coupling/restriction affect predictive structure and complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve ambiguity in what world models predict by focusing on the modeling channel rather than only predicted variables. Leverages computational mechanics to define canonical models for environment, agent, and joint interactions, and to analyze how coupling and support restrictions shape predictive structure.

Method: Apply computational mechanics to define ε-transducers/ε-machines as canonical predictive models for three channels (environment O|A, agent A|O, joint (A,O)). Derive factorization results showing environment models factor through joint causal states; exploit duality for the agent; construct closed-loop, support-restricted models; illustrate with a POMDP/controller example where canonical models are finite.

Result: Canonical environment models recover standard predictive state representations; agent and joint models provide analogous notions. In closed-loop coupling, predictive equivalences extend over realizable interaction continuations. Canonical environment states factor through joint causal states, with transitions induced by the joint model; the agent-side construction is dual. A POMDP example demonstrates a finite canonical model under restriction even when the unrestricted environment model has infinitely many states.

Conclusion: The framework clarifies what different world models are modeling, and how coupling and support restriction influence the canonical predictive structure and complexity. It provides new canonical models (ε-transducers) for environment, agent, and joint systems and highlights how coupling shapes model size and predictive capabilities.

Abstract: World models are a central component of model-based reinforcement learning. They are usually discussed in terms of what variables they predict, such as observations, rewards, states, latent or information states. We argue that there is a prior distinction: which channel they model. We consider three cases: the environment channel $O_{:} \mid A_{:}$, the agent channel $A_{:} \mid O_{:}$, and the realised joint process $(A, O)_{:}$, equivalently viewed as a channel with no inputs. Using computational mechanics, we define canonical predictive models for these three cases as $ε$-transducers or $ε$-machines. Canonical environment models recover standard predictive state representations, while the other two give analogous notions of canonical models for the agent and the joint system. We then build canonical support-restricted environment and agent models induced by closed-loop coupling, whose predictive equivalences range over continuations supported by the realised interaction. The key structural result is that canonical support-restricted environment states factor through the canonical joint causal states, and their transition structure is induced directly from the joint model; the agent-side construction is dual. Finally, we give a POMDP/controller example in which the unrestricted environment model has infinitely many states while the canonical support-restricted model induced by the coupling is finite. The framework clarifies what different world models are models of, and how coupling and support restriction can change their canonical predictive structure and complexity.

</details>


### [174] [StateSight: Benchmarking Latent Spatial-State Reconstruction in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20414)
*Michelle Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: StateSight: a procedurally generated benchmark for spatial-state reasoning in vision-language models, with three tasks and a companion StateSight-Steps dataset; findings show format-valid answers can mask spatial reconstruction failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Isolate latent spatial structure reasoning from perception, OCR, and language priors in multimodal QA; existing benchmarks conflate these elements.

Method: Three task families (cube-net opposite-face reasoning, occluded cube-tower counting, 4-neighbor connected-component counting); 300 single-image prompts per task with deterministic oracle labels and exact-match scoring; evaluated GPT-5.5 and Claude Sonnet 5; 30-participant human baseline; visible-derivation analysis; StateSight-Steps: 900 interleaved image-text examples and 3,600 deterministic intermediate visual states.

Result: Model accuracies: GPT-5.5: 59.3%, 33.3%, 28.3%; Claude Sonnet 5: 53.3%, 18.7%, 7.3%; humans outperform models on all tasks: 80.8%, 68.8%, 64.3%; final direct runs with zero format errors; visible-derivation analysis reveals recurring errors in image-state reconstruction and reasoning; StateSight-Steps helps diagnose when format-valid outputs mask spatial-reasoning failures.

Conclusion: Format validity is insufficient to assess spatial-structure understanding; intermediate-state datasets (StateSight-Steps) are essential to diagnose and improve verifiable visual inference in VLMs.

Abstract: Vision-language models are increasingly used for multimodal question answering, yet their ability to reconstruct latent spatial structure from a single image remains difficult to isolate. Broad benchmarks often combine perception, optical character recognition, domain knowledge, linguistic priors, and reasoning in the same evaluation. We introduce StateSight, a procedurally generated benchmark for cube-net opposite-face reasoning, occluded cube-tower counting, and 4-neighbor connected-component counting. Each task family contains 300 single-image prompts with deterministic oracle labels and exact-match scoring. OpenAI GPT-5.5, using the API model identifier gpt-5.5, achieved 59.3%, 33.3%, and 28.3% accuracy across the three tasks, while Claude Sonnet 5 achieved 53.3%, 18.7%, and 7.3%. All final direct runs had zero format errors. A 30-participant human baseline on 60 items exceeded both models on every task, with mean accuracies of 80.8%, 68.8%, and 64.3%. Visible-derivation analysis identified recurring errors in image-state reconstruction and reasoning procedure. We also introduce StateSight-Steps, a companion dataset of 900 interleaved image-text examples and 3,600 deterministic intermediate visual states. The results show that format-valid responses can mask failures to recover the spatial structure required for verifiable visual inference.

</details>


### [175] [Categorical AI phenomenology: A first-person approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20420)
*Robert Prentner*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Phenomenology-first, category-theoretic framework for artificial consciousness using Q-networks as relational interfaces, aligning with 4E cognition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fill the gap in artificial phenomenology by grounding subjective experience in agent–world interfaces and first-person structures; formalize via categories derived from Q-networks to capture actions and invariants; integrate 4E cognition with computation.

Method: Propose a phenomenology-first methodology; model first-person structures with categories derived from Q-networks; interpret Q-networks as relational interfaces encoding agent–world interaction; relate to how dynamical states depend on inputs, history, and actions; situate within enactive, embedded, and extended cognition; employ categorical mathematics.

Result: Offers a rigorous, relational, and phenomenological account of artificial phenomenology; principled framework for embedding information-processing into phenomenological structure; connects Q-network interfaces to 4E cognition.

Conclusion: Provides a coherent theory of artificial phenomenology via interface consciousness grounded in category theory; advances 4E cognition in AI; enables formalization of subjective-like experience in computational systems.

Abstract: This paper develops a phenomenology-first approach to artificial consciousness by reframing consciousness as the subjective experience enacted through an agent's interface with the world. We shift the methodological focus to first-person structures, modeled mathematically by categories derived from Q-networks to capture actions and phenomenological invariants. In this framework, Q-networks are conceptualized as relational interfaces encoding agent-world interaction, analogous to how the dynamical states of a computer depend on its sensory inputs, previous states, and actions. Our work provides a rigorous framework for interface consciousness to describe computational systems that embed information-processing into phenomenological structure. The approach aligns with 4E approaches to cognition by emphasizing enactive, embedded, and extended dimensions of experience. The paper thus offers a principled, relational, and phenomenological account of artificial phenomenology grounded in categorical mathematics.

</details>


### [176] [Who Delegates to AI? Evidence from 53,000 Agent Configurations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20425)
*Hyeongjae Lee,Jihyang Cheon,Lanu Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces the Agentic Adoption Index (AAI) to measure delegated exposure to AI by quantifying alignment between agentic work routines and existing agentive skill specs; uses 53k skills from Manus Skills Marketplace and 18k O*NET tasks to derive occupation-level AAI; finds distinct delegation patterns from prior risk frameworks, shows AAI aligns more with AI capabilities than current usage, and reveals a mid-range wage/education pattern with major variation explained by technical availability though not entirely, suggesting discretion or codification limits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To capture actual AI adoption (delegated exposure) in the labor market, rather than mere exposure to AI capabilities, by quantifying how closely workers’ workflow routines have been integrated with AI-enabled tasks.

Method: Link agentic skills (≈53,000 from Manus Skills Marketplace) to O*NET task statements (≈18,000) via semantic similarity; aggregate to the occupation level to construct the Agentic Adoption Index (AAI); compare AAI with established exposure measures and with wage/education distributions; decompose variance into technical availability vs other factors; assess cross-occupation patterns of delegation.

Result: Three main findings: (i) delegation-concentrating occupations differ sharply from those flagged as high-risk by pre-AI frameworks; (ii) AAI tracks AI capabilities more closely than workers’ current usage; (iii) AAI peaks below top wages and bachelor-level education, declining at both extremes. Technical availability explains most variance but not the shortage among the most educated occupations, implying factors beyond feasibility (e.g., codification limits or discretionary pacing). Repeated measurements are needed to monitor divergence over time.

Conclusion: The proposed AAI adds a dynamic, adoption-focused layer to AI exposure metrics, highlighting a gap between AI capability and actual delegated use and suggesting that both codification processes and professional discretion shape adoption. Ongoing, time-series measurement is required to track how delegation evolves and interacts with policy and organizational change.

Abstract: A growing literature measures how far occupations are exposed to AI, but these measures capture where AI could perform tasks, not whether workers have adopted it. We propose a new layer of exposure, delegated exposure, which records whether a worker has committed a task to AI by building it into a workflow. We operationalize it as the Agentic Adoption Index (AAI), which measures how closely an occupation's tasks match the agentic routines practitioners have already built and shared. We embed roughly 53,000 agent skill specifications from the Manus Skills Marketplace, compute their semantic similarity to about 18,000 O*NET task statements, and aggregate to the occupation level. Three findings follow. First, the occupations where delegation concentrates differ sharply from those pre-AI frameworks identified as most at risk. Second, the AAI tracks what AI could do more closely than what workers currently use it for. Third, the AAI peaks below the top of the wage distribution and at the bachelor's level, declining at both extremes. Technical availability explains most of this variation, but not the shortfall among the most educated occupations, so feasibility alone cannot account for who adopts. That shortfall may reflect work that resists advance specification, or professional discretion over the pace of codification. Distinguishing the two, and tracking how these measures diverge over time, will require repeated measurement.

</details>


### [177] [STCO: Conditional Neural Operators for Time-Dependent PDEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20477)
*Xingxin Yang,Zhan Zhang,Juan Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces Spatiotemporal Conditional Operator (STCO) for prescribed-condition operator learning (PCOL) to inject target-time condition fields into neural operators, improving predictive accuracy in PDE surrogates across multiple backbones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural operators often predict future states from observed states and static problem descriptors; for control/optimization tasks, predictions should be conditioned on prescribed motions, inflows, or forcing rather than determined solely by the state.

Method: Proposes STCO with a condition interface that combines Flow-Aware Graph Leaf (FAGL) and Dual-Site Feature-wise Linear Modulation (DSFiLM). FAGL uses final-frame vorticity to create an adaptive partition and co-locates history and target-time condition fields; DSFiLM routes motion, inflow, and force signals through slot- and channel-wise gates before and after core operator computation. Evaluated across twelve backbones on an immersed-boundary CFD benchmark with varied prescribed conditions.

Result: Across twelve backbones, three regimes, and two lead ranges, STCO achieves mean reductions of 31.1% in relative-L2 field error and 24.7% in normalized pressure-derived load error. It reduces longer-lead field error for 11 backbones, and targeted interventions on condition groups yield measurable prediction changes for all groups.

Conclusion: STCO provides a flexible, architecture-agnostic interface for incorporating prescribed-condition fields into neural operators, yielding significant accuracy gains and enabling improved control/optimization performance in PDE surrogates.

Abstract: Neural operators have emerged as efficient surrogates for time-dependent physical systems governed by partial differential equations (PDEs), but their future-state predictions are often conditioned only on observed states and static problem descriptors. For control or optimization, however, body motion, inflow, or forcing are prescribed for the query without being determined solely by the observed state. We introduce the Spatiotemporal Conditional Operator (STCO) for prescribed-condition operator learning (PCOL), a common interface that supplies prescribed target-time condition fields to heterogeneous backbone architectures while retaining their architecture-specific core computation and context pathways. Its condition interface combines Flow-Aware Graph Leaf (FAGL) with Dual-Site Feature-wise Linear Modulation (DSFiLM). Non-learned FAGL uses vorticity from the final observed frame to construct a fixed-cardinality adaptive partition, then co-locates the observed history and target-time condition fields at its regional coordinates. DSFiLM injects separate motion, inflow, and force routes before and after operator computation through current-feature-driven slot- and channel-wise gates. We evaluate twelve matched backbone architectures with different existing physical and temporal inputs. The immersed-boundary computational fluid dynamics (CFD) benchmark spans prescribed motion, inflow disturbances, body-force actuation, and morphology. Across twelve matched backbones, three regimes, and two lead ranges, STCO yields mean paired reductions of 31.1% in relative-L2 field error and 24.7% in normalized pressure-derived load error. It also lowers longer-lead field error for 11 backbones, while interventions on individual condition groups produce measurable prediction changes for every group evaluated.

</details>


### [178] [Terminal Agents: A Survey of AI Agents in Command-Line Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20485)
*Yi Bin,Xiaoyang Yuan,Haoxi Zeng,Wencheng Ye,Wenqi Shao,Chen Qian,Wei Ye,Yujuan Ding,Zheng Wang,Pengpeng Zeng,Jingkuan Song,Heng Tao Shen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A survey framing terminal-mediated LLM agents through a seven-dimensional competence profile, linking model, interface, harness, runtime, and environment; highlights evaluation gaps and the need for replayable traces and process-level evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Terminal-mediated behavior is increasingly central to LLM action but is dispersed across software engineering, tool use, and HCI literatures; a unified framework is needed to reason about workload-level behavior, learning from executable trajectories, and robust evaluation.

Method: Conceptual synthesis that defines a seven-dimensional terminal competence profile; analyzes how model, interface, harness, runtime, and environment shape observable behavior; introduces bounded fixed-condition diagnostics to study benchmark signals and attribution across systems.

Result: Behavior is jointly shaped by model, interface, harness, runtime, and environment. Executable trajectories ground learning in action consequences and recovery; prevailing evaluations focus on final outcomes and provide uneven coverage of process quality, recovery, and governance. Diagnostics show benchmark families yield different process signals; matched-system comparisons reveal benchmark-dependent performance and limits of component attribution.

Conclusion: Proposes a unified framework for studying terminal-mediated agency across software engineering and emerging domains, urging explicit reporting of system/runtime conditions, replayable traces, and process-level evidence to enable fair comparisons and robust attribution.

Abstract: Large language model agents increasingly act through terminals, yet existing surveys disperse terminal-mediated behavior across software engineering, tool use, and computer-use research. We regard terminal agents as systems whose dominant progress-bearing action--observation loop is mediated by terminal command execution, textual feedback, and stateful environment interaction. Using terminal-mediated execution as an organizing lens, this survey establishes workload-level boundaries and connects system architecture, competence acquisition, and evaluation through a seven-dimensional terminal competence profile. Our synthesis shows that realized behavior is jointly shaped by the model, interface, harness, runtime, and environment. Executable trajectories ground learning in action consequences, verification, and recovery, whereas prevailing evaluations emphasize final outcomes and expose process quality, recovery, and governance unevenly. Bounded fixed-condition diagnostics illustrate two implications: benchmark families expose different process signals, and matched system comparisons reveal benchmark-dependent performance and limits of component attribution. These findings motivate explicit reporting of system and runtime conditions, supported by replayable traces and process-level evidence. The framework provides a unified basis for studying terminal-mediated agency across software engineering and emerging application domains.

</details>


### [179] [Lost in Translation: How Universal Ethical Values Fail to Translate Across Global Contexts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20490)
*Ozioma C. Oguine,Munachimso B. Oguine,Cesar Cervera,Jenny Yang,Pooja Voladoddi,Mario Rodriguez,Saif Eddin Bani Malhem,Karla Badillo-Urquiola,Daricia Wilkinson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Global AI ethics frameworks clash with local contexts; experts reinterpret core values, revealing translation gaps and governance needs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate how universal AI ethics values are understood and applied in practice across diverse settings, and identify how context shapes ethical interpretation and governance.

Method: Qualitative study of 14 experts across 10 countries examining sense-making of AI in practice, reinterpretation of core values, and envisioned governance alternatives; analysis of structural constraints and risk perceptions.

Result: Findings show structurally unequal deployment conditions (infrastructure, extractive practices, tech mystification) shaping risk/opportunity perceptions; values reinterpreted to local moral logics—privacy as collective/relational, transparency as trust-building accountability, fairness as equity in access/representation; identifies translation gaps between encoded frameworks and local practices.

Conclusion: Advocate plural governance with redistributed epistemic authority and ongoing, context-sensitive ethical negotiation rather than settled technical standards.

Abstract: AI ethics frameworks treat values such as fairness, transparency, and accountability as universal and uniformly operationalizable across contexts. We examined how 14 experts across 10 countries made sense of AI in practice, reinterpreted core values, and envisioned governance alternatives. We found that AI deployment is characterized by structurally unequal conditions, marked by infrastructural constraints, extractive practices, and a "mystification" of technology, which fundamentally shape perceptions of risks and opportunities. Our findings reveal that experts reinterpret values to fit local moral logics: privacy as collective and relational rather than individual; transparency as trust-building accountability rather than technical disclosure; and fairness as equity in access and representation rather than parity in outcomes. We identify these as translation gaps between encoded global frameworks and situated local practices. Finally, we propose pathways toward plural governance that redistributes epistemic authority and treats ethical negotiation as an ongoing, context-sensitive process rather than a settled technical standard.

</details>


### [180] [A Temporal Planning Approach for Intelligent Flood Response](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20510)
*Fazlul Hasan Siddiqui,Md. Monjurul Islam,Sabah Binte Noor*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Temporal-planning-based flood-response framework enabling multi-area coordination, triage, routing, and resource management with mid-execution re-planning; implemented in ANML and PDDL 2.1; validated for feasibility and scalability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses the need for time-aware, resource-constrained coordination in simultaneous flood scenarios; supports dynamic adaptation and compatibility with diverse planners.

Method: Develops an intelligent flood-response framework modeled in ANML and PDDL 2.1; integrates priority-based triage, route accessibility, travel costs, resource allocation, and supply management; enables mid-execution re-planning; demonstrates across temporal planners.

Result: Experiments show the approach is feasible and scalable; temporal planning can model and solve flood-response problems; provides practical guidance for planner selection.

Conclusion: Temporal planning is viable for comprehensive flood response life-cycle modeling; the framework offers adaptability, planner interoperability, and actionable recommendations for real-world deployment.

Abstract: Effective response to multiple, simultaneously flooded areas requires coordinating appropriate actions in the correct temporal order, under severe resource constraints. Automated planning provides a foundation for addressing this challenge by generating time-aware schedules, given a formal description of available resources, constraints, and goals. This work presents an intelligent flood-response framework that exploits temporal planning and models the complete operational life cycle of flood response. The framework incorporates priority-driven triage, route accessibility and travel costs, resource allocation, and supply management, while also supporting mid-execution re-planning in response to unexpected environmental changes. The framework is formulated both in the Action Notation Modeling Language (ANML) and the Planning Domain Definition Language (PDDL) 2.1, facilitating compatibility with a wider range of temporal planners. Experimental results establish the feasibility and scalability of the proposed framework, showing that flood response scenarios can be effectively modeled and solved using temporal planning, while providing guidance on planner selection.

</details>


### [181] [FL-MAESTRO: Multi-Agent LLM Orchestration for Resource-Constrained Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20518)
*Jiajun Wu,Zirui Wang,Jiayu Zhou,Qiang Ye,Steve Drew*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FL-MAESTRO introduces a multi-agent LLM-based orchestrator for joint runtime decisions in Federated Learning: topology, per-client resource allocation, and aggregation rule, with a non-LLM feasibility check and a failure-list pruning mechanism to minimize wasted energy while preserving accuracy on non-IID CIFAR-10.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated Learning faces highly dynamic networks where client availability and resource constraints change each round. Existing work treats only one runtime dimension and lacks a unified approach to jointly optimize topology, allocation, and aggregation rules. Reducing wasted communication rounds and energy while maintaining accuracy is essential, especially with heterogeneous devices.

Method: Three specialist LLM agents are assigned to per-decision dimensions (topology, resource allocation, aggregation rule). A coordinator fuses their analyses into a single round decision. A non-LLM feasibility check validates the decision before execution. The server's predicted-failure list is used to withhold updates from clients unlikely to be aggregated. Client state is read as natural-text profiles to support device heterogeneity without per-class energy models.

Result: On non-IID CIFAR-10, FL-MAESTRO achieves accuracy comparable to the strongest energy-aware baseline while reducing wasted round energy from over one-third to near zero.

Conclusion: Joint runtime orchestration via specialized LLM agents, coordinated by a central aggregator and validated by a feasibility check, can substantially improve energy efficiency in FL without sacrificing accuracy, and extends naturally to heterogeneous devices.

Abstract: In Federated Learning (FL), the communication topology is a runtime variable rather than a fixed design choice, since links and edge devices drop in and out during training. Each round, the server must commit three coupled decisions, namely the communication topology, per-client resource allocation, and the aggregation rule for combining local updates. Recent agentic systems have begun bringing large language models (LLM) into FL, but the existing line of work either operates at setup time or handles a single runtime dimension such as client selection. We propose FL-MAESTRO, a multi-agent orchestrator that makes the joint runtime FL decision directly through three specialist LLM agents, one per decision dimension. A coordinator combines their analyses into a single decision, and a non-LLM feasibility check confirms it before the round executes. Because the orchestrator consumes the server's predicted-failure list, it withholds clients whose updates would never be aggregated, which removes the dominant source of wasted round energy in classical FL on volatile edge networks. Because client state is read as natural-text profiles, the same orchestrator extends to heterogeneous device classes without per-class energy models. On a non-IID CIFAR-10 benchmark, FL-MAESTRO matches the accuracy of the strongest energy-aware baseline while cutting wasted round energy from over a third to near zero. Code is available at https://github.com/denoslab/FL-MAESTRO.

</details>


### [182] [Volumetric Radiology AI in the Era of Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20549)
*Zanting Ye,Shengyuan Liu,Xin Liu,Chenhui Wang,Zhisong Wang,Jiashuai Liu,Zipei Wang,Cheng Wang,Wentao Pan,Mengjie Fang,Di Dong,Mohammad Salmanpour,Arman Rahmim,Yu Gu,Yong Xia,Hongming Shan,Yixuan Yuan,Yefeng Zheng,Lijun Lu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Survey of volumetric multimodal LLMs in radiology, focusing on preserving full-volume 3D context and acquisition-specific data, and on system-level agentic orchestration across workflows; introduces a Claim-Design-Validation framework to ensure claims, design, and validation align; highlights native volumetric modeling and evaluation needs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical radiology requires full-volume spatial context and quantitative acquisition-dependent information. Current MLLMs often rely on 2D views, compressed representations, or report-driven reasoning, which can miss critical 3D context and workflow integration.

Method: Literature review of over 200 publications through July 2026, organized by (i) volumetric representation and multimodal understanding at the model level, (ii) agentic orchestration at the system level, and (iii) links to clinical applications and evaluation. Covers volumetric foundation models, language alignment/compression, and agentive systems (planning, tools, memory, workflow interaction). Distinguishes when 2D views suffice versus native volumetric modeling and proposes a framework for evaluation.

Result: Synthesis emphasizes that native volumetric modeling and agentic capabilities must align with task, context, and workflow requirements. Clinical credibility hinges on faithful volumetric representation, traceable system behavior, and claim-aligned validation with human oversight. The paper proposes a Claim-Design-Validation framework to ensure that technical claims are supported by appropriate design and realistic validation.

Conclusion: Advancing radiology MLLMs requires prioritizing faithful 3D representations and integrated agentic systems that fit real-world workflows, alongside rigorous, workflow-aware validation and transparent oversight.

Abstract: Advances in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are extending radiological artificial intelligence (AI) beyond task-specific image analysis toward multimodal understanding and reasoning. Volumetric radiology, however, presents a fundamental representational mismatch: clinical interpretation often requires full-volume spatial context and acquisition-dependent quantitative information, whereas current MLLMs are commonly conditioned on selected two-dimensional (2D) images, compressed visual representations, or report-derived text. Reliable volumetric radiology AI therefore requires representations that preserve task-relevant three-dimensional (3D) information and systems that can access, verify, and integrate this information across clinical workflows. In this Review, we examine more than 200 publications through July 2026. We organize the literature around volumetric representation and multimodal understanding at the model level, agentic orchestration at the system level, and their links to clinical applications and evaluation. We review volumetric foundation models, language alignment and compression strategies, and agentic systems that extend MLLMs through planning, tools, memory, and workflow interaction. We distinguish settings in which selected 2D views or report-mediated reasoning may suffice from those that warrant native volumetric modeling. We also introduce a Claim-Design-Validation framework to assess whether technical, workflow, and clinical claims are matched by appropriate design and validation. Across the literature, native volumetric modeling and agentic capabilities depend on the spatial, quantitative, contextual, and workflow requirements of the intended task. Clinical credibility requires faithful volumetric representation, traceable system behavior, claim-aligned validation, and clearly defined human oversight in realistic workflows.

</details>


### [183] [Consilience: Conformally Calibrated Communication Control for Hidden-Profile Multi-Agent Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20564)
*Abhijith Babu,Ramneet Kaur,Vishal Pramanik,Olivera Kotevska,Nathaniel D. Bastian,Susmit Jha,Sunny Raj,Yanzhao Wu,Sumit Kumar Jha,Anirban Roy*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Consilience is an inference-time orchestration framework for multi-agent LLMs that uses a round-wise conformal calibration to guarantee one-step regret bounds for communication actions, improving accuracy and communication efficiency on hidden-profile tasks, sometimes matching full-information baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve decision quality in hidden-profile multi-agent settings by coordinating communication with formal guarantees.

Method: At each turn, summarize discussion into a compact state (uncertainty, disagreement, evidence gain, redundancy, premature consensus). Select a communication action (challenge, clarify, seek evidence, route) and an appropriate speaker. Apply a round-wise conformal calibration to provide distribution-free, finite-sample regret guarantees; enforcement via an acceptance mechanism that replaces inadmissible proposals with admissible ones.

Result: On HiddenBench-style hidden-profile tasks across 12 LMs, Consilience yields higher decision accuracy and more efficient communication than fixed/unstructured protocols; in some cases it matches or surpasses the full-information baseline where all evidence is observed.

Conclusion: Certified adaptive communication control can surpass simply increasing information availability, offering a practical, reliable framework for coordinating multi-agent LLMs.

Abstract: Multi-agent LLM systems can improve reasoning by pooling diverse perspectives, but their effectiveness depends on coordinating communication, particularly in hidden-profile settings where each agent holds only part of the evidence required for a correct decision. Existing protocols, including fixed schedules, round-robin exchange, and unstructured debate, provide no guarantee that a conversational action is appropriate. We propose Consilience, an inference-time orchestration framework that both steers and certifies multi-agent communication under distributed private information. At each turn, Consilience summarizes the discussion using a compact state capturing uncertainty, disagreement, evidence gain, redundancy, and premature consensus, then selects both a communication intervention (challenge, clarify, seek evidence, or route) and an appropriate speaker. Its central contribution is a round-wise conformal calibration procedure that provides a distribution-free, finite-sample guarantee: at each discussion round, conditional on reaching that round, the one-step regret of a controller's proposed action is bounded by a calibrated threshold with marginal probability at least 1 - alpha; an acceptance mechanism enforces the same guarantee for the executed action by replacing inadmissible proposals. On HiddenBench-style hidden-profile tasks spanning 12 open and closed weight language models, Consilience improves decision accuracy and communication efficiency over fixed and unstructured discussion protocols, sometimes surpassing a full-information baseline where every agent observes all evidence. These results demonstrate that certified adaptive communication control can be more valuable than increasing information availability, providing a practical mechanism for reliable multi-agent LLM coordination.

</details>


### [184] [Open-Weight Masked Introspection: Measuring What Language Models Can Report About Their Own Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20569)
*Emilio Ferrara*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Open-weight models do not reliably introspect their internal state changes; the OWMI framework finds no discriminative signal distinguishing real interventions from sham across eight models. Internal signals exist but do not reach a verbal report; the failure is in the path from state to report, not in the state itself.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Empirically test whether advanced language models can audit and report on their own internal changes, challenging or validating prior claims of self-auditing capabilities.

Method: Open-Weight Masked Introspection (OWMI) framework that intervenes on residual streams, attention heads, and sparse-autoencoder features; compares real interventions to sham (no change), impact-matched random perturbations, and a text-only observer; 78,000 measurements; evaluates with AUROC; trains a model to report interventions and uses a linear probe to recover intervention presence from activations; examines signals in words vs. confidence; tests last hidden layer before speech.

Result: No model’s report discriminates real intervention from sham beyond chance (AUROC ≈ 0.5007). An equivalence test bounds the effect to <0.15 percentage points of AUROC. Signals exist internally: a fine-tuned reporter can recover directions; a linear probe achieves 75–95.8% accuracy in detecting intervention from activations; the signal can be isolated in confidence rather than language in at least one model. The failure lies in the mapping from internal state to verbal report, suggesting oversight claims need internal references for validation.

Conclusion: Current open-weight models do not reliably introspect their internal changes; results may differ for future models, but baseline introspection claims require careful validation against internal references.

Abstract: Are frontier models able to introspect about their internal states? Recent work suggests that under certain conditions a complex enough model can audit its own internals, call out what changed, and report back confidently about it. We tested that claim on eight open-weight models from seven families and found no such ability: asked whether their own computation had been altered, none answered better than chance. To test it we built Open-Weight Masked Introspection (OWMI), a framework that intervenes on residual-stream sites, attention heads and sparse-autoencoder features, then interrogates the model about the change against the null conditions an answer has to beat: sham runs where nothing was altered, impact-matched random perturbations, and a text-only observer that sees only the visible output.
  Over 78,000 measurements, no model's report discriminates a real intervention from a sham beyond chance (AUROC ~0.5007), and an equivalence test bounds the effect below 0.15 percentage points of AUROC. Surprisingly, all the information needed is in the models. A model fine-tuned to report this class of intervention reaches near-perfect recovery on held-out directions, and a linear probe recovers intervention presence from the same activations at 75% to 95.8% accuracy, sharpening to no held-out error at the last layer before the model speaks. In one model the signal surfaces in the confidence rather than the words: its yes-or-no report never varies, while the confidence attached to it separates intervention from sham at AUROC 0.647. The failure sits in the path from internal state to verbal report, so oversight that reads a model's own testimony needs validating against an internal reference.
  While our results show the inability of current open-weight models to introspect, the debate is not settled for future models.

</details>


### [185] [FlavourBench: Ranking Frontier Language Models with Executable Culinary Ground Truth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20574)
*Josef Chen,Erim Hayretci*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FlavourBench is an automated, executable benchmark for open-ended language models using a dense, ground-truth culinary domain. It evaluates 27 endpoints across 534 tasks with 56 portfolio choices per task, plus robust statistical analyses and full data release.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To remove human-preference bottlenecks and brittle exact-match keys, enabling scalable, reproducible evaluation of open-ended models with dense ground-truth scoring that reflects model-driven decision tasks.

Method: A versioned culinary system provides dense ground truth. Each task presents eight ingredients and asks for a three-ingredient portfolio; before model execution, Epicure scores all 56 possible portfolios. The evaluation covers 27 frontier endpoints on a fixed 534-task core spanning substitution, pairing, and constrained composition. Each ranked model submits 89 valid responses per panel and family (14,418 model-task cells total). FlavourBench Score is the equal-family mean of the frozen task scores. Statistical support uses 50,000 anchor-cluster bootstrap replicates for 95% score bands and 100,000 sign-flip draws for all 351 paired model contrasts with Holm correction. Two independently compiled panels yield r=0.89 (rank rho=0.80).

Result: Grok 4.6 attains the largest point estimate at 65.1 (simultaneous 95% CI 61.0–69.2); 101 of 351 model pairs are resolved. The release includes prompts, all portfolio score maps, raw responses, exact routes, content hashes, and an offline verifier that reconstructs every result.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates a transparent, reproducible benchmark for open-ended LMs with automated scoring and full data release, validating automated evaluation and enabling robust cross-model comparisons on constrained, recipe-style tasks.

Abstract: Open-ended language-model benchmarks usually inherit a judge: a human preference panel, another model, or a brittle exact-match key. We introduce FlavourBench, an automated benchmark in which a versioned culinary system supplies dense, executable ground truth. Each task presents eight ingredients and asks for a three-ingredient portfolio; before model execution, Epicure scores all 56 possible portfolios. We evaluate 27 frontier endpoints on an identical 534-task core spanning substitution, pairing, and constrained composition. Every ranked model has exactly 89 valid responses per panel and family (14,418 model-task cells total), eliminating differential missingness from the leaderboard. The FlavourBench Score is the equal-family mean of the frozen task scores. We use 50,000 anchor-cluster bootstrap replicates for simultaneous 95% score bands and 100,000 sign-flip draws for all 351 paired model contrasts, with Holm control. The two independently compiled panels correlate at r = 0.89 (rank rho = 0.80). Grok 4.6 has the largest point estimate at 65.1 (simultaneous 95% CI 61.0-69.2); 101 of 351 model pairs are resolved. The release includes the prompts, all portfolio score maps, raw responses, exact routes, content hashes, and an offline verifier that reconstructs every result.

</details>


### [186] [ForeTime-VLA: Causal Future-Token Distillation from a World Action Model for Conveyor-Belt Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20735)
*Siyuan Ma,Yutian Zhang,Boshi Zhang,Qinglian Wu,Jiaqi Zhai,Dong Wei,Xiaojin Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ForeTime-VLA is a future-aware, causal VLA policy distilled from a frozen Fast-WAM teacher. It compresses offline current and future video latents into a 64-D target and uses an 8-frame history encoder plus future tokens to condition a VLM-based action policy, achieving better offline metrics (MAE, L2) and higher real-robot grasp success across belt speeds, with modest latency cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable dynamic manipulation with anticipatory contact awareness without expensive deployment-time imagined futures or video-scale teachers, by distilling a future-aware representation into a causal policy that runs efficiently at inference.

Method: Offline: compress current and predicted future video latents into a whitened 64-D target. Online: an eight-frame history encoder predicts the target, manipulation phase, and time-to-transition. Four future tokens and one phase token condition the VLM prefix; the predicted future and horizon condition the action expert. Training optimizes flow-matching action targets plus cosine, relational geometry, phase, time-to-transition, and action-equivalence objectives. Evaluation on deduplicated conveyor-belt dataset (40k-step checkpoints, 768 windows) and real-robot experiments.

Result: Offline test MAE decreased from 0.134119 to 0.130593 (2.63% improvement, 95% CI 0.82-4.48%). Test L2 decreased by 3.02% with ~2.46-2.93% latency cost. Real-robot: stationary grasp success 81.1%, slow-moving 58.9% (improvements of 12.2 and 22.2 percentage points over baselines). Belt-speed experiments: 44/90 grasps vs 23/90; at fast speed 11/30 vs 2/30. The results align offline orientation gains with reduced contact-pose failures, supporting causal future-token distillation.

Conclusion: Causal distillation of future tokens into a VLA policy yields tangible gains in both offline predictive accuracy and real-robot manipulation performance, enabling efficient dynamic manipulation without deploying the explicit world-model teacher.

Abstract: Manipulating moving objects requires a policy to anticipate contact events, yet vision-language-action (VLA) policies are commonly fine-tuned from the current observation alone. World action models (WAMs) learn predictive dynamics, but running a video-scale teacher or explicitly imagining future frames at deployment is costly. We introduce ForeTime-VLA, a dense pi0.5 policy that distills a future-aware, action-equivalent representation from a frozen Fast-WAM-derived teacher while remaining causal at inference. Offline, current and future video latents are compressed into a whitened 64-D target. Online, an eight-frame history encoder predicts this target together with manipulation phase and normalized time-to-transition. Four future tokens and one phase token condition the VLM prefix, while the predicted future and transition horizon condition the action expert. Training retains the original flow-matching action target and adds cosine, relational geometry, phase, time-to-transition, and action-equivalence objectives. On a deduplicated conveyor-belt dataset, we compare 40k-step checkpoints on 768 matched windows per split. Test MAE decreases from 0.134119 to 0.130593 (2.63%; paired-bootstrap 95% CI: 0.82-4.48% improvement), and test L2 decreases by 3.02%, at a 2.46-2.93% latency cost. In quantitative real-robot evaluation, ForeTime-VLA achieves 81.1% stationary and 58.9% slow-moving grasp success, exceeding the next-best reference by 12.2 and 22.2 percentage points, respectively. Across three belt speeds, it completes 44/90 grasps versus 23/90 for pi0.5, including 11/30 versus 2/30 at fast speed. The agreement between offline orientation gains and reduced real-robot contact-pose failures supports causal future-token distillation as an effective way to improve dynamic manipulation without deploying the world-model teacher.

</details>


### [187] [Difficulty-Aware Semantic-ID Optimization for Generative Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20611)
*Xin Yu,Stephen Li,Sina Aghaei,Zifan Zhu,Jiamu Bai,Guanjie Huang,Bo Peng,Yiyao Liu,Lingzhou Xue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DASO is a tree-aware post-training method for semantic-ID-based generative recommendation. It mitigates on-policy GRPO's target-missing in tree-structured SID tasks by online rollout allocation using SID-prefix rewards and an auxiliary SFT anchor, yielding substantial gains across public and internal benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vanilla GRPO under a frozen SFT struggles with the tree-structured Semantic-ID (SID) task: the exact target SID often does not appear among top candidates, causing weak reward signals and degenerate learning. A robust reward and allocation strategy is needed to align generation with the SID tree path.

Method: DASO profiles each rollout group by prefix-match depth, detects bottleneck SID levels where candidates diverge from the target path, and reallocates a bounded portion of the group to prefix-guided completions while keeping raw rollouts for contrast. It introduces a SID-prefix reward that provides graded credit and uses an auxiliary SFT anchor to prevent regression on solved examples.

Result: On public benchmarks, DASO outperforms MiniOneRec-style GRPO on 11 of 12 metrics and achieves the best result on 9 of 12 metrics; it also improves most level-wise recall metrics on an internal recommendation task.

Conclusion: DASO effectively mitigates the target-missing failure mode in tree-structured, on-policy GRPO for semantic-ID-based generation, delivering consistent performance gains through dynamic rollout allocation and prefix-guided rewards.

Abstract: Semantic-ID-based generative recommendation casts retrieval and ranking as autoregressive generation over hierarchical item identifiers. A common recipe is SFT followed by GRPO, yet vanilla GRPO is poorly matched to this tree-structured task. Under the frozen SFT checkpoint, the exact target is absent from the first 16 candidates of the 50-beam constrained ranking for many prompts, and in harder cases none of these candidates enters the target SID branch. This prompt-level diagnostic motivates a training concern: when on-policy GRPO groups are similarly target-missing, item-level rewards may produce weak or degenerate reward variation even if some candidates follow part of the target path. We propose Difficulty-Aware Semantic-ID Optimization (DASO), a tree-aware post-training method that addresses this failure mode as an online rollout-allocation problem. Instead of using fixed difficulty buckets or uniformly injecting ground-truth completions, DASO profiles each current rollout group by prefix-match depth, locates the bottleneck SID levels where candidates leave the target path, and reallocates a bounded portion of the group to prefix-guided completions while retaining raw rollouts for contrast. A SID-prefix reward provides graded credit, while an auxiliary SFT anchor mitigates regression on examples already solved by the SFT checkpoint. On the public benchmarks, DASO improves over MiniOneRec-style GRPO on 11 of 12 metrics and achieves the best result on 9 of 12 metrics; it also improves most level-wise recall metrics on the internal recommendation task.

</details>


### [188] [Evaluating Skills, Not Just Agents: Agentic Continuous Evaluation of Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20614)
*Christopher Kevin,Narendran Raghavan,Jean-Francois Puget,Roshni Malani,Meghana Puvvadi,Moshe Abramovitch,Mohit Gupta,Rama Akkiraju,Subodh Prabhu,Yogesh Dangi,Wei Luo,Seong Hee Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ACES: a repository-native framework for evaluating enterprise agent skills via live trials, producing Skill Lift metrics, ATIF trajectory formatting, and comparator across product-owned task suites; open-source as NVIDIA SkillEvaluator.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gatekeeping for enterprise agent capabilities relies on static structure/quality checks; there is a need to evaluate whether capability packages actually improve live-agent task performance under the same constraints.

Method: Paired live trials with/without a target skill; normalize to ATIF; score six runtime metrics; compute Skill Lift (value added by skill for a fixed task, harness, workspace, scorer); supports product-owned task suites comparing baseline, skill, bundle, team-skill, plugin; evaluation across 145 real skills (scan-only gates) and 947 paired cases across 58 of 64 production skills and four harnesses.

Result: Scan-only gates identify authoring issues but correlate weakly with live performance (Spearman ρ=0.14). Composite Skill Lift 0.2134 (95% CI [0.1967,0.2301]); outcome-only lift 0.1799; composite lift positive in 72.8% of cases; strongest gains in skill execution, behavior check, and skill efficiency; open-source NVIDIA SkillEvaluator.

Conclusion: The methodology provides measurable, positive improvements where live evaluation reveals benefits not captured by gates, enabling enterprise-grade evaluation of capability packages and facilitating adoption through an open-source tool.

Abstract: Enterprise agent programs are moving from prototypes into production, where reusable skills, tools, and workflow packages must be reviewed with evidence rather than prose. Current gates often scan these artifacts for structure, style, and security, but they do not answer the deployment question: does the capability package help a live agent complete enterprise tasks under the same model, sandbox, and grading policy?
  We present ACES (Agentic Continuous Evaluation of Skills), a repository-native framework for evaluating skills and product capability packages as executable agent artifacts. ACES runs paired live trials with and without a target skill, normalizes trajectories into the Agent Trajectory Interchange Format (ATIF), grades six default runtime metrics, and reports Skill Lift: the target skill's added value for a fixed task, harness, workspace, and scorer. The same protocol supports product-owned task suites that compare baseline, skill, bundle, team-skill, and plugin targets.
  On 145 real skills from internal enterprise repositories and public catalogs, scan-only gates surface useful authoring issues but measure complementary facets (structural versus LLM-judge Spearman $ρ= 0.14$). Across 947 scored paired cases from 58 of 64 production skills and four primary harnesses, mean composite Skill Lift is 0.2134 (95\% paired-case CI [0.1967, 0.2301]); mean outcome-only lift, the average of accuracy and goal accuracy, is 0.1799. Composite lift is positive in 72.8\% of paired cases. The largest process-metric gains appear in skill execution, behavior check, and skill efficiency---signals about discovery, routing, workflow following, and tool use that document scans cannot observe. An open-source implementation of the methodology is available in NVIDIA SkillEvaluator.

</details>


### [189] [Dual-Cache Latent Space Communication between Heterogeneous Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20617)
*Jiyao Liu,Qi Zhang,Yaoyi Jia,Ziwen Kan,Song Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: XKV improves cross-agent knowledge transfer by learned-query attention pooling of sharer and receiver caches, enabling heterogeneous models to share context efficiently; it outperforms LCF-X and text-based schemes in macro score, speed, and parameter efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the limitations of text-based and shared-context approaches (C2C, LCF-X) that require identical inputs, layer alignment, or joint memory; aims to fuse caches across heterogeneous models with minimal training.

Method: XKV uses learned-query attention to pool both the sharer's and receiver's KV caches; applies self-attention over receiver-aligned layer tokens; employs a learned layer map to reconcile depth differences; combines these into a compact joint memory; uses a shared position decoder so every receiver cache position can retrieve its own per-head-gated residual in the receiver's KV geometry; all models remain frozen with only the translator trained.

Result: XKV achieves the highest macro score across 45 dataset-model-pair settings, with the best average rank. It improves on LCF-X by 4.6 exact-match and 4.2 F1 on ROPES and surpasses text-based communication on four of five datasets. It uses 76% fewer parameters and translates a cache pair 10.3x faster (5.8 ms vs. 59.9 ms). End-to-end, XKV is 26% faster than LCF-X and 6.8x faster than text communication.

Conclusion: XKV demonstrates robust, efficient cross-model KV cache translation for heterogeneous multi-agent LLM systems, enabling faster, leaner communication while maintaining or improving task performance, and relaxing prior constraints on depth, KV geometry, and shared contexts.

Abstract: Multi-agent LLM systems split work across models, so answering often requires knowledge that sits in another agent's context: a Sharer has encoded information that a Receiver needs to complete its task. They usually communicate by exchanging text, which puts autoregressive decoding on the critical path and reduces the exchange to a discrete message written without sight of the receiver's state. Recent latent protocols instead translate the sharer's key-value (KV) cache into the receiver's: C2C supports heterogeneous models but requires both to read the same input, while LCF-X removes this shared-context requirement through position-free sharer-cache pooling. Three restrictions remain: LCF-X compresses the sharer alone, supplies the same layer-local summary to every receiver position with no joint cross-layer memory to retrieve from, and assumes matched layer count and KV geometry. We introduce XKV, which lifts all three: learned-query attention pools both caches; self-attention over receiver-aligned layer tokens, with a learned layer map reconciling different depths, mixes the pooled summaries into a compact joint memory; and a shared position decoder lets every raw receiver cache position retrieve its own per-head-gated residual in the receiver's native KV geometry. Both models stay frozen and may differ in family, depth, KV-head count, head dimension, and tokenizer; only the translator is trained. Across 45 dataset-model-pair settings (six heterogeneous and three same-model ordered pairings, five datasets), XKV attains the highest macro score and best average rank, improving on LCF-X on every dataset (by 4.6 exact-match and 4.2 F1 points on ROPES) and surpassing text communication on four of the five, while training 76% fewer parameters and translating a cache pair 10.3x faster (5.8 vs. 59.9 ms); end to end, XKV is 26% faster than LCF-X and 6.8x faster than text communication.

</details>


### [190] [Applying Anthropic Primitives at Large Enterprises: Harness Paradigm for Knowledge Work](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20622)
*George Juraj Salapa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a governance-friendly, enterprise-ready harness architecture for coding agents, using an unchanged harness backed by four mechanisms (credential-scoped tooling, external authorization logic, and registration-as-side-effect) implemented on microcc to enable auditable, reusable deployments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the continued costs of reviewing and maintaining code created by coding agents, and to overcome governance limitations of centralised/off-the-shelf or bespoke graph-orchestration approaches.

Method: Introduce a back-bone harness that remains unmodified across deployments; implement four governance-oriented mechanisms to scope and audit actions without altering the harness; build as a reference using microcc.

Result: Abstract outlines a concrete architectural proposal with four mechanisms but does not report empirical results; claims improved auditability, reusability, and governance alignment for enterprise deployments of coding agents.

Conclusion: A unified, auditable harness architecture can close the governance gap in enterprise adoption of coding agents, by keeping code identical and managing permissions, tooling, and registration externally.

Abstract: Frontier models have collapsed the cost of writing custom code: a niche problem a specialist sees in their own domain now costs an afternoon. The cost of reviewing and maintaining that code hasn't collapsed. Each solution drifts from the next; understanding one means reading its codebase from scratch. Large enterprises build something centrally governed instead: at worst an off-the-shelf product, at best a graph-orchestration framework wired bespoke per use case, or a low-code platform used as the orchestrator. These are custom every time and limited in scope. Enterprises don't weigh a third option that escapes both constraints: the harness paradigm.
  Recent work treats the coding-agent harness as enterprise infrastructure rather than a coding tool, converging on three findings: harnesses suffice at the task level and outperform more elaborate architectures on enterprise work (arXiv:2604.00073, arXiv:2604.13107); harness choice accounts for most of the variance in agent benchmark results, more than model choice does (arXiv:2605.23950); and the gap between that finding and enterprise adoption is governance (arXiv:2605.10223, arXiv:2605.18747).
  We propose an architecture that closes that gap. One harness runs unmodified as the backbone; the code stays identical across every deployment, so reviewing what gets built collapses to reading its instructions file. Section 4 gives four mechanisms: credential-scoped tooling, where each backend gets one generic request tool and a scoped credential instead of a hand-built method; authorization logic outside the harness, so one artifact runs as a cron backbone, a chat-surface engine, and a terminal tool; registration is a side effect of pushing code, collapsing an audit a review of a text file.
  Built on microcc (<https://pypi.org/project/micro-cc/>), our reference harness.

</details>


### [191] [Graph-Operator World Models for Morphology-Parameter Generalization in Continuous Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20936)
*Xu Yang,Yiqin Yang,Qianchuan Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GraphOp-WM is a structured world model that generalizes across unseen morphology in articulated robots by factorizing transitions into a morphology-independent local dynamics basis and a morphology-conditioned operator that combines node-local modulation, kinematic-tree coupling, and a low-rank global correction. It uses an attributed graph representation, architecture separation, basis normalization, and paired-morphology supervision to confine morphology dependence to the operator pathway, enabling graph-level readout and edge-wise action representations for reward, value, and TD-MPC-style planning. The authors define controlled MuJoCo parameter splits (interpolation, extrapolation, held-out compositions) across Hopper, Walker2d, and HalfCheetah.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed-morphology world models degrade when key physical parameters (link lengths, masses, damping, actuation) change; there is a need to specify which learned transition components should stay reusable versus adapt to morphology.

Method: Represent bodies and their kinematic relations as an attributed graph and factorize transitions into a morphology-independent local dynamics basis and a morphology-conditioned structured operator. The operator combines node-local modulation, kinematic-tree coupling, and a low-rank global correction. The approach enforces static morphology dependence to be carried by the operator pathway via architectural separation, basis normalization, and paired-morphology supervision. It uses graph-level readout and edge-wise action representations to interface with reward/value/TD-MPC planning. The work also defines controlled MuJoCo parameter splits covering interpolation, extrapolation, and held-out compositions of link geometry, mass, damping, and actuation for Hopper, Walker2d, and HalfCheetah.

Result: The paper claims generalization across unseen morphology parameters within related articulated robot families and supports planning via a compatible graph-based interface. It defines and evaluates controlled MuJoCo parameter splits (interpolation, extrapolation, held-out) on Hopper, Walker2d, and HalfCheetah to demonstrate the expected generalization behavior.

Conclusion: GraphOp-WM offers a morphology-aware, structured world model that separates morphology dependence to a dedicated operator pathway, enabling reuse of local dynamics across morphologies and enabling planning-compatible interfaces for reward, value, and TD-MPC-style methods.

Abstract: World models for continuous control are commonly trained for a fixed physical system and can degrade when known morphology parameters such as link lengths, masses, damping, and actuation change. Existing approaches often provide these parameters as conditioning information, but leave unspecified which part of the learned transition should remain reusable and which part should change with morphology. We propose Graph-Operator World Models (GraphOp-WM), a structured world model for generalization across unseen morphology parameters within related articulated robot families. GraphOp-WM represents bodies and their kinematic relations as an attributed graph and factorizes each transition into a morphology-independent local dynamics basis and a morphology-conditioned structured operator. The operator combines node-local modulation, kinematic-tree coupling, and a low-rank global correction, while architectural information separation, basis normalization, and paired-morphology supervision encourage static morphology dependence to be carried by the operator pathway. Graph-level readout and edge-wise action representations provide a compatible interface for reward, value, and TD-MPC-style planning. We further define controlled MuJoCo parameter splits covering interpolation, extrapolation, and held-out compositions of link geometry, mass, damping, and actuation parameters in Hopper, Walker2d, and HalfCheetah.

</details>


### [192] [SAGE: A Unified Algebra and Self-Adaptive Execution for AI Functions in SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20630)
*Xiangqi Wang,Nhan H. Pham,Oktie Hassanzadeh,Dharmashankar Subramanian,Xiangliang Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Unifies AI functions in SQL into three primitives (AI_SCALAR, AI_AGG, AI_JOIN) under SAGE, with confidence-gated execution; AI_JOIN uses analysis, decomposition, recipe cards and probes to select strategies; achieves strong performance gains (SemBench, 358x cost reduction).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI-enabled SQL functions are diverse but largely serve three relational roles; a unified framework is needed to improve execution quality and efficiency across scalar, aggregate, and join workloads.

Method: Introduce SAGE with three typed primitives AI_SCALAR, AI_AGG, AI_JOIN; integrate with standard relational operators; implement a confidence-gated interface; AI_JOIN uses predicate analysis, decomposition of compound conditions, recipe-card guidance, and a label-free probe to pick among complete execution strategies; apply physics-based strategies tailored to the relational shape.

Result: Extensive evaluation across public AI operators and workloads shows consistent improvements in execution quality and efficiency; achieves strongest SemBench performance; for a representative factorable join, reduces pairwise model calls by >100x and yields a 358x cost reduction.

Conclusion: A unified, efficient framework for AI in SQL is feasible and beneficial, with SAGE delivering strong performance and significant cost savings across scalar, aggregate, and join AI tasks.

Abstract: SQL systems increasingly expose AI functions for tasks such as classification, extraction, filtering, ranking, retrieval, joining, and summarization. Despite their diverse APIs, these functions play only three relational roles: transforming individual rows, aggregating groups, or generating relationships between row pairs. We present SAGE (Self-Adaptive Generative Execution), a unified logical and physical framework that captures these roles with three typed primitives, AI_SCALAR, AI_AGG, and AI_JOIN, and composes them naturally with standard relational operators. All primitives share a confidence-gated execution interface while supporting physical strategies tailored to their relational shape. The main challenge is AI_JOIN, where SAGE analyzes the predicate, decomposes compound conditions when possible, and uses a recipe card together with a small label-free probe to select among complete execution strategies. Across a broad audit of public AI operators and evaluations spanning scalar, aggregate, and join workloads, this formulation covers common AI functionality while consistently improving execution quality and efficiency. SAGE achieves the strongest overall SemBench performance and, on a representative factorable join, reduces pairwise model calls by more than two orders of magnitude, yielding a 358-fold measured cost reduction.

</details>


### [193] [Weighted Memory Tree: Remembering What Matters for Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20631)
*Quang Dao,Purvi Kathalkar,Kenneth Eaton*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces a hierarchical memory system (WMT) with dynamic retention scores to manage long-horizon LLM reasoning, improving accuracy and reducing prompt tokens while resisting memory poisoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long execution histories increase inference costs and risk outdated or misleading information; a mechanism is needed to select which memories stay active.

Method: Proposes a hierarchical memory architecture organizing execution into tasks, subtasks, and actions, with dynamic retention scores updated via event-based updates and selection-based decay. Evaluated on GAIA-Text using Qwen3-8B, Gemma 4 E4B, and Llama-3.1-8B, with ablations and memory-poisoning experiments.

Result: Compared to linear memory, WMT yields an average +9.97 percentage points in accuracy and a -32.8% reduction in prompt-token usage; memory-poisoning tests show reduced persistence of unreliable information; ablations quantify contributions of memory structure and decay mechanisms.

Conclusion: Effective long-horizon agent memory relies more on selective activation than on storing more information; WMT preserves useful context, folds completed trajectories, and suppresses low-utility data.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents have demonstrated the ability to solve multi-step tasks requiring planning, tool use, and external information access, yet growing execution histories increase inference cost and expose reasoning to outdated, irrelevant, or misleading information, potentially degrading reasoning quality. Existing memory approaches organize or compress execution histories but provide limited mechanisms for deciding which memories remain active. We introduce the, a hierarchical memory system that organizes execution into tasks, subtasks, and actions while assigning each memory a dynamic retention score. Event-based updates and selection-based decay revise these scores, allowing WMT to preserve useful information, fold completed trajectories, suppress low-utility content, and retain access to folded context. We evaluate WMT on GAIA-Text using Qwen3-8B, Gemma 4 E4B, and Llama-3.1-8B, with ablations and memory-poisoning experiments. Relative to linear memory, WMT improves accuracy by an average of 9.97 percentage points while reducing prompt-token usage by 32.8%. Memory-poisoning experiments show that WMT limits the persistence and propagation of unreliable information. Our results suggest that effective long-horizon agent memory depends less on storing more information than on deciding which information should remain active.

</details>


### [194] [Unified Branch-and-Bound Search for the Steiner Traveling Salesman Problem on Graphs of Convex Sets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21319)
*Jingtao Tang,Hang Ma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Formalizes Steiner-TSP on Graphs of Convex Sets (GCS) and proposes a unified branch-and-bound framework over rooted walk prefixes with additive lower bounds and a cut-separated connected-flow relaxation. Proves finite termination and ε-optimality certificates under uniform positive costs for best-first and depth-first traversals. Demonstrates joint sensing-mode, visitation-order, and continuous-trajectory planning for a mobile-manipulator with LTL_f constraints, achieving feasible solutions within 30s and mean certified gaps around 28–30%, outperforming two baselines which succeed on roughly half the instances.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the combinatorial explosion and infinite solution space of Steiner-TSP on graphs of convex sets by developing a provably convergent search framework with practical performance guarantees and applicability to integrated sensing and trajectory planning under temporal logic constraints.

Method: 1) Unified branch-and-bound search over rooted walk prefixes. 2) Additive lower bounds from graph costs bound committed prefixes. 3) Cut-separated connected-flow relaxation to bound remaining targets and return to root. 4) Analyze termination properties under uniform positive-cost assumption; establish ε-optimality certificates for either strategy. 5) Apply to joint sensing-mode, visitation-order, and continuous-trajectory selection for mobile-manipulator inspection, with action precedences expressed in LTL_f. 6) Empirical evaluation against baselines on benchmark instances.

Result: Best-first traversal terminates after finitely many expansions even without an initial incumbent; DFS terminates once a finite incumbent exists. Global lower bound guarantees that either strategy’s incumbent cost is within ε of the global optimum. On benchmarks, both strategies find feasible solutions within 30 seconds with mean certified optimality gaps of 28.1% (best-first) and 29.7% (DFS). Two recent baselines succeed on only about half of the instances.

Conclusion: The proposed unified B&B framework for Steiner-TSP on GCS provides provable termination and ε-optimality certificates while delivering competitive, practical performance. It supports integrated sensing-mode, visitation-order, and continuous-trajectory planning under LTL_f constraints for mobile-manipulator tasks.

Abstract: We formalize the Steiner Traveling Salesman Problem (Steiner-TSP) on Graphs of Convex Sets (GCS), which seeks a minimum-cost closed trajectory through required convex sets while allowing optional transit vertices and revisits. To explore the resulting infinite solution space, we propose a unified branch-and-bound search over rooted walk prefixes. Additive lower-bound-graph costs bound committed prefixes, while a cut-separated connected-flow relaxation lower-bounds the residual cost of visiting every remaining target and returning to the root. Under a uniform positive-cost assumption, best-first traversal terminates after finitely many expansions on every feasible instance without an initial incumbent, whereas depth-first traversal does so once a finite incumbent is available. For a user-specified factor $ε\geq1$, a global lower bound certifies that either strategy's incumbent cost is at most $ε$ times the global optimum. We further demonstrate joint sensing-mode, visitation-order, and continuous-trajectory selection for a mobile-manipulator inspection task, including action precedences expressed in linear temporal logic over finite traces (LTL$_f$). Both traversal strategies find feasible solutions on all benchmark instances within 30s with mean certified optimality gaps of 28.1% and 29.7%, respectively, whereas two recent baselines succeed on only about half of the instances

</details>


### [195] [Beyond Effectiveness: A Multi-Criteria Framework for Comparing Practical Socio-Technical Interventions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20649)
*Catherine King,Lynnette Hui Xian Ng,Kathleen M. Carley*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multi-criteria framework for evaluating sociotechnical interventions is proposed and instantiated in misinformation; expert judgments show a trade-off between effectiveness, public acceptance, and feasibility, with no single best intervention across criteria; a practitioner-oriented decision framework is offered for broader sociotechnical contexts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish a principled basis for comparing diverse sociotechnical interventions across multiple criteria, addressing gaps where interventions are evaluated mainly by effectiveness or considered in isolation from cost, effort, and feasibility.

Method: Developed a five-criteria evaluation framework and applied it to misinformation by surveying 39 researchers about 40 interventions across five criteria: political feasibility, effectiveness, user acceptance, cost, and implementation effort.

Result: A mismatch is observed: interventions ranked as most effective are not consistently the most acceptable or feasible; trade-offs among criteria are evident; results inform the design of interventions beyond misinformation.

Conclusion: Provide a practitioner-oriented decision framework to navigate trade-offs in sociotechnical interventions and discuss implications for broader domains beyond misinformation.

Abstract: Designers and policymakers in sociotechnical domains like content moderation, privacy interfaces, recommender systems and beyond, must choose among a growing menu of proposed interventions, but typically lack a principled basis for comparing them.
