Prior work tends to evaluate interventions individually and mostly along the effectiveness criteria, while implementation constraints such as cost, effort and feasibility are often considered separately. We present a multi-criteria framework for evaluating sociotechnical interventions. This framework is instantiated through the case of misinformation, a domain of intense focus for proposed countermeasures. We survey $N=39$ researchers on 40 operationalized interventions across five evaluative criteria: political feasibility, effectiveness, user acceptance, cost, and implementation effort. We find that the interventions that experts judge to be the most effective are not always the most acceptable to the public or the most feasible to implement. We also discuss how this tension has implications for the design of sociotechnical interventions beyond misinformation, and offer a decision framework for practitioners navigating the trade-offs of sociotechnical interventions.

</details>


### [196] [Anatomy-Informed Neural Networks: Encoding Anatomic Priors in Loss and Architecture, with an SE(3) Formulation of Guidewire-Induced Aortoiliac Deformation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21332)
*David P. Stonko*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes Anatomy-Informed Neural Networks (AINN) that combine soft anatomic priors in loss with hard priors embedded in architecture to enforce anatomical validity, demonstrated in a physics-based vessel-wire system via SE(3) kinematics and constrained optimization; no neural network training in this work; future work to apply to CT data for endovascular navigation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the gap where deep-learning anatomy models are plausible numerically but anatomically invalid, especially with scarce data; leverage explicit anatomical priors to improve plausibility, generalization, and safety for autonomous endovascular procedures.

Method: Integrate soft priors as penalties in a loss function and hard priors by architectural/state representation; model vessel centerline and wire path as SE(3) curves; couple a Cosserat-rod wire to a tortuosity-modulated vessel via a unilateral lumen-contact constraint; predict via a constrained minimizer of the coupled elastic energy; use Lagrange multipliers for contact forces; supervise with a Wasserstein-2 OT loss between projected predictions and 2D angiograms; validate kinematics, loss, and projection against ground truth; mechanics solver checked against optimality, not mesh-converged; no network training performed.

Result: Framework demonstrates a consistent, geometry-constrained prediction scheme and validates kinematics and projection against ground truth; shows how a physics-based solver can serve as the predictor within the AINN paradigm; no data-driven neural network training is performed in this work; remains to transfer to real CT data and test predictive accuracy with limited data.

Conclusion: AINN offers a viable path to anatomically faithful predictions by fusing soft and hard priors with a physics-based, architecture-enforced representation; promising for data-scarce scenarios in endovascular navigation, pending transfer to patient CTs and eventual 3D training with 2D angiogram supervision.

Abstract: Deep-learning models of anatomy can be numerically plausible yet anatomically impossible, and they generalize poorly when data are scarce. We introduce Anatomy-Informed Neural Networks (AINN), in which soft anatomic priors enter as penalty terms in the loss (e.g., a branching penalty that treats a renal transplant artery off the iliac instead of the aorta as unexpected rather than impossible), in direct analogy to a physics-informed neural network, and hard anatomic priors (e.g., continuity of the vessel) are built into the architecture and state representation, making such invalid predictions impossible by construction wherever the prior admits architectural enforcement.
  We develop it on a clinical test case with limited data: how the aortoiliac tree deforms when a stiff wire is introduced endoluminally. This is important to contemporary aortic surgery and will matter to autonomous endovascular navigation. We lift the vessel centerline and the wire path from R^3 to curves of frames in the Lie group SE(3), and couple a Cosserat-rod wire to a tortuosity-modulated, anatomically anchored vessel through a unilateral lumen-contact inequality. The prediction is a constrained minimizer of the coupled elastic energy, with contact forces as its Lagrange multipliers. Supervision is a Wasserstein-2 optimal-transport loss between the predicted projection through the C-arm geometry and the observed angiogram, so a 2D angiogram can train a 3D prediction. The kinematics, loss and projection are verified against known ground truth; the mechanics solver only against its own optimality conditions, and predicted displacement is not yet mesh-converged. Here, no network is trained. Future work will transfer this in silico model to real CT scans and test whether it improves predictive accuracy and reduces the training data required.

</details>


### [197] [Auditable by Construction: An Ontology-Driven Framework for Trustworthy LLM Analytics in Enterprise Finance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20661)
*Sergiy Lunyakin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: KDAF is an ontology-driven retrieval-augmented framework for enterprise finance that prioritizes auditability over accuracy, using Context-Aware Relevance Propagation to provide provenance; evaluation shows retrieval improves correctness over zero-context, but accuracy among retrieval methods is similar; auditability becomes the key value proposition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In regulated FP&A workflows, trust and traceability to authoritative sources are required; retrieval-augmented generation must be auditable after the fact, not just accurate.

Method: Six iterative stages to build ontology-driven knowledge systems; Context-Aware Relevance Propagation (CARP) retrieves evidence with relationship type, confidence, and source lineage; comparative evaluation on FinanceBench against zero-context inference, BM25, concept-weighted lexical retrieval, and ungrounded graph traversal.

Result: Retrieval is necessary (zero-context: 4.1% vs retrieval: 10-12%). On answer correctness, KDAF is statistically indistinguishable from BM25 (-0.007, CI [-0.021, 0.000]). On auditability, KDAF achieves the highest citation traceability F1 (0.515) and better provenance; graph-structured retrieval avoids outside-question evidence (0 of 426 items) vs 16.8% and 20.2% for lexical baselines.

Conclusion: Auditability, not accuracy, is the primary axis for enterprise ontology-grounded retrieval; KDAF's value lies in complete provenance and traceability of retrieved facts.

Abstract: Enterprise adoption of large language models in finance is constrained less by fluency than by trust: in Financial Planning and Analysis (FP&A) and other regulated workflows, an answer is usable only if it is traceable to authoritative sources and auditable after the fact. This paper argues that retrieval-augmented generation for enterprise finance should be evaluated on auditability alongside accuracy, and presents the Knowledge-Driven Analytics Framework (KDAF), which builds ontology-driven knowledge systems through six iterative stages and retrieves evidence via Context-Aware Relevance Propagation (CARP), so that every retrieved fact carries its relationship type, confidence, and source lineage.
  An evaluation on FinanceBench (145 questions) compares KDAF against zero-context inference, BM25, concept-weighted lexical retrieval, and ungrounded graph traversal. First, retrieval is necessary: zero-context inference reaches 4.1% correctness against 10-12% for retrieval-augmented conditions. Second, on answer correctness the retrieval conditions are statistically indistinguishable (KDAF vs BM25: -0.007, 95% CI [-0.021, 0.000]), so accuracy alone does not justify structured retrieval here -- a negative result we report explicitly. Third, on auditability the ordering reverses: KDAF attains the highest citation traceability F1 (0.515), exceeding ungrounded traversal by +0.027 (CI [0.006, 0.050]) and BM25 by +0.052 (CI [0.024, 0.083]), intervals excluding zero. Graph-structured retrieval also admits no evidence from outside the question subject entity (0 of 426 items, against 16.8% and 20.2% for lexical baselines), and every selected item resolves to a complete provenance chain. We argue that auditability, not accuracy, is the axis on which ontology-grounded retrieval earns its cost.

</details>


### [198] [DreamBench-SWE: A Multi-Session Memory-Hygiene Benchmark for Software Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20664)
*Sarthak Singh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DreamBench-SWE is a multi-session benchmark evaluating software-agent memory hygiene across conditions, with preregistered and original folds. Results show limited success for several memory configurations and no clear external-memory superiority; the audit discriminates a specific hosted-memory configuration but does not establish external-system mechanisms or broad generalizability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether software agents rely on external/internal memory across sessions using executable profiles and hidden/oracles; validate a preregistered audit design and compare against an original fold to judge discriminability and generality of memory-bearing configurations.

Method: Two experimental paths: the original v2 fold and preregistered v2.1 successor audit, each with multiple memory conditions and four experimental conditions. The successor involved 360/360 work units and 720/720 S3 cells. Memory types tested included external memory, deterministic verbatim event memory, typed-plus-raw reference probe, and a pinned hosted Mem0 literal-storage configuration. Statistical analyses included Holm corrections for multiple comparisons, with several contrasts preregistered and then evaluated for conformance and availability.

Result: Original fold: primary DF-hybrid--B5 contrast null (p=.518 after clustering; Holm p=1); no evidence of equivalence; C9/C10 showed B0-headroom limitations. Successor: external memory 21/180 passes (rate 0.1167); verbatim memory 82/180 (0.4556); reference probe 83/180 (0.4611); literal-storage 97/180 (0.5389). Six-slot Family A retained unavailable slots; all comparisons against no memory rejected after Holm correction. preregistered mechanism contrasts unavailable post conformance rejection. Literal-storage vs verbatim nonconfirmatory; reference probe comparison not rejected. Overall: supports DreamBench-SWE as discriminating executable profile benchmark and characterizes one hosted-memory configuration, but does not establish external-system mechanism, superiority among memory-bearing conditions, equivalence, or broad product generality. Original v2.0.5 findings unchanged.

Conclusion: The audit demonstrates discriminative potential of DreamBench-SWE for profiling executable memory usage but highlights substantial uncertainty regarding external-memory mechanisms and generalizability. Further preregistered, conformance-approved studies are needed to establish firm mechanisms, equivalence, and broader applicability.

Abstract: DreamBench-SWE is a multi-session benchmark for software-agent memory hygiene in which later software tasks depend on non-inferable evidence from earlier sessions and are scored by executable hidden oracles. We report the original scaled v2 fold and a separately preregistered v2.1 successor audit designed after that study but frozen before successor outcome inspection. The successor run completed 360/360 work units and 720/720 S3 cells across four conditions. In the original fold, the primary DF-hybrid--B5 contrast was null (95/180 versus 89/180; clustered p=.518, Holm p=1), not evidence of equivalence, and C9/C10 retained B0-headroom limitations. In the successor, no external memory achieved 21/180 passes (rate 0.1167), deterministic verbatim event memory 82/180 (rate 0.4556), the typed-plus-raw reference probe 83/180 (rate 0.4611), and one pinned hosted Mem0 literal-storage configuration 97/180 (rate 0.5389). The registered six-slot Family A retained unavailable slots at p=1; all three available comparisons against no memory rejected after Holm correction. Both preregistered mechanism contrasts were unavailable after pre-evaluation conformance rejection. The secondary literal-storage-versus-verbatim comparison was nonconfirmatory and sensitivity-dependent, while the comparison with the reference probe did not reject. The audit therefore supports DreamBench-SWE as a discriminating executable profile benchmark and characterizes one exact hosted-memory configuration, but it does not establish an external-system mechanism, superiority among memory-bearing conditions, equivalence, or broad product generality. The original v2.0.5 findings and artifacts remain unchanged.

</details>


### [199] [Why2Speak: Faithful Reasoning for Abstaining Action Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20670)
*Shreya Mendi,Brinnae Bent*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A study on intervention timing in multi-party chat using Qwen3-8B compares direct decision policies, reasoning policies, supervised fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning. It reveals a capability-auditability tradeoff: the strongest direct policy performs best but offers no reasoning trace, while the reasoning policy provides a trace at a performance cost. Supervised fine-tuning and RL fail to improve decision quality or reasoning. Mechanisms show group-relative objectives lack signals when all rollouts pick the same action; faithfulness metrics can misstate alignment; various evaluative probes bias results. The work presents controls for evaluating reasoning-based oversight where agents can act or abstain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable faithful oversight of agentic systems by explanations, while recognizing that exposing reasoning may alter the policy itself. The work investigates how explanation strategies interact with decision quality under class imbalance, asymmetric costs, and potential leakage of reasoning.

Method: Experiment with Qwen3-8B, decoding with or without chain-of-thought. Compare four learning strategies for an intervention-timing task in multi-party conversation: (1) direct decision policies, (2) reasoning policies, (3) supervised fine-tuning, and (4) reinforcement learning. Use controlled activation probes and behavioral ablations to diagnose faithfulness and inference-mode confounds; analyze issues like group-relative objectives and probe susceptibility to class imbalance and textual leakage.

Result: Direct policies achieve higher decision quality and higher operational accuracy but reveal no reasoning. Reasoning policies provide trace but at reduced performance, especially recall of true intervention opportunities. Supervised fine-tuning either suppresses reasoning or preserves it without enhancing decision quality. Reinforcement learning fails to improve the reasoning policy. Mechanistically, group-relative objectives offer no learning signal for confidently wrong prompts when all rollouts pick the same action. Faithfulness metrics can overstate evidence of alignment; probability-based metrics saturate for confident decisions; probes can be biased by class imbalance and leakage; reasoning ablations can conflate content with inference mode.

Conclusion: Exposing reasoning can alter an agent’s action policy rather than merely making the process observable. The work provides controls for auditing reasoning-based oversight in agents capable of acting or abstaining, highlighting the need for evaluation paradigms that disentangle decision quality from explainability exposure.

Abstract: Many agentic systems must repeatedly choose between acting and abstaining, making faithful reasoning important for oversight: an explanation is useful only if it reflects the computation that produced the action. We study this problem through intervention timing in multi-party conversation, where an assistant must decide whether to speak or remain silent. This setting exposes class imbalance, asymmetric action costs, and the possibility that exposing reasoning changes the policy being audited. Using Qwen3-8B, decoded with or without chain-of-thought reasoning, we compare direct decision policies, reasoning policies, supervised fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning. We find a capability-auditability tradeoff: the strongest direct policy achieves higher quality but exposes no reasoning to inspect, while the reasoning policy provides a trace at the cost of lower performance, particularly recall of true intervention opportunities. Supervised fine-tuning either suppresses reasoning or preserves it without improving decision quality, while reinforcement learning also fails to improve the reasoning policy. We identify one mechanism underlying this failure: group relative objectives provide no learning signal on confidently wrong prompts when sampled rollouts all select the same action. Controlled activation probes and behavioral ablations show that standard faithfulness methods can overstate evidence that exposed reasoning reflects the underlying decision process. Probability-based metrics saturate under confident decisions, probes are vulnerable to class imbalance and textual leakage, and reasoning ablations can confound reasoning content with changes in inference mode. Together, these results show that exposing reasoning can change an agent's action policy rather than simply make it observable. We provide controls for evaluating reasoning-based oversight of agents that can act or abstain.

</details>


### [200] [CDRL: Certification-Driven Reinforcement Learning for Neutrino Flavor Model Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20686)
*Piyush Jha,Jake Rudolph,Victoria Knapp-Pérez,Max Fieg,Aishik Ghosh,Vijay Ganesh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CDRL uses certificates from symbolic reasoning to prune invalid actions in RL over large combinatorial spaces, improving discovery efficiency; demonstrated on neutrino flavor model discovery with substantial gains and interpretable rule extraction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL with scalar rewards yields limited failure feedback, causing wasteful exploration in constrained combinatorial spaces. Symbolic certificates provide structured, actionable feedback to prune invalid regions.

Method: When a candidate violates domain constraints, symbolic tools generate certificates indicating the responsible actions. These certificates are converted into reusable hard constraints to eliminate classes of invalid solutions. Additionally, a post-hoc decision-tree framework extracts interpretable rules from search trajectories (40 rules) and these are reused as soft constraints to further guide exploration. Evaluated on three theory spaces for neutrino flavor model discovery (hypothesis space > 10^26), comparing to a state-of-the-art RL baseline.

Result: CDRL yields up to 1.95× higher valid model rates and up to 6.33× higher neutrino model rates, while evaluating up to 4× fewer candidates. The 40 extracted rules enable soft-constraint gains of up to 2× in valid model rates and 3× in neutrino model discovery across all theory spaces.

Conclusion: CDRL uncovers reusable structure in combinatorial search spaces and offers a general framework for scientific model discovery by integrating symbolic certificates and interpretable rule-based guidance into RL.

Abstract: Many scientific discovery problems require searching combinatorial hypothesis spaces under complex domain constraints. Reinforcement learning (RL) offers a promising approach, but existing methods rely on scalar rewards that provide limited information about why candidate solutions fail, leading agents to repeatedly explore invalid regions. We introduce Certification-Driven Reinforcement Learning (CDRL), a framework that leverages structured feedback from symbolic reasoning tools. When a candidate violates domain constraints, these tools produce certificates identifying the actions responsible for failure. CDRL converts these certificates into reusable constraints that eliminate classes of invalid solutions and guide exploration toward valid regions. We evaluate CDRL on neutrino flavor model discovery in theoretical particle physics, where the hypothesis space exceeds $10^{26}$ possible models, and compare it with the state-of-the-art RL approach previously used for this task. Across three theory spaces, CDRL achieves up to 1.95$\times$ higher valid model rates and up to 6.33$\times$ higher neutrino model rates while evaluating up to 4$\times$ fewer candidates. We further extract 40 interpretable rules from search trajectories using a post-hoc decision-tree framework and show that reusing them as soft constraints yields gains of up to 2$\times$ in valid model rates and 3$\times$ in neutrino model discovery across all three theory spaces. These results suggest that CDRL uncovers reusable structure in combinatorial search spaces and provides a general framework for scientific model discovery.

</details>


### [201] [VortexChat: An agentic framework for autonomous multi-objective integrated photonic design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20688)
*Faqian Chong,Yulun Wu,Shilong Li,Andrew Forbes,Hongsheng Chen,Song Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An LLM-driven agent conducts autonomous, end-to-end inverse design for integrated photonics, combining topology generation, gradient refinement, and full-wave simulations to meet predefined benchmarks without human input; demonstrates fabrication of a broadband terahertz vortex beam multiplexer with results matching simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Alleviate bottlenecks in photonic design from manual, intuition-driven workflows and lack of end-to-end automation; enable autonomous, fabrication-feasible designs under clear performance thresholds.

Method: Integrates a large language model decision agent with topology generation, gradient-based refinement, and full-wave electromagnetic simulation in a closed-loop system guided by natural-language specifications and benchmark constraints (Vortex100). The agent decomposes objectives, orchestrates tools, and updates strategies iteratively with minimal human intervention.

Result: Autonomously generates devices meeting all predefined performance thresholds per the Vortex100 benchmark. Demonstrates fabrication of a broadband terahertz perfect vortex beam multiplexer with high efficiency, high mode purity, and low inter-channel crosstalk; experimental measurements align with full-wave simulations.

Conclusion: LLM-based agents can assume key aspects of expert decision-making in photonic inverse design while maintaining physical fidelity and fabrication feasibility, enabling scalable, autonomous design of complex integrated photonic systems.

Abstract: The advancement of modern integrated photonics is frequently bottlenecked by device design workflows that rely heavily on manual simulation and expert intuition. While inverse design offers an alternative, it remains constrained by expert supervision and a lack of end-to-end automation. To address these issues, we present VortexChat, an agentic framework for the autonomous, end-to-end inverse design of integrated photonic devices directly from natural language specifications. VortexChat couples a large language model (LLM) decision agent with topology generation, gradient-based refinement, and full-wave electromagnetic simulation. This closed-loop architecture enables the system to iteratively decompose design objectives, orchestrate computational tools, and update strategies based on feedback with minimal human intervention. Constrained by the absolute metrics of the Vortex100 Benchmark, VortexChat autonomously generates devices that strictly meet all predefined performance thresholds without any human-in-the-loop. As an experimental demonstration, we fabricated a broadband terahertz perfect vortex beam multiplexer, autonomously designed by VortexChat, with measurements confirming high-efficiency operation, high mode purity and low inter-channel crosstalk in agreement with full-wave simulations. These results demonstrate that an LLM agent can assume key aspects of expert decision-making in photonic inverse design while maintaining physical fidelity and fabrication feasibility, providing a scalable route towards autonomous design of complex integrated photonic systems.

</details>


### [202] [DirEAG: Dirichlet Evidence Aggregation for Calibrating Verbalized Confidence in Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20717)
*Haorui Xu,Yuzhou Zhu,Liyuan Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DirEAG converts elicited answer-confidence into calibrated soft evidence over candidate answers plus a null state using Dirichlet aggregation, improving calibration of self-reported confidence in LLM math reasoning across prompts/models/datasets with competitive answer selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Black-box confidence estimates from LLMs are hard to calibrate. Existing metrics (agreement, entropy) capture output variation but not the numerical meaning of confidence. Calibration must handle prompt-, task-, and model-dependent biases across steering prompts and datasets for reliable use in mathematical reasoning.

Method: Each elicited confidence observation is transformed into calibrated soft evidence across generated candidate answers and a null state, forming Dirichlet-distributed evidence. The evidences are aggregated across prompts/models/datasets, followed by a final calibration step (binary calibration) to align confidence with correctness. The approach decouples evidence aggregation from final calibration.

Result: DirEAG consistently improves confidence calibration compared to direct averaging and heuristic aggregation on GSM8K, SVAMP, and GSM-Hard across Qwen, Mistral, and Gemma, while maintaining competitive answer selection. Ablations show that evidence aggregation and binary calibration address distinct calibration components.

Conclusion: DirEAG provides a principled, model- and prompt-aware method to calibrate self-reported confidence for math reasoning by mapping observations to Dirichlet evidence over candidate answers and a null state, yielding better calibration without sacrificing answer quality.

Abstract: Reliable confidence estimation is essential for using large language models in mathematical reasoning, but black-box verbalized confidence is difficult to calibrate. When the same problem is queried under multiple confidence-steering prompts, the resulting answer-confidence observations contain useful uncertainty information, yet their scales may shift across steering levels, models, and datasets. Existing black-box uncertainty methods often rely on answer agreement, sample consistency, or entropy, which describe output variation but do not model the numerical meaning of self-reported confidence. Conversely, direct averaging or heuristic aggregation of elicited confidence cannot learn prompt- and task-dependent bias. We propose DirEAG, a Dirichlet Evidence Aggregation method that converts each elicited answer-confidence observation into calibrated soft evidence over generated candidate answers and an additional null state, allowing the model to represent cases where none of the candidates is correct. Experiments on GSM8K, SVAMP, and GSM-Hard with Qwen, Mistral, and Gemma models show that, compared with direct confidence averaging and heuristic confidence-steering aggregation, DirEAG often achieves better calibration while maintaining competitive answer selection. Ablations further reveal that evidence aggregation and final binary calibration address distinct parts of the calibration problem.

</details>


### [203] [Calibrating Criterion Revision in LLM Agents: Failure Modes and a Trace-Anchored Protocol](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20729)
*Guodong Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Evaluates a criterion-revision test framework for language-model agents (CMB). Finds CMB-0.1 fails to meet five non-compensatory conditions across 12 cross-domain cases, casting results as instrument-calibration rather than ranking. Proposes CMB-0.4 as a more discriminating protocol with concealed transfer, explicit action logs, and a frozen oracle, plus a measurement chain and empirical diagnosis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To formalize when a language-model agent has adopted and persistently used a revised success criterion (K1) that differs from a broader commitment (B), addressing attribution in criterion revision.

Method: Empirical evaluation of CMB-0.1 across 12 cross-domain cases with 4 arms (stateless inference, append-only history, model-generated but harness-committed state, evaluator-written oracle state). 7 mechanism fixtures yield 84 deterministic scorer trials; 4 local artifacts yield 96 calls and 192 model-case-arm trials.

Result: No trial satisfied all five non-compensatory conditions for criterion revision. Some commitments revealed target distinctions; harnesses performed commits; deletion reused stateless calls; Qwen2.5-7B provided transfer/preservation answers without revision state, indicating zero-state reconstruction. The outcome characterizes CMB-0.1 as instrument-calibration rather than a ranking metric.

Conclusion: Proposes a successor protocol, CMB-0.4, with concealed transfer, explicit WRITE/NO-WRITE/ESCALATE actions, separately logged policy-selected commits, matched interventions, repeated hidden items, and a frozen executable oracle. This is not a completed confirmatory result but a measurement chain, an empirical diagnosis, and a more discriminating protocol for future tests of criterion revision.

Abstract: Language-model agents can improve after failure or carry text across episodes without revising what counts as success. We study the narrower attribution problem of criterion revision: when criterion K0 accepts an outcome violating a broader commitment B, what observations justify saying that the system formed and persistently used K1? We require five non-compensatory conditions: criterion-failure detection, a model-emitted proposal, new-episode transfer, intervention sensitivity on the claimed carrier, and preservation.
  We evaluate CMB-0.1 on twelve cross-domain cases and four arms: stateless inference, append-only history, model-generated but harness-committed state, and evaluator-written oracle state. Seven mechanism fixtures yield 84 deterministic scorer trials; four local quantized artifacts yield 96 calls and 192 model-case-arm trials. No model trial satisfies all five conditions, but this zero does not establish general capability absence. Eleven calls remain invalid after one retry; several commitments disclose the target distinction; the harness performs commits; deletion reuses a stateless call; and conflict changes multiple factors. Qwen2.5-7B answers every transfer and preservation item without revision state, exposing zero-state reconstruction.
  These failures make CMB-0.1 an instrument-calibration result rather than a model ranking. We derive a prospective, trace-anchored CMB-0.4 protocol requiring concealed transfer, explicit WRITE/NO-WRITE/ESCALATE actions, a separately logged policy-selected commit, matched interventions, repeated hidden items, and a frozen executable oracle. It is a successor design, not a completed confirmatory result. The paper contributes a measurement chain, an empirical diagnosis of its first implementation, and a more discriminating protocol for future tests of criterion revision.

</details>


### [204] [Continuous-Time Quantum Walks based Graph Neural Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20738)
*Yuliang Zhan,Zefeng Gao,Jian Li,Yang Liu,Hao sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A quantum-walk-inspired GNN (CTQW-GNN) uses a unitary propagator to address both low-pass smoothing and over-smoothing, with three aggregations (CTQW, CTQW-attention, LF) and theoretical guidance via spectral-gap and Lieb–Robinson-type bounds for choosing the walk time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard GNNs suffer from: (1) low-pass filtering under homophily, hurting heterophilic graphs, and (2) over-smoothing as depth increases. Existing joint solutions are heuristic or reduce expressiveness. A principled, unitary propagation mechanism could preserve high-frequency information and prevent energy decay, addressing both issues.

Method: Introduce CTQW-GNN with three modules: (i) CTQW-based Aggregation using the unitary e^{-iHt} to propagate features while preserving mid/high frequencies and preventing Dirichlet-energy collapse; (ii) CTQW-Attention Aggregation builds a multi-hop neighbor graph from CTQW amplitudes and applies attention to aggregate distant nodes; (iii) LF Aggregation—a standard low-pass GAT branch to retain performance on homophilic graphs. Theoretical support includes spectral-gap analysis and a Lieb–Robinson-type bound to guide the walk time t.

Result: The framework provides theoretical justification that energy is preserved under CTQW and that Dirichlet energy does not decay with depth; the authors also supply bounds for selecting the walk time t, and demonstrate a principled, hybrid architecture that can adapt to both heterophilic and homophilic graphs.

Conclusion: CTQW-GNN offers a principled, unified approach to address both low-pass bias and over-smoothing via continuous-time quantum walks, combining complementary aggregations and providing theoretical guidance for hyperparameters, with potential robustness across graph types.

Abstract: Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) are widely used on graph-structured data, but most suffer from two key weaknesses. First, message passing behaves as a low-pass filter under the homophily assumption, leading to poor performance on heterophilic graphs. Second, stacking layers drives node features toward constants, causing over-smoothing. Existing methods usually address these issues separately, while the few joint solutions rely largely on empirical heuristics, and many over-smoothing remedies sacrifice model expressiveness.
  We propose \textbf{CTQW-GNN}, a GNN based on Continuous-Time Quantum Walks (CTQW), to address both issues with theoretical justification. Its design exploits two properties of the CTQW propagator $e^{-\mathrm{i}Ht}$. First, it is unitary and has eigenvalues on the unit circle, so no frequency component is damped, counteracting the low-pass bias. Second, unitarity preserves feature norms and prevents the Dirichlet energy from decaying exponentially with depth, thereby mitigating over-smoothing.
  CTQW-GNN combines three complementary aggregation modules. \textit{CTQW-based Aggregation} evolves node features through the unitary propagator, preserving mid- and high-frequency signals for heterophilic graphs while preventing Dirichlet-energy collapse. \textit{CTQW-Attention Aggregation} constructs a multi-hop neighbor graph from CTQW amplitudes and applies attention over it, enabling access to distant homophilic nodes missed by single-hop aggregation. \textit{LF Aggregation} uses a standard low-pass GAT branch to retain strong performance on homophilic graphs, where pure CTQW aggregation can be suboptimal. We further provide a spectral-gap analysis explaining energy preservation and a Lieb--Robinson-type bound that gives a principled rule for selecting the walk time $t$.

</details>


### [205] [Is Multimodal Speculative Decoding Ready for Diffusion-Based Parallel Drafting? A Survey and Empirical Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20743)
*Yantao Li,Huanlin Gao,Fang Zhao,Chao Tan,Qiang Hui,Shuting Liu,Fuyuan Shi,Ting Lu,Shaoan Zhao,Xueqiang Guo,Xinpei Su,Jianbing Zhang,Xinyu Dai,Kai Wang,Shiguo Lian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A survey and empirical study assessing diffusion-based, block-parallel speculative drafting for multimodal models, offering taxonomy, benchmarks, and directions for readiness and improvement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether block-parallel generative drafting (diffusion-based) can extend to multimodal autoregressive models, bridging the gap between text-only advances and multimodal workloads where speculation and parallel drafting are less explored.

Method: Systematic analysis across Vision-Language, Video-Language, Audio, and Vision-Language-Action architectures; introduces a unified taxonomy separating drafter-side parallelism from verification/tree construction; provides an empirical comparison of existing methods under varying parallelism on OCR, VQA, visual reasoning, and image captioning.

Result: Proposes a unified taxonomy and reports empirical comparisons across multimodal benchmarks under different parallelism degrees, revealing current limitations, open challenges, and actionable future directions for multimodal speculative drafting.

Conclusion: Multimodal speculative decoding is promising but not yet fully mature; while diffusion-based parallel drafting shows potential, substantial challenges remain in cross-modal information interaction and verification, warranting targeted future work.

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates autoregressive generation by allowing a lightweight drafter to propose future tokens while a target model verifies them in parallel. Its lossless guarantee has motivated a line of work that pushes the drafter itself toward parallel generation. The most recent paradigm is block-parallel generative drafting, including diffusion-based methods such as DFlash and DSpark, achieving up to 3.6x speedup on common daily chatting tasks. While this transition is well studied in text-only LLMs, its applicability to multimodal models remains an open question. Existing multimodal speculative decoding efforts focus on input compression, adapter alignment, candidate coverage, or modality-specific verification; however, block-parallel generative drafting remains largely unexplored. To bridge this gap, this paper combines a modality-centered survey with a cross-architecture empirical study to ask: Is multimodal speculative decoding ready for diffusion-based parallel drafting? In this survey, we systematically analyze a wide spectrum of multimodal models, spanning Vision-Language, Video-Language, Audio, and Vision-Language-Action (VLA) architectures, from the dual perspectives of drafting parallelism and cross-modal information interaction. We introduce a unified taxonomy that isolates drafter-side parallelism from orthogonal design choices such as tree construction and verification strategies. Furthermore, we provide a comprehensive empirical comparison of existing methods under varying degrees of parallelism across standardized multimodal benchmarks, including OCR, VQA, visual reasoning, and image captioning. Finally, we summarize the limitations of current approaches, discuss open challenges, and outline promising future directions for this rapidly evolving field.

</details>


### [206] [Natural-Language-Guided Generator-Agnostic Shortlisting for Protein Binder Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20755)
*Gyubok Lee,Kiwoong Yoo,Jimin Seo,Kyunghoon Hur,Edward Choi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs can learn multi-metric ranking policies for post-generation shortlisting of protein binders, modestly outperforming single-feature baselines by integrating heterogeneous proxy scores; target-conditioned policies show strongest performance on specific target sets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce wet-lab validation burden by outsourcing the decision layer for shortlisting binders, using LLMs to synthesize multiple proxy metrics into interpretive ranking policies.

Method: Utilize precomputed structural-confidence and interface-quality scores as proxies; apply iterative GPT-4o and GPT-5.4 prompting to generate ranking policies that select top-K binders from large candidate pools; evaluate on held-out target splits (10-target global and 3-target Nipah/RBX1/TREM2) using Recall@10 and NDCG@10; compare against fixed baselines (e.g., Protenix ipTM).

Result: On the 10-target held-out split, averaged over five GPT-4o policies, Recall@10 = 0.589, modestly above Protenix ipTM at 0.571. On the 3-target held-out set (Nipah, RBX1, TREM2), target-conditioned GPT-5.4 policies achieve Recall@10 = 0.519 and NDCG@10 = 0.583, outperforming single-feature baselines.

Conclusion: LLM-generated ranking policies can serve as an interpretable post-generation layer to fuse heterogeneous proxy metrics for binder prioritization, with modest gains and evident potential for improved, explainable shortlisting in large candidate pools.

Abstract: Modern de novo design workflows generate many candidate protein binders, but wet-lab validation capacity remains limited, making shortlisting a major bottleneck. We study whether LLMs can generate multi-metric ranking policies from precomputed structural-confidence and interface-quality proxy scores. Rather than proposing a new protein binder design pipeline, we focus on post-generation binder shortlisting: selecting the final top-K candidates from already generated binder pools using a shared panel of precomputed proxy scores. On the 10-target held-out split, averaging performance over five sampled global iterative gpt-4o policies reaches 0.589 Recall@10, modestly improving over the strongest single-feature fixed baseline, Protenix binder ipTM, which reaches 0.571 Recall@10. On the 3-target held-out subset comprising Nipah, RBX1, and TREM2, target-conditioned iterative gpt-5.4 policies reach the strongest LLM performance, with 0.519 Recall@10 and 0.583 NDCG@10. These results suggest that LLM-generated ranking policies can act as an interpretable post-generation decision layer for combining heterogeneous proxy metrics to prioritize binders from large candidate pools.

</details>


### [207] [Beyond Endpoint Gains: A Weight-Delta Audit of Medical Specialization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20768)
*Praphul Singh,Shanu Kumar,Akshat Agarwal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A paired weight-delta audit shows decoder updates reconstruct medical-task performance, but the movement cannot be localized to a single component; MLP is implicated but controls prevent a simple explanation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To interrogate how updates from generalist to medical-specialist checkpoints move capabilities, beyond endpoint gains, by auditing the actual parameter deltas.

Method: Apply a paired weight-delta path audit on two public generalist-to-medical-specialist checkpoint pairs; analyze the full decoder-side update's ability to reconstruct benchmark movement; use controls (off-domain movements, 10-seed matched controls) and endpoint-anchored rollbacks to assess localization.

Result: The full decoder delta strongly reconstructs medical benchmark movement (endpoint-normalized retention: 0.974 and 1.183) but the movement is not cleanly localized to a single component; MLP is the strongest broad component family yet mixed movements and controls prevent a unique coarse-family explanation.

Conclusion: The audit separates update-level reconstruction from component-level explanation; claims concern text-only multiple-choice benchmark movement, not clinical validation, repair, or circuit-level mechanism.

Abstract: Specialist language models are usually understood through endpoint gains: the generalist scores lower, the specialist scores higher, and the difference is treated as evidence of specialization. This leaves the released update itself largely unexamined. We propose a paired weight-delta path audit and apply it to two public, aligned generalist-to-medical-specialist checkpoint pairs: Gemma-3-4B-IT to MedGemma-4B-IT and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct to HuatuoGPT-o1-7B. In both pairs, the full decoder-side update strongly reconstructs measured medical benchmark movement (0.974 and 1.183 endpoint-normalized retention), making each decoder delta an appropriate substrate for the audit. Yet the movement is not cleanly localized. MLP is the strongest broad component family in both pairs, but mixed off-domain movements, 10-seed matched controls, and endpoint-anchored rollbacks prevent a unique coarse-family explanation. The audit therefore separates update-level reconstruction from component-level explanation. Its claims concern text-only multiple-choice benchmark movement, not clinical validation, repair, or circuit-level mechanism.

</details>


### [208] [CAS: Conformalized Agentic Search via Adaptive Retrieval and Policy Weighting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20771)
*Zixi Zhu,Jiayuan Su,Jian Zhang,Yu Lin,Hongwei Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Conformalized Agentic Search (CAS) uses conformal prediction to provide adaptive, reliability-guaranteed retrieval and training in reinforcement-learning fine-tuning, improving QA accuracy and reducing redundant tool use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses reliability issues in RL fine-tuning for search agents: Top-K retrieval can lose critical evidence or introduce noise, and progressive RL can induce over-confidence, hallucinations, and wasteful searches.

Method: Integrates Conformal Prediction with Adaptive Prediction Sets (APS) for retrieval and Adaptive Conformal Inference (ACI) for training, forming CAS. APS adapts document truncation based on coverage; ACI dynamically builds prediction sets with controllable coverage, penalizing low-confidence trajectories within the Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) objective.

Result: Empirical results on single-hop and multi-hop QA datasets show higher reasoning accuracy and substantially fewer redundant tool invocations, indicating a more reliable and efficient agent.

Conclusion: CAS provides a reliable, efficient agent paradigm with theoretical reliability guarantees for both retrieval and training stages; code is available at the referenced GitHub repository.

Abstract: Search Agents face a severe reliability crisis during reinforcement learning (RL) fine-tuning. Heuristic Top-K retrieval often causes critical evidence loss or noise inclusion, while over-confidence induced by progressive RL leads to hallucinated answers and redundant searches.
  To build highly reliable agents, we introduce Conformal Prediction (CP) and propose Conformalized Agentic Search (CAS). This framework establishes reliability guarantees on both the retrieval and training sides: on the retrieval side, an Adaptive Prediction Set (APS), a specific CP realization, translates statistical coverage into dynamic document truncation to construct prediction sets that are adaptive in size; on the training side, Adaptive Conformal Inference (ACI), a dynamic CP algorithm, dynamically constructs prediction sets with controllable coverage to quantify answer confidence, which is then used to penalize low-confidence trajectories within the Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) objective, ensuring the model learns only from reliable ones.
  Experiments across single-hop and multi-hop QA datasets demonstrate that our framework significantly improves reasoning accuracy while drastically reducing redundant tool invocations, establishing a highly reliable and efficient agent paradigm. Our code is available at https://github.com/S1llyBird/CAS.

</details>


### [209] [Structure for Reading, Prose for Writing: Asymmetric Structural Conditioning in Multi-Agent Document Authoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20786)
*Cheng Yu,Nikhil Mathew,Zhengjie Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An open-weights LLM-based tender-response pipeline can match or exceed human bids in many evaluation sections; most observed gaps arise from missing knowledge rather than writing quality; structural markup improves information extraction but does not transfer to prompting/conditioning; explicitly naming restricted constructions concentrates defects; using stochastic annotation with a deterministic windowing method reduces extracted requirement counts; overall guidance: structure should be used where the model reads, prose for writing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate multi-agent pipelines that read requester forms and write formal documents in tender processes, and study how input structure and prompt conditioning affect reading/extraction versus writing quality.

Method: Deploy a tender-response system using an open-weights model under sovereignty constraints; conduct blind comparison with a human-submitted bid; have an LLM judge rate the system's answers against ground-truth sections; classify judge-identified gaps; analyze effects of converting bid instructions to nested XML vs prose; assess impact of naming forbidden constructions; test stochastic annotation with a deterministic windowing function; report extraction and writing-quality metrics.

Result: The system matched or exceeded human performance on many ground-truth sections: 40 of 55 sections rated at least as good as human-submitted; 4 rated better; none worse; 1 unsupported claim flagged. Of judge-identified gaps, 68% were due to content missing from the system's sources (knowledge the human author held); only 6 of 15 adverse verdicts were potentially avoidable. Conditioning asymmetry: converting instruction prose to nested XML reduced answer quality from 74% to 48% in a paired comparison. 96% of surviving defects fall into two forms the prompt explicitly names. Adding stochastic annotation with a deterministic windowing function reduced the extracted requirement count from 68 to 51 on a byte-identical file.

Conclusion: Structure belongs in the reading path to improve extraction; prose and self-applied tests remain appropriate for the writing path.

Abstract: Multi-agent pipelines that author formal documents must both read a requester's forms and write against them. We report a deployed tender-response system, running an open-weights model under sovereignty constraints, and evaluate it against human-written bids the same organisation actually submitted. On a blind comparison where the system had no worked example available, an LLM judge rated its answers at least as good as the human-submitted answer on $40$ of $55$ ground-truth sections, better on $4$, missing on none, and flagged one unsupported claim in total. Classifying every gap the judge identified shows that $68\%$ were content absent from the system's own sources -- knowledge the human author held and the pipeline was never given -- so only $6$ of the $15$ adverse verdicts involve a deficiency the system could have avoided. A divergence from ground truth is more often an information-availability result than a writing-quality one, and evaluations that do not separate the two understate such systems. Against this backdrop we report a conditioning asymmetry. It is well established that rendering documents as structural markup rather than flat prose improves extraction, and we reproduce that on three reading tasks. The benefit does not transfer to conditioning: converting a bid's \emph{instruction} material from prose to nested XML dropped answer quality from $74\%$ to $48\%$ under a paired comparison. We further find that naming a forbidden construction concentrates rather than removes it -- $96\%$ of surviving defects fall in the two forms the prompt explicitly names -- and that coupling a stochastic annotation to a deterministic windowing function moves the extracted requirement count from $68$ to $51$ on a byte-identical file. Structure belongs where the model reads; prose and self-applied tests belong where it writes.

</details>


### [210] [Knowing but Not Saying: Preventing Factual Access Failures in LLM SFT via Recall-Anchored Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20794)
*Haodong Chen,Yadong Wang,Shengtao Wen,Dong Liang,Xiang Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RAD (Recall-Anchored Distillation) mitigates factual access failure after domain SFT by aligning adapted model to base model's soft continuation on unlabeled OOD text; preserves in-domain performance while recovering OOD recall; outperforms simple replay.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SFT can degrade factual behavior in open-ended generation due to factual access failure, including wrong-generation and expression-level issues; need data-efficient methods to preserve OOD recall without labeled data.

Method: Propose Recall-Anchored Distillation (RAD): a base-anchored self-distillation objective that aligns the fine-tuned model's soft distribution with the base model's continuation on unlabeled out-of-domain text. No gold OOD answers or external judges required. Evaluated on MedMCQA with three backbones.

Result: RAD recovers a consistent portion of lost OOD recall while maintaining target-domain adaptation; the preservation signal comes from the base model's soft distribution rather than mere extra exposure; results across three backbones show robustness.

Conclusion: RAD offers a practical, label-free approach to mitigate factual access failure post-SFT, maintaining in-domain performance and improving out-of-domain recall by anchoring to the base model's soft distribution.

Abstract: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) can degrade factual behavior outside the target domain. This degradation is often described as catastrophic forgetting, yet open-ended factual failures do not necessarily imply that the underlying facts have been erased. In this work, we identify a more specific phenomenon, factual access failure: after domain SFT, models can still recognize or rank the correct answer under constrained evaluation, while failing to produce it in closed-book generation. Through benchmark-level comparisons, same-fact multiple-choice and generation probes, and failure-mode analysis, we show that SFT-induced factual degradation reflects both genuine wrong-answer generations and expression-level failures such as verbosity, formatting mismatch, and exact-match artifacts. To address this problem, we introduce Recall-Anchored Distillation (RAD), a base-anchored self-distillation objective that preserves out-of-distribution generation behavior by aligning the adapted model with the original base model's soft continuation distribution on unlabeled OOD text. RAD requires no gold OOD answers, external judges, or labeled factual data. Across three backbones fine-tuned on MedMCQA, RAD recovers a consistent portion of the lost OOD recall while preserving target-domain adaptation. Compared with replay on the same OOD text, RAD shows that the key preservation signal is the base model's soft distribution rather than additional text exposure alone.

</details>


### [211] [Automated Trajectory Evaluation for Mobile Agents via Step-Level Consequence Reasoning and Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20797)
*Pengshuai Yang,Zijing Gao,Xue Yu,Benhui Zhuang,Bo Yuan,Junlan Feng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CRATE is a two-stage VLM-based evaluator that derives step-level evidence from visual cues and action changes to produce trajectory-level task completion scores; CRATE-S extends this framework to safety assessment, delivering robust, evidence-grounded evaluation aligned with benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional holistic, rule-based or model-based evaluations process entire trajectories and focus on task completion, neglecting context overload and operational safety. There is a need for scalable, evidence-grounded, safety-aware evaluation compatible with open- and closed-source models.

Method: CRATE performs step-level consequence reasoning by extracting task-relevant visual cues and inferring action-conditioned state changes at each step, then aggregates these step-level evidences into a trajectory-level score. CRATE-S applies the same approach to evaluate safety of agent behavior. The framework is powered by a vision-language model (VLM) and is compatible with models like Qwen2.5-VL-72B-Instruct.

Result: CRATE achieves an F1 of 0.833 on AndroidWorld (≈20% better than SPA-Bench); CRATE-S achieves an F1 of 0.697 on MobileRisk, with strong alignment to ground-truth benchmarks.

Conclusion: CRATE and CRATE-S offer an evidence-grounded, scalable evaluation paradigm for language-guided mobile agents, addressing context overload and safety concerns, with open-source code available.

Abstract: Evaluating language-guided mobile agents has recently shifted from rule-based to model-based approaches to achieve scalable and automated assessments. However, existing holistic evaluation paradigms process entire trajectories at once, leading to substantial context overload. Moreover, they primarily focus on task completion while overlooking operational safety. To address these limitations, we introduce CRATE, a novel two-stage VLM-as-judge framework for automated mobile agent evaluation that is compatible with both open- and closed-source models. Leveraging a step-level consequence reasoning mechanism, CRATE independently extracts task-relevant visual clues and infers action-conditioned state changes at each step. The resulting step-level textual evidence is then synthesized through trajectory-level aggregation to deliver an evidence-grounded evaluation of task completion. Building upon this evaluation scheme, we further extend CRATE to CRATE-S for operational safety assessment. Extensive experiments validate the effectiveness and robustness of both CRATE and CRATE-S. Powered by Qwen2.5-VL-72B-Instruct, CRATE achieves an F1-score of 0.833 on AndroidWorld (outperforming SPA-Bench by 20%), while CRATE-S reaches an F1-score of 0.697 on MobileRisk, demonstrating strong alignment with benchmark ground truths. Code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/CRATE-D580.

</details>


### [212] [Dynamic Context Scheduling: Learning Beyond the Static Universe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20799)
*Martin Mráz,André Biedenkapp*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Dynamic context scheduling (DYNAMICCARLENV) treats intra-episode context variation as a shaping mechanism in contextual RL, wrapping environments with pluggable schedules to vary parameters within episodes. It improves OOD generalization and, in some cases, ID performance, with initial results suggesting automatic multi-stage curricula can rival grid search over single-stage schedulers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enhance generalization and robustness in contextual RL by exposing policies to richer, temporally structured context distributions within training episodes instead of treating context variation as fixed deployment randomness.

Method: Introduce DYNAMICCARLENV, a framework that wraps contextual environments with pluggable schedule families (e.g., sinusoidal offsets, cosine annealing). Evaluate on CartPole, BipedalWalker, and VehicleRacing with CARL contextualization, comparing dynamic schedules to static baselines in OOD and ID regimes. Explore automatic search for multi-stage curricula to discover effective schedules.

Result: Dynamic schedules match or outperform static context baselines in OOD regimes. In more complex environments (BipedalWalker, VehicleRacing), dynamic schedules also yield higher ID performance. Preliminary findings indicate automatic search for multi-stage curricula can discover schedules that improve generalization, comparable to grid search over single-stage schedulers.

Conclusion: Dynamic context scheduling is a promising approach to improve generalization in contextual RL, enabling richer training distributions and potentially reducing exhaustive hyperparameter search via automatic curriculum discovery.

Abstract: We study dynamic context scheduling as a training instrument for contextual re- inforcement learning. Rather than treating intra-episode context variation as a deployment reality, we treat it as a controlled shaping mechanism. Thereby, context evolves within each training episode according to a predetermined schedule, expos- ing the policy to a richer and more temporally structured region of the environment parameter space. We introduce DYNAMICCARLENV, a framework that wraps contextual environments with pluggable schedule families, such as sinusoidal off- sets or cosine annealing. Across CartPole, BipedalWalker and VehicleRacing with CARL contextualization, we show that dynamic schedules match or outperform static context baselines in the out-of-distribution (OOD) regimes. Interestingly, for the more complex BipedalWalker and VehicleRacing environments we also achieve higher in-distribution (ID) evaluation performance. Preliminary findings indicate that automatic search for multi-stage curricula can successfully discover schedules that improve generalization, performing comparably to extensive grid search over single-stage schedulers.

</details>


### [213] [SPARC: Single-Pass Scaling for Motion Forecasting with Conformal Bayesian Last Layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20802)
*Sakif Hossain,Julian Teusch,Jörg P. Müller*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SPARC is a Bayesian-conformal, single-pass uncertainty layer for motion forecasting that yields horizon-wise epistemic scales and calibrated prediction tubes without MC sampling, achieving state-of-the-art NLL and MPJPE+NLL across multiple datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a need for reliable, structured, and efficient uncertainty in motion forecasting. Traditional Bayesian/ensemble methods are computationally heavy, and conformal calibration alone lacks epistemic signaling and preserves trajectory covariance.

Method: A deterministic MLP backbone predicts the future mean. A conjugate Bayesian last layer converts time-domain feature leverage into an analytic horizon-wise epistemic scale κ_t(x) that inflates a graph-temporal Gaussian covariance without altering correlations. Split conformal calibration yields 95% prediction tubes with finite-sample validity under exchangeability. The factorization κ_t(x) Σ_str,t(x) injects feature-space epistemic uncertainty into trajectory densities without Monte Carlo sampling.

Result: SPARC surpasses baselines across nine dataset-protocol blocks for deterministic, multimodal, and calibration settings, ranking first on negative log-likelihood (NLL) and on the combined MPJPE+NLL criterion while maintaining competitive point accuracy and efficiently calibrated tubes. Ranking by κ highlights high-error cases, enabling SPARC to function as a lightweight risk monitor.

Conclusion: SPARC delivers efficient, structured, and calibrated epistemic uncertainty for motion forecasting in a single pass, enabling practical deployment and intrinsic risk monitoring without MC sampling.

Abstract: Human motion forecasters are increasingly accurate and fast, but reliable deployment requires uncertainty estimates that are structured, calibrated, and efficient. Bayesian and ensemble-based uncertainty estimates often require repeated stochastic inference [15, 26], while conformal calibration alone does not provide an epistemic signal or preserve trajectory covariance structure [14, 50]. We introduce SPARC (Single-Pass Adaptive Risk Calibration), a Bayesian-conformal uncertainty layer for motion forecasting. A deterministic MLP backbone predicts the future mean, and a conjugate Bayesian last layer converts time-domain feature leverage into an analytic horizon-wise epistemic scale $κ_t(x)$. This scale inflates a graph-temporal Gaussian covariance without changing its correlation structure, and split conformal calibration produces 95% marginal prediction tubes with finite-sample validity under exchangeability. The key interface is the structured factorization $κ_t(x)Σ_{\mathrm{str},t}(x)$, which injects feature-space epistemic uncertainty into trajectory densities without Monte Carlo sampling. Across nine dataset-protocol blocks and deterministic, multimodal, and calibration baselines, SPARC ranks first on NLL and on the combined MPJPE+NLL criterion while retaining competitive point accuracy and efficient calibrated tubes. Ranking windows by $κ$ separates high-error cases, making the scale usable as a lightweight risk monitor.

</details>


### [214] [Neuro-Geospatial Modelling of EEG Affective States Using Literature-Informed Environmental Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20807)
*Utsav Poudel,Jagannath Aryal,Subramaniyaswamy Vairavasundaram*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Multimodal EEG-environment model improves affective-state classification by using literature-informed environmental priors; achieves 76.2% accuracy vs 67.4% with EEG alone; environment treated as priors, not measured exposure; not causal; framework for future joint mobile EEG-environment studies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Environmental exposures are linked to affective and cognitive outcomes, but jointly georeferenced EEG and environmental data are rare. The study investigates whether literature-informed environmental priors can augment EEG-based classification when individual exposure data are unavailable.

Method: Dual-tower architecture combining EEG-Conformer representations with a graph-based environmental encoder. Environmental context is a literature-informed prior rather than measured exposure because datasets are not co-registered. Data sources include OpenAQ, Sentinel-2, Sentinel-5P, and OpenStreetMap for Astana. Subjects: 42 participants (20–30 years). Extensive controls: subject-level repeated splits, permutation/label-shuffling controls, dose-response reversal, and domain-shift experiments to separate architecture gains from prior gains. Ablations include replacing Astana distribution with independently modeled Singapore distribution. Implementation available at https://github.com/r11up/geo-cog.

Result: The multimodal model achieves 76.2% accuracy, higher than EEG alone at 67.4%. Controls disrupting environmental-label structure preserve part of the gain, indicating improvements are not solely due to environmental priors. Replacing Astana exposure with a Singapore distribution reduces accuracy to 72.8%. These results demonstrate feasibility but do not establish a causal exposure–affect association; the study provides a framework for future jointly collected mobile EEG–environment studies.

Conclusion: Shows technical feasibility of augmenting EEG with literature-informed environmental priors for affective-state classification; not causal; offers a pipeline and implementation reference for future, jointly collected EEG–environment datasets.

Abstract: Environmental exposures such as air pollution and greenness have been associated with affective and cognitive outcomes, but EEG and environmental datasets are rarely jointly georeferenced. We investigate whether literature-informed environmental priors can serve as an auxiliary geospatial modality for EEG-based affective-state classification when individual-level exposure data are unavailable. We combine 30-channel EEG from the EAV benchmark (42 participants, aged 20-30 years) with environmental representations derived from OpenAQ, Sentinel-2, Sentinel-5P, and OpenStreetMap data for Astana. A dual-tower architecture combines EEG-Conformer representations with a graph-based environmental encoder. Because the datasets are not co-registered, environmental context is treated as a literature-informed prior rather than measured exposure. Subject-level repeated splits, permutation and label-shuffling controls, dose-response reversal, and domain-shift experiments distinguish architecture-level gains from prior-dependent gains. The multimodal model achieves 76.2% accuracy versus 67.4% for EEG alone. Controls disrupting environmental-label structure retain part of this gain, indicating that the improvement is not attributable solely to environmental information. Replacing the Astana environmental distribution with an independently modeled Singapore distribution reduces accuracy to 72.8%. These findings demonstrate technical feasibility but do not establish an observed or causal exposure-affect association. The study provides a framework for future jointly collected mobile EEG-environment studies. Implementation: https://github.com/r11up/geo-cog

</details>


### [215] [Certified Multi-Turn Robustness for LLM Safety via Compositional Bounds and Safety Persistence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20820)
*Yang Liu,Bin Chong,Wenkai Yang,Shuai Zhang,Yancheng Chen,Feiyu Han,GuoZhen,Cheng Zhang,Huaibing Xie,Changze Lv,Shihan Dou,Pluto Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MTCR defines k-turn certified robustness for LLMs against multi-turn jailbreaks via State-Adversarial MDPs, offering compositional, tighter bounds through embedding-space mode decomposition, α,β-safety persistence to slow degradation, tight information-theoretic upper bounds, and a unified algorithm; validated empirically across six LLMs against ε-bounded and Crescendo-style attacks, with observed safety surpassing certified bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are vulnerable to progressive, multi-turn jailbreaks that modify conversation context. Existing certified robustness methods cover only single-turn inputs; naive multi-turn composition causes exponential degradation of bounds. A rigorous, scalable, and interpretable multi-turn certification framework is needed.

Method: Propose Multi-Turn Certified Robustness (MTCR) by modeling conversations as State-Adversarial MDPs. Define k-turn certified robustness as the worst-case safety probability over k adversarial turns. Introduce (i) compositional certification via embedding-space mode decomposition for tighter lower bounds than naïve product; (ii) (α,β)-safety persistence yielding degradation rate β^k with β>p_low, enabling interpretable horizon estimates; (iii) information-theoretic upper bounds rendering tightness; (iv) a unified algorithm that integrates these results.

Result: The framework yields tighter lower bounds than naïve multiplicative bounds, shows improved degradation behavior via α,β-safety persistence, and matches information-theoretic upper bounds, indicating tightness. Empirical evaluations on six LLMs under ε-bounded and Crescendo-style attacks show empirical safety exceeds certified bounds.

Conclusion: MTCR offers a principled, tight, and interpretable approach to multi-turn robustness, combining strong theoretical guarantees with practical empirical validation. It closes gaps left by single-turn certs, and its unified algorithm facilitates deployment; future work could extend to broader attack models, streaming efficiency, and deeper comparisons with alternative robustness paradigms.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are vulnerable to multi-turn jailbreak attacks that progressively manipulate conversation context. Existing certified robustness methods are limited to single-turn inputs; naive multi-turn composition yields bounds that degrade exponentially in the number of turns. We introduce Multi-Turn Certified Robustness (MTCR), a framework that models conversational safety via State-Adversarial MDPs and defines $k$-turn certified robustness as the worst-case safety probability across $k$ adversarial turns. MTCR comprises: (i) compositional certification via embedding-space mode decomposition, yielding tighter certified lower bounds than naive multiplication; (ii) $(α,β)$-safety persistence, improving the degradation rate from $\underline{p}^{k}$ to $β^k$ (with $β> \underline{p}$) and yielding interpretable horizon estimates; (iii) matching information-theoretic upper bounds establishing tightness; and (iv) a unified algorithm combining these results. Experiments on six LLMs under $ε$-bounded and Crescendo-style attacks confirm that empirical safety consistently exceeds the certified bounds.

</details>


### [216] [Prediction certification cannot replace explanation certification: a competence envelope for trustworthy AI under compound stress](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20825)
*Nataliya Shakhovska,Ivan Izonin,Stergios-Aristoteles Mitoulis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Prediction-side certificates (accuracy, calibration, coverage) are insufficient for trust; a separation theorem shows models can share identical predictions under these certificates yet differ arbitrarily in explanations and deployment behavior; the authors propose the competence envelope to jointly certify predictions and explanations, revealing failure modes unseen by prediction metrics; certification thus requires access to the model's decision mechanism in addition to its outputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation metrics focus on predictive performance but not on how the model reasons or how it behaves in deployment. This work argues that explanation fidelity and decision mechanisms can diverge even when standard prediction metrics look identical, potentially undermining trust.

Method: Theoretically proves a separation theorem showing identical prediction-side certificates even for compromised models, then defines a competence envelope that couples prediction and explanation certificates into a deployable criterion. Empirically validates the framework across diverse datasets and model classes, demonstrating failure modes not captured by prediction-only certification.

Result: Demonstrates that relying solely on accuracy, calibration, and coverage can miss crucial failure modes related to explanations and deployment behavior. The competence envelope detects such failures and provides a more robust, deployable certification.

Conclusion: Trustworthy AI certification must consider the model's decision mechanism (explanations) in addition to its outputs. The competence envelope offers a practical framework to integrate prediction and explanation certificates into a single deployable criterion.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence systems increasingly make consequential judgments - which patient is deteriorating, which building is safe to enter, whether an image is authentic and are trusted on the strength of how accurately and confidently they predict. The safeguards that certify them are correspondingly prediction-based: accuracy, calibration and conformal coverage all measure how well a model performs. Whether such checks are sufficient to establish model trustworthiness has remained unclear. Here we prove that they cannot. We establish a separation theorem showing that a reliable model and a compromised one can be identical under every prediction-side certificate, including accuracy, calibration and coverage, yet differ arbitrarily in explanation fidelity and deployment behaviour. Detecting this failure requires access to the model's decision mechanism in addition to its predictions. We introduce the competence envelope as an operational framework that combines prediction and explanation certification into a single deployable criterion. Across diverse datasets and model classes, the proposed framework reveals failure modes that prediction-side certification alone does not capture. Certification against failures that are invisible in prediction behaviour therefore requires evidence about the model's decision mechanism as well as its outputs.

</details>


### [217] [Foundation Models for Partial Causal Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20841)
*Alexis Bellot,Anish Dhir*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes causal foundation models with a canonical prior over discrete structural causal models to bound counterfactuals from observational data by learning distributions over functions that map data (and structure) to the causal query; enables estimation of partially-identifiable causal effects under unobserved confounding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bounding interventions and counterfactuals using observational data is hard under unobserved confounding; existing point-identification fails, necessitating a principled probabilistic framework to quantify uncertainty and incorporate structural assumptions.

Method: Define a canonical prior with full support over the space of structural causal models with discrete observables. Recast the counterfactual bounding problem as learning distributions over functions that take data and possibly structural assumptions as input and output the causal query of interest.

Result: Frames bounding counterfactuals and partially-identifiable effects within a causal foundation model, enabling estimation under unobserved confounding by learning distributions over mapping functions rather than single estimates.

Conclusion: Extends causal foundational modelling to partially-identifiable causal effects, providing a principled approach to quantify uncertainty when multiple outcomes are compatible with the data and prior assumptions.

Abstract: This paper investigates the development of causal foundation models for bounding the effect of interventions and counterfactuals from observational data. We show that a canonical prior can be defined with full support over the space of structural causal models with discrete observables. With this canonical prior, we translate the problem of bounding counterfactuals into that of learning distributions over functions that map data (and possibly structural assumptions) to a causal query of interest. This extends the promising causal foundational modelling paradigm to the estimation of partially-identifiable causal effects, i.e., under unobserved confounding, where multiple values are equally compatible with the observed data and prior structural assumptions.

</details>


### [218] [TRACE: Agentic Catalog Enrichment with Multi-source Evidence Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20844)
*Rohan Kumar,Steven Xu,Kyle MacDonald,Matthew Long,Bernice Chow,Mac VanRenterghem,Sudeep Das*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TRACE presents an agentic-LLM framework (ScoutAgent + JudgeAgent) for automated catalog attribute enrichment using multimodal evidence; achieves high offline accuracy with substantial attribute coverage and real-world business impact, including a notable uplift in checkout conversion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Catalog attribute sparsity hinders search, discovery, and recommendations; manual enrichment is impractical at scale; need automated, scalable, evidence-grounded enrichment.

Method: ScoutAgent triangulates evidence from merchant catalogs, feeds, and web search to propose candidate attribute values with evidence; JudgeAgent verifies against evidence and decides publish vs human review.

Result: Offline evaluation: 98.2% accuracy at 74.7% attribute coverage. Production deployment: 90.4% increase in impression-weighted enrichment coverage across four verticals. Online experiment: 0.48% uplift in checkout conversion on product detail pages.

Conclusion: Agentic LLM-based enrichment can scale attribute completion while maintaining accuracy and delivering measurable business value; the dual-agent design effectively balances automation and quality control.

Abstract: Product catalogs underpin search, discovery, and recommendation in e-commerce, yet they are often attribute-sparse: the attributes shoppers and downstream systems rely on are either buried in unstructured content such as titles and images or missing from the catalog altogether. Manually enriching e-commerce catalogs is impractical given their scale and rapid growth. This paper introduces TRACE, a novel framework for automated catalog attribute enrichment using agentic Large Language Models (LLMs).
