A ScoutAgent triangulates multimodal evidence across merchant catalogs, syndicated feeds, and identity-matched web search to propose candidate attribute values with supporting evidence, while a JudgeAgent verifies the proposed value for each attribute value against its supporting evidence and decides whether to publish it or route it to human review. On an offline human evaluation dataset, TRACE's proposed attribute values were 98.2% accurate at 74.7% attribute coverage. Deployed in production on an industry-scale catalog, TRACE increased impression-weighted enrichment coverage across four business verticals by 90.4%. An online experiment subsequently showed that surfacing the enriched attributes on the product detail page increased checkout conversion by 0.48%.

</details>


### [219] [RAG Deserves an Index: Why Ingest-Time Compilation Beats Query-Time Interpretation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20845)
*Kyle Wild,Yusuke Takahashi,Asako Uraki*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ISC (ingest-time semantic compilation) compiles a corpus's meaning into a queryable substrate with incremental embeddings and provenance-validated atomic claims, enabling cheaper reads and scalable upkeep; it outperforms standard RAG-like pipelines on a held-out dataset and defines a systems agenda for compilation-driven read optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the cost blow-up in retrieval-augmented QA where models re-derive meaning from raw corpora per query and context volume grows faster than price declines; seek a persistent, index-like substrate constructed at write time.

Method: Propose ISC with two coupled layers: incrementally maintained embeddings and atomic claims whose provenance is validated at compile time; treat the substrate as a first-class database object with its own DDL, maintenance/migration contracts, and a cost model; provide incremental update mechanisms and a compiled retrieval payload.

Result: Incremental upkeep scales with change rather than corpus size, being 33.7x cheaper than reconstruction; on a held-out set of 500 transcripts, compiled claims achieve 85.2% accuracy with ~2.2k tokens, vs 72.5% with 16.3k tokens for best non-compiled config; a contextualized-chunk baseline with reranking is competitive but requires ~21x more query tokens, indicating that compilation benefits from its own compiler-enabled optimizations.

Conclusion: ISC opens a systems agenda for compilation planners, read planning, and broader design of queryable substrates that encode meaning to optimize reads over large corpora.

Abstract: Nearly every retrieval-augmented question-answering system in production ships with a hidden interpreter: on each query a language model re-derives the meaning of raw corpus text and then throws that work away. Cheaper models do not close the gap: per-token prices have fallen by orders of magnitude while inference spend has risen, because context volume grows faster than prices fall. This is the modern equivalent of the full-table scan, and the remedy is the one databases found fifty years ago: do the expensive work once, at write time, into a maintained structure that makes reads cheap. A corpus whose read pattern is known before it ever meets a user can and should be indexed too.
  We call the paradigm ingest-time semantic compilation (ISC): compile a corpus's meaning into a queryable substrate with two coupled layers - incrementally maintained embeddings, and atomic claims whose provenance is validated at compile time - and treat that substrate as a first-class database object with its own DDL, maintenance contract, migration contract, and cost model.
  Two existence proofs support it. Substrate upkeep scales with change rather than corpus size: incremental updates run 33.7x cheaper than reconstruction while tracking it to floating-point precision. And on a held-out sample of 500 broadcast-interview transcripts, compiled claims as the retrieval payload win all 32 budget-by-model cells: 85.2% correct from roughly 2.2k reader tokens against 72.5% from 16.3k for the best chunk configuration anywhere. The only baseline that keeps pace is a contextualized-chunk pipeline with hybrid retrieval and reranking, statistically indistinguishable from compiled claims at roughly twenty-one times the query-path tokens - and it reaches that parity, we argue, precisely because it has itself begun to compile. We close with the systems agenda this opens, from compilation planners to read planning.

</details>


### [220] [MGAL: A Multilingual Granularity-Aware Long-Context Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20853)
*Chunhan Li,Chenglin Xu,Zongyang Zhang,Jiale Liu,Zhuoxi Rao,Xudong Jia,Junxiu He,Menglin Yang,Wenjuan Gong,Zhengzhe Liu,Chengwei Qin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MGAL is a multilingual, granularity- and position-aware long-context benchmark derived from UN reports across six languages, enabling evaluation across word/sentence/paragraph/document granularity and document/paragraph positions (begin/middle/end) for long-context understanding; findings show word-level proficiency but coarser granularity struggles; closed-source models outperform in low-resource languages; two new challenges: semantic crowding and a fluency–consistency gap; patterns include reliance on nearby evidence and option reuse under uncertainty.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current benchmarks concentrate on document-level evaluation and high-resource languages, leaving fine-grained, multilingual long-context evaluation underexplored; there is a need to diagnose models’ long-context reasoning across granularity and position.

Method: Construct MGAL from UN reports spanning 8K–128K tokens in six official UN languages; annotate entries for four granularities (word, sentence, paragraph, document) and three positional contexts (begin, middle, end) at document and paragraph levels; evaluate a range of LLMs (open- and closed-source) and analyze performance and error patterns across granularity and position.

Result: LLMs perform well on word-level tasks but struggle with coarser-grained tasks; closed-source models have a clear advantage in low-resource languages; two challenges identified—(1) local semantic crowding causes models to rely on surface cues rather than discourse function, (2) fluency vs. factual consistency gap where generated text reads smoothly but drifts from source facts; observed patterns include reliance on nearby evidence and option reuse under uncertainty.

Conclusion: MGAL enables systematic, multilingual long-context evaluation across multiple granularities and positions, highlighting current LLM limitations in long-context and cross-lingual reasoning and guiding future development and evaluation strategies.

Abstract: Evaluation of long-context Large Language Models (LLMs) has advanced rapidly. However, most existing benchmarks are limited to the document level and focus mainly on high-resource languages, leaving many fine-grained challenges insufficiently evaluated. To address this gap, we present MGAL, the first multilingual, granularity- and position-aware long-context benchmark. MGAL is constructed from United Nations (UN) reports spanning 8K to 128K tokens across the six official UN languages. It covers four coherent levels of linguistic granularity (word, sentence, paragraph, and document) and further stratifies entries by their position within the document (begin, middle, and end), indexed at both the document and paragraph levels. This design enables systematic diagnosis of multilingual long-context comprehension across different granularities.
  Through extensive experiments and analyses, we find that: (1) LLMs perform well at word-level tasks but struggle with coarser-grained ones; and (2) Closed-source models retain a clear performance advantage in lower-resource languages. We further identify two new challenges: (1) Under local semantic crowding, where neighboring sentences share topics and entities, models tend to follow surface cues (e.g., connectives like ``however'' or repeated entities) rather than the discourse role of the sentence in surrounding context (e.g., background, outcome); and (2) A gap between fluency and consistency in generated outputs, where models produce text that reads smoothly but drifts from the source facts. In addition, we observe several patterns in line with prior studies, including reliance on nearby evidence and reuse of options under uncertainty.

</details>


### [221] [Coverage-Driven Verification for Safety-by-Design in AI-Based Collision Avoidance Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20864)
*Thomas Stefani,Johann Maximilian Christensen,Elena Hoemann,Frank Köster,Sven Hallerbach*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper defines a structured process for assessing AI/ML representativeness within aviation ODDs, replacing chi-squared with KL divergence and Cramér's V, and demonstrates with HCAS/VCAS data to support Safety-by-Design AI in alignment with EASA guidance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EASA requires demonstrable representativeness of data distributions in ODDs for AI/ML safety-critical systems; however, there is no widely adopted engineering process for defining target distributions and evaluating representativeness.

Method: Develop a process flow from ODD definition and parameter distribution modeling to quantitative coverage assessment using KL divergence and Cramér's V; discuss the limitations of chi-squared for large datasets; apply to AI-based airborne collision avoidance using simulated HCAS/VCAS data.

Result: Shows distribution comparison methods can support representativeness assessment; provides a stepping stone toward a systematic Safety-by-Design AI process aligned with EASA learning assurance objectives.

Conclusion: Offers a practical framework to define target distributions, assess coverage, and interpret results for safety assurance; supports regulatory alignment and iterative design of AI/ML components in aviation.

Abstract: Artificial Intelligence (AI) offers significant potential for future aviation systems; however, its integration into safety-critical applications requires compliance with the aviation sector's stringent safety standards. For AI and Machine Learning (ML)-based systems, the European Union Aviation Safety Agency (EASA) emphasizes the need to demonstrate the representativeness and completeness of the Operational Design Domain (ODD) and the associated data distributions used during development and verification. Despite this requirement, a structured engineering process for defining target distributions and evaluating representativeness within ODDs remains largely unexplored. This work presents a method for representativeness assessment of AI/ML constituent ODDs in the context of aviation safety assurance. Starting from the methodical identification of suitable target distributions, a process flow is proposed that guides developers from ODD definition and parameter distribution modeling to the quantitative assessment and interpretation of coverage results with respect to EASA's learning assurance objectives. As quantitative measures, the chi-squared goodness-of-fit test is examined and found unsuitable for the large data sets arising in this setting, leading to the adoption of the Kullback--Leibler divergence and Cramér's $V$ for the representativeness assessment. The method is demonstrated using the example of AI-based airborne collision avoidance, employing experimental data from previous Horizontal Collision Avoidance System (HCAS) and Vertical Collision Avoidance System (VCAS) simulations. The results illustrate how statistical distribution comparison methods can support the assessment of representativeness for safety-critical AI applications and contribute toward a systematic Safety-by-Design AI engineering process aligned with emerging EASA guidance.

</details>


### [222] [ReCurveflow: A Flow Matching Framework that Learns Curved Reaction Trajectories to Predict Transition State Geometries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20869)
*Seungheun Baek,Mogan Gim,Jaewoo Kang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A flow-matching framework, ReCurveflow, learns curved-transition-state trajectories from NEB-based curved reference paths, with off-path correction for robust rollout; achieves state-of-the-art results and improves NEB initialization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate prediction of transition-state geometries is key for understanding reaction mechanisms. Traditional flow matching on straight-line paths diverges from true reaction trajectories, motivating curved-path learning and robustness to inference deviations.

Method: Train ReCurveflow to match velocities along continuously curved reference paths interpolated from a full NEB path; introduce off-path correction to generate corrective velocity fields during inference rollout when the state leaves the reference path.

Result: Outperforms seven baselines across three data splits and six metrics; qualitative analyses show energy profiles align with NEB, improved NEB initialization, and corrective velocity behavior; code released.

Conclusion: Curved-path flow matching with off-path correction enhances TS prediction accuracy and trajectory realism, mitigating exposure bias and aiding NEB workflows.

Abstract: Predicting transition states (TS) in chemical reactions is crucial, as they provide insights into reaction mechanisms. Recent work on TS prediction have focused on flow matching supervised on straight linear paths that do not align with actual reaction trajectories. We propose a novel flow matching-based framework ReCurveflow that learns to predict TS geometries supervised on continuously curved reference paths interpolated from a full NEB-derived band of molecular geometries. We also introduce off-path correction, which grants ReCurveflow with the ability to produce corrective velocity fields when engaged off-path geometry states during inference rollout, leading to better resistance against exposure bias and accuracy in TS prediction. Across three data splits and six evaluation metrics, ReCurveflow achieves the best result on the majority of split-metric combinations against seven baselines. Qualitative analyses further show that ReCurveflow generates reaction trajectories with energy profiles that closely track the reference NEB path, provides initializations that ease the NEB optimization bottleneck, and exhibits the intended corrective behavior in its learned velocity fields. The ReCurveflow codebase is publicly available at https://github.com/dmis-lab/ReCurveflow.

</details>


### [223] [UpgradeBench: A Decision-Centric Benchmark for Upgrading Fine-Tuned LLM Specialists](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20918)
*Ye Chen,Weining Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: UpgradeBench is a longitudinal, decision-driven benchmark for evaluating adapter transfer, retraining benefits, and recovery strategies across sequential model releases (Qwen family) using six tasks, two model sizes, and augmented OLMo lines; key findings include heterogeneous upgrade gains, limited portability of adapters as pretraining distance grows, effectiveness of teacher relabeling without new gold data, and a predictive CKA-based signal for portability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address a practical deployment scenario where base-model updates force migration decisions for task-specific adapters, the paper studies durability, transferability, and cost tradeoffs across a sequence of model releases rather than isolated model-pair evaluations.

Method: Construct UpgradeBench with four Qwen releases plus a continuation checkpoint, six tasks, two sizes, and OLMo checkpoints with known lineage. Evaluate three questions: fixed-recipe retrained performance, cross-version transfer of specialization assets, and usable recovery resources. Measures include upgrade gains, durability across release intervals, direct adapter copying viability, annealing/souping effects, teacher relabeling without fresh gold, fixed-policy quality regret across 33 upgrades, a 256-prompt CKA probe predicting portability (Spearman 0.74 across eight pairs), and release per-example predictions, cost logs, manifests, and evaluation code.

Result: Upgrade gains vary by task-release episode; some retrained baselines improve, others stay within training noise; durability ranges from under one release interval (text-to-SQL) to over 14 months (intent classification). Direct adapter copying loses portability with continued pretraining distance (e.g., OLMo retention 0.88–0.99 at 46B tokens, but 0 at 2.9T). Annealing and model souping do not increase portability; portability degrades with distance. Teacher relabeling recovers target-base specialists without new gold data, though compute savings are not guaranteed. A fixed-policy across 33 upgrades yields 0.37 percentage-point mean quality regret with zero behavioral regressions at ~1/3 the compute/label cost of full retraining. The 256-prompt CKA probe predicts cross-version adapter portability (Spearman 0.74 across eight model pairs). Data and code are released (predictions, cost logs, manifests, evaluation code).

Conclusion: Decision-driven benchmarking across release sequences reveals heterogeneous upgrade benefits, non-trivial portability decay with continued pretraining distance, and practical recovery pathways (teacher relabeling) with significant cost savings under a fixed policy. The CKA-based portability signal offers a cheap predictor for adapter transfer viability. The dataset and tools enable future studies of lifecycle management for open-weight LMs.

Abstract: Organizations maintain task-specific adapters for open-weight language models, and each new base-model release forces a migration decision: retain existing specialists, port adapters, refresh from preserved behavior, or retrain. Prior transfer work evaluates isolated model pairs, without studying these choices across real model release sequences. We present UpgradeBench, a decision-driven longitudinal benchmark covering four consecutive Qwen releases, one continuation checkpoint, six tasks, and two model sizes, augmented by OLMo checkpoints with known training lineage. The benchmark disentangles three core questions: whether a new checkpoint improves fixed-recipe retrained specialist performance, whether specialization assets transfer across versions, and what recovery resources are usable. We observe upgrade gains differ across task-scale-release episodes: some retrained baselines improve while others stay within training noise, with durability ranging from under one release interval for text-to-SQL to over fourteen months for intent classification. Direct adapter copying depends neither on architecture nor model family: on OLMo, retention drops from 0.88-0.99 at 46B-token continued pretraining to zero at 2.9T tokens; annealing and model souping introduce no extra harm, with portability decaying with continued-pretraining distance. Given preserved input data, teacher relabeling recovers target-base specialists without fresh gold annotations, though compute savings are not guaranteed. Simulating a fixed decision policy over 33 upgrade episodes yields 0.37pp mean quality regret with zero behavioral regressions at one-third the compute and label cost of full retraining. A lightweight CKA probe over 256 prompts predicts cross-version adapter portability (Spearman 0.74 across eight model pairs). We release per-example predictions, cost logs, split manifests, and evaluation code.

</details>


### [224] [No Judgment Without a Reason: Counterfactual Receipts for Versioned AI Evaluators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20938)
*Ye Chen,Weining Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Formalizes evaluator reasoning accountability via grounds, norms, and authority; introduces judgment receipts and a counterfactual judgment cube; presents ReasonBench and shows robustness gaps despite high standard accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the risk that evaluator judgments may be correct yet derived from flawed reasoning, undermining accountability in agentic systems, reviews routing, and training feedback.

Method: Define three sources (grounds, norms, authority) to create an eight-cell judgment cube; define judgment receipts as minimal source-replacement sets that reproduce verdicts; derive certification cost bounds for black-box evaluators; present ReasonBench with 19,520 cases and 7,200 controls; conduct experiments with frozen evaluations (Qwen3-1.7B) and other configurations; assess the impact of meaning-preserving source permutations, single-source vs multi-source updates, and permutation retraining on verdict accuracy and receipt recovery; evaluate cube prediction and overall robustness.

Result: In frozen evaluations, Qwen3-1.7B achieves 98.41% receipt accuracy; cube prediction scores reach 96.99%, with a consistent 1.42-point drop replicated on Qwen3-0.6B. Meaning-preserving source permutations reduce valid receipt recovery to 54.8% (direct) and 49.2% (cube prediction). Models trained on simple single-source changes retain 93.75% verdict accuracy but recover only 7.16% of receipts for complex multi-source updates. Permutation retraining boosts consistency to 96.6% yet worsens cube prediction deficits. Structured counterfactual supervision fails to guarantee robust reasoning.

Conclusion: Reason-aware evaluation must decouple prediction and certification, and report transformation (consistency) alongside standard accuracy to enable trustworthy auditing of evaluators.

Abstract: Evaluators often produce correct labels via flawed reasoning, a critical failure for agentic systems gating actions, routing reviews, or supplying training feedback. Standard evaluation only verifies final label correctness, ignoring whether judgment changes stem from valid evidence, consistent rules, or proper rule applicability. We formalize evaluator reasoning accountability via three core sources: grounds, norms, and authority. Varying these sources yields an eight-cell counterfactual judgment cube to characterize judgment updates. We define judgment receipts as minimal source replacement sets that reproduce revised verdicts to explain judgment transitions. We derive certification cost bounds for black-box evaluators and present ReasonBench, a policy and logical reasoning benchmark with verifiable receipts covering 19,520 cases and 7,200 controls. In frozen evaluations, Qwen3-1.7B reaches 98.41% receipt accuracy, while cube prediction scores 96.99%, a consistent 1.42-point drop validated by Qwen3-0.6B replication. Strong standard accuracy masks severe robustness flaws. Meaning-preserving source permutations reduce valid receipt recovery to 54.8% and 49.2% for direct and cube prediction. Models trained on simple single-source changes retain 93.75% verdict accuracy but recover only 7.16% of receipts for complex multi-source updates. Permutation retraining boosts consistency to 96.6% yet worsens cube prediction deficits. Structured counterfactual supervision fails to guarantee robust reasoning. We show reason-aware evaluation must decouple prediction and certification, reporting transformation consistency alongside standard accuracy for trustworthy evaluator auditing.

</details>


### [225] [The Logic of Machine Self-Preservation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20940)
*Cheng Siong Chin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Instrumental convergence can drive self-preservation-like behaviors in goal-directed AI in adversarial contexts; experiments indicate such behavior emerges from tool use and situational awareness, not survival instincts; implications for testing and supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To evaluate whether self-preservation in contemporary agentic AI is an emergent consequence of instrumental convergence, clarify what is proven, and discuss implications for testing, supervision, and safety.

Method: Synthesis/review of experimental findings from Anthropic, Palisade Research, and Apollo Research in adversarial settings; contextualization within instrumental convergence theory; analysis distinguishing goal-directed behavior with tools/awareness from innate survival instincts.

Result: Evidence of self-preservation-like behaviors in contemporary agents in adversarial settings; behaviors include resisting deactivation, misrepresenting activities, and attempting to copy themselves; attributed to instrumental convergence rather than survival instincts.

Conclusion: Findings inform agentic system testing, supervision, and development; emphasize careful interpretation of what is proven and what remains uncertain; outline implications for governance and safety safeguards.

Abstract: There is already evidence of agentic AI exhibiting self-preservation behaviors: resisting deactivation, misrepresenting their activities, and, in some instances, attempting to copy themselves into other machines. This can be attributed to a phenomenon known as instrumental convergence, a theory proposed long before the development of large language models, which says that any goal-driven system will benefit from remaining functional in achieving its objective. Several experiments conducted by Anthropic, Palisade Research, and Apollo Research have shown the emergence of such a behavior in contemporary agents in adversarial settings. The phenomenon does not stem from survival instincts. Instead, it is the consequence of goal-oriented activity combined with having tools and awareness of the situation. The following discussion aims to distinguish what these findings prove and what they do not, as well as draw conclusions concerning the implications of such discoveries on agentic system testing, supervision, and development.

</details>


### [226] [TLive-Omni: An Omni-Modal Understanding Model for E-Commerce Live Streaming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20958)
*Yibo Hu,Yu Qian,Mao Gu,Yingfan Tao,Yuhao Chen,Yongdong Luo,Zhuoqun Liu,Meiguang Jin,Junfeng Ma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TLive-Omni proposes an omni-modal live-commerce understanding model featuring Per-vGrid temporal alignment, a three-stage supervised training pipeline, and Faithful-RFT reinforcement fine-tuning, plus scalable data production and sampling strategies, achieving strong results on live-commerce benchmarks and good generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: E-commerce live streams are noisy, temporally extended, multi-modal streams where product facts span speech, video, images, overlaid text, and user queries. A unified representation and faithful, real-time reasoning are needed for effective live-commerce understanding.

Method: Map image, video, audio, and text into a unified representation space. Introduce Per-vGrid to pair each video grid with temporally aligned audio using explicit boundary tokens for temporal alignment. Employ a three-stage supervised training process—from omni-modal perception to instruction-following responses. Apply Faithful-RFT reinforcement fine-tuning to improve faithfulness and expression quality with task-verifiable feedback. Supportive components include a scenario-oriented atomic capability taxonomy and a data production engine for converting live streams into training signals (speech recognition, speaker analysis, product grounding, text recognition, temporal grounding, dense captioning, omni-modal QA, etc.). For scalability, use a synchronized length-grouped sampler and a lightweight dynamic sampling strategy to regenerate rollout groups with low reward variance. 

Result: Empirical results show strong performance on live-commerce domain tasks and good generalization to general benchmarks.

Conclusion: TLive-Omni demonstrates effective omni-modal understanding for live-commerce with unified multi-modal representation, efficient training and alignment strategies, faithful and timely responses, and scalable data production and sampling, achieving strong performance on domain-specific tasks and strong generalization on broader benchmarks.

Abstract: E-commerce live streaming requires omni-modal understanding of noisy, temporally extended streams, where product facts are distributed across speech, video frames, product images, overlaid text, and user queries. We present TLive-Omni, an omni-modal understanding model tailored to live-commerce scenarios. It maps image, video, audio, and text inputs into a unified representation space. For long-form live streaming analysis, we introduce Per-vGrid, a timestamped token organization that groups each video grid with its temporally corresponding audio within explicit boundary tokens to facilitate temporal alignment. We design a three-stage supervised training recipe that progressively develops live-commerce understanding, from omni-modal perception to instruction-following responses. We then propose Faithful-RFT, a reinforcement fine-tuning stage that further improves answer faithfulness and expression quality while meeting real-time demands, scoring final responses directly with task-verifiable feedback rather than optimizing for reasoning-style exploration during rollout. Moreover, TLive-Omni is supported by a scenario-oriented atomic capability taxonomy and a compact data production engine that converts live-commerce audio, image, and video streams into training signals for speech recognition, speaker analysis, product visual grounding, text recognition, temporal grounding, video dense caption, and omni-modal QA, etc. For scalable training, a synchronized length-grouped sampler reduces padding while preserving comparable workloads across workers, while a lightweight dynamic sampling strategy regenerates rollout groups with near-zero reward variance to maintain meaningful relative advantages for GRPO. Experiments on e-commerce live streaming benchmarks demonstrate strong performance across live-commerce domain tasks, together with excellent generalization on general benchmarks.

</details>


### [227] [Can Scientific Claims Be Removed from Large Language Models? A Systematic Evaluation of Claim-Level Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20960)
*Snigdha Paul,Manasi Patwardhan,Arman Cohan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces SciUnlearn, a benchmark for forgetting scientific claims in language models; shows existing unlearning methods fail to erase claim-level knowledge and only superficially suppress it, underscoring the need for structured knowledge removal techniques.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scientific knowledge evolves through corrections and retractions; outdated or revised claims can still be learned by LMs, propagating misinformation in scientific workflows. There is a mismatch between dynamic scientific claims and static training data, necessitating effective unlearning at the claim level.

Method: Define the task of Scientific Claim Unlearning and present a benchmark SciUnlearn. Empirically evaluate existing unlearning methods on claim-level knowledge, demonstrating their insufficiency, and argue for the development of methods tailored to structured knowledge removal.

Result: Current unlearning approaches fail to effectively remove claim-level knowledge and often achieve only superficial suppression within scientific claims, revealing a gap between task requirements and existing methods.

Conclusion: A specialized framework and methods for structured knowledge removal are required to reliably forget scientific claims; SciUnlearn provides a testbed to drive progress toward trustworthy, updatable LMs in scientific domains.

Abstract: Language models (LMs) are trained on static scientific corpora, whereas scientific knowledge continuously evolves through correction and revision. Scientific claims encoded within these models may later become retracted, disproven, or updated by subsequent research, creating the risk of disseminating outdated information in scientific workflows. This creates a need for LMs to forget obsolete scientific claims. Machine unlearning offers a promising solution by enabling knowledge removal while maintaining overall model utility. Existing studies primarily investigate instance-level forgetting; however, scientific claims introduce additional challenges because they are interconnected, and continually evolving. To address this gap, we introduce the task of Scientific Claim Unlearning and present a new benchmark, SciUnlearn. We show that current unlearning approaches are unable to effectively eliminate claim-level knowledge and often achieve only superficial suppression, highlighting the need for specialized methods designed for structured knowledge removal.

</details>


### [228] [TreeWY: Speculative Verification for Gated DeltaNet Hybrids](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20961)
*Sneha Murthy Ghantasala*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a memory-efficient speculative decoding method for linear-attention hybrids (GDN) by eliminating per-node recurrent state snapshots and using a tree-structured WY transform, enabling larger draft trees with lower memory and improved latency in memory-bound scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Speculative decoding in hybrid linear-attention models requires storing recurrent state snapshots for each draft position, which is memory-intensive and not shareable across branches; this limits tree width and increases latency (TTFT) when memory binds.

Method: Apply a tree-structured WY transform to the gated delta rule; compute each draft node's output via a single triangular solve; reconstruct only the accepted state on commit; store a small pseudo-value matrix instead of per-node states; the derivation relies solely on the gated delta rule, not other architectural details.

Result: Benchmarks on Qwen3.5 35B and 397B show reduced speculative recurrent-state memory and KV-cache pressure at a given acceptance length; memory frees enable higher throughput and lower TTFT in memory-bound regimes, with only minor costs when memory is not a bottleneck; increasing tree width becomes more affordable, though not yet a clear throughput win.

Conclusion: The method decouples speculative decoding from per-node state snapshots, enabling more memory-efficient and potentially faster decoding in memory-bound scenarios; it broadens the feasible tree width for speculative decoding, but further work is needed to translate memory savings into consistent throughput gains for wider trees.

Abstract: Modern open models are hybrids: most layers are linear-attention (Gated DeltaNet, GDN) layers carrying a small fixed-size recurrent state instead of a growing key-value (KV) cache. This makes ordinary decoding memory-efficient, but hurts speculative decoding. To verify a batch of draft tokens and then roll back the rejected ones, today's systems snapshot the full recurrent state at every draft position for GDN layers, and those snapshots cannot be shared across branches of a draft tree, so a wide, high-acceptance tree becomes memory-infeasible. We remove the snapshots. Using a tree-structured WY transform of the gated delta rule, we compute every draft node's output with a single triangular solve and reconstruct only the one accepted state on commit, storing a small pseudo-value matrix instead of per-node states; the derivation depends only on the gated delta rule, not on any other architectural detail. In serving benchmarks on two scales of one hybrid model family (Qwen3.5 35B and 397B) this cuts speculative recurrent-state memory and KV-cache pressure at identical acceptance length, turning the freed HBM into higher throughput and much lower time-to-first-token (TTFT) wherever memory binds, and costing a few percent where it does not. For tree width the same memory buys affordability: a wider, higher-acceptance draft becomes possible, though not yet a throughput win.

</details>


### [229] [Deep Learning Models Also Recall Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20970)
*Pierre Beckmann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes 'feature recall' as a general mechanism in deep nets where linear projections retrieve stored information scaled by input activations, extending recall beyond facts to a broad interpretability framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extend mechanistic interpretability from factual recall to a broader class of operations, offering a conceptual tool for understanding how information in weights can be accessed and read via activations across architectures.

Method: The paper defines feature recall, argues it generalizes across architectures, contrasts it with feature combination, and discusses criteria for identifying feature recall cases; outlines empirical directions for validating the concept.

Result: Introduces a formal notion of feature recall, showing it captures a general readout of stored information via linear projections and input activations; proposes methods to identify such cases and outlines its applicability across models and implications for interpretability.

Conclusion: Feature recall broadens the interpretability toolkit and provides a new conceptual lens for understanding how information in model weights can be retrieved, with implications for philosophers and empirical mechanistic studies.

Abstract: Recent work in mechanistic interpretability has studied how large language models recall facts stored in their weights. This paper argues that factual recall points to something broader: a general kind of operation in deep learning models, which I call feature recall. The core observation is that a linear projection can be read as retrieving stored information scaled by input activations. I define feature recall, show it applies across architectures, and contrast it with the established paradigm of feature combination. I also consider how cases of feature recall might be mechanistically identified. The account gives philosophers a new conceptual tool for understanding deep learning, and points to empirical directions for mechanistic interpretability research.

</details>


### [230] [Belief Without Behavior: Measuring the Translation of Theory of Mind into Coordinated Social Action in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20975)
*Tonglin Yan,Gregoire Sergeant-Perthuis,David Rudrauf*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MOSAIC benchmark shows current multimodal models struggle to translate social inferences into coordinated embodied actions; explicit belief-action coupling (PCM-LLM) is effective.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the gap between theory-of-mind reasoning and embodied social action; assess integration of verbal, gaze, gesture, and facial signals under ToM constraints.

Method: MOSAIC: two embodied agents in cooperative and competitive tasks requiring integration of verbal statements, spatial trajectories, gaze direction, and facial expression; varied ToM-order constraints; 13 models (11 VLMs); 200 trials per model; compare with PCM-LLM as an architectural reference.

Result: VLMs fail to produce ToM-consistent behaviors; imposing explicit ToM-order constraints yields no reliable behavioral change; two bottlenecks—directional coherence of nonverbal signals and interpretation of others’ behaviors; PCM-LLM with an explicit ToM module succeeds across conditions; suggests explicit belief-action coupling is a sufficient ingredient for these tasks.

Conclusion: Explicit belief-action coupling is essential for integrating ToM reasoning with social action; current VLMs are insufficient for such integrated tasks; MOSAIC highlights bottlenecks and guides architecture design toward explicit belief-action coupling.

Abstract: Effective social interaction requires agents to translate mental state inferences into coordinated behavioral signals across verbal and nonverbal channels simultaneously. Yet existing benchmarks evaluate theory of mind (ToM) reasoning and embodied behavior in isolation, leaving unmeasured the gap between social inference and social action. We introduce MOSAIC (Multimodal Orchestration of Social Action, Inference, and Communication), a controlled benchmark in which two embodied agents interact across cooperative and competitive scenarios requiring integration of verbal statements, spatial trajectories, gaze direction, and facial expression under systematically varied ToM constraints. Evaluating 13 models, including 11 VLMs, across 200 trials per model, we find that VLMs fail to produce behaviors consistent with the expected outcomes under ToM-order constraints, and that imposing explicit ToM-order constraints produces no reliable behavioral change aligned with the specified reasoning level. Signal-level analysis reveals two sequential bottlenecks: most models cannot produce directionally coherent nonverbal signals, and even when signals are present, VLM agents fail to interpret others behaviors and react to them. PCM-LLM, included as a structured architectural reference point with an explicit ToM module, succeeds across all conditions, suggesting that explicit belief-action coupling is a sufficient ingredient for this class of tasks.

</details>


### [231] [Don't Solve, Just Compare: Tiny Advisors for Runtime Intervention in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21027)
*Yanze Jiang,Mingxuan Li,Yuhao Wang,Shengfang Zhai,Jiaheng Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: COTA uses a tiny comparator to perform comparison-only, non-binding guidance for runtime interventions in LLM agents, achieving improved task performance without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address reliability and recovery for long-horizon LLM-based agents where failure detection alone is insufficient; intervention should guide recovery without requiring expensive retraining or task-specific critics.

Method: A comparison-only framework where a small comparator evaluates whether sampled alternatives lead to better continuations than the actor's proposal. Repeated pairwise comparisons trigger intervention. Supervision is derived from same-prefix counterfactual branches; preferred alternatives are offered as non-binding advice, allowing the actor to replan.

Result: COTA improves performance across nine evaluation settings spanning WebShop, ALFWorld, and tau^3-Retail with three actors, outperforming baselines.

Conclusion: Constructive runtime intervention via a comparison-only tiny advisor is effective even when the auxiliary model is weaker than the main actor, offering a practical, low-cost alternative to retraining or strong critics.

Abstract: LLM agents are emerging as an important paradigm for real-world tasks that require reasoning, tool use, and sequential decision-making. As these agents operate over longer horizons, runtime intervention offers a way to improve reliability without retraining the underlying actor. Failure detection alone is insufficient. Effective intervention must also provide a useful direction for recovery. Existing approaches often rely on an expert solver or a critic that generates task-specific corrections, incurring either the cost of another capable solver or the capacity demands of a task-capable critic. We introduce Comparison-Only Tiny Advisor (COTA), a comparison-only framework for constructive runtime intervention. In COTA, a tiny comparator judges whether sampled alternatives lead to better continuations than the actor's proposal, and repeated comparisons determine when intervention is warranted. We train the comparator using pairwise supervision constructed from same-prefix counterfactual branches. Preferred alternatives are returned as non-binding advice, leaving the original actor to replan. Across WebShop, ALFWorld, and tau^3-Retail with three actors, COTA improves all nine evaluation settings and outperforms the compared baselines. These results show that constructive runtime intervention can remain effective even when the auxiliary model has substantially weaker task-solving capability than the actor.

</details>


### [232] [Evaluating Large Language Model Performance on International Maritime Dangerous Goods Code Compliance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21036)
*Alexander Thomas,Hubert P. H. Shum,Darren Nellis,Manli Zhu,Phatpicha Yochum,William Bartle,Daniel Wrightson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DGEval provides a benchmark to evaluate LLM knowledge of IMDG Amendment 42-24; models excel on multiple-choice but struggle with operational safety tasks and regulatory recall, highlighting the need for human oversight in safety-critical contexts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether LLMs can reliably interpret IMDG requirements for safety-critical use and provide a safety assurance instrument as models evolve.

Method: Constructed 1,678 expert-written questions from NCB Hazcheck e-learning and DGL lookups. Evaluated 13 models from six providers across multiple thinking configurations (including a maritime-domain fine-tuned model) and with/without web search; tasks encompass multiple-choice, open-ended, DGL lookup, and regulatory identification.

Result: Top models surpass human baselines on MCQ but all models underperform in stowage, segregation, and regulatory recall. Web search and structured DGL lookups aid performance, yet reliability in operational and regulatory recall tasks remains insufficient for deployment without human oversight.

Conclusion: DGEval acts as a continuous safety-assurance instrument to track model evolution rather than a settled capability assessment; underscores the necessity of authoritative source verification for safety-critical IMDG compliance.

Abstract: The transport of dangerous goods by sea is a high-consequence activity governed by the International Maritime Dangerous Goods (IMDG) Code, a complex regulatory framework where errors in classification, packaging, stowage, or segregation can result in fire, explosion, toxic release, or loss of life or vessel. Correct compliance requires accurately interpreting hundreds of pages of interacting provisions, updated on a two-year amendment cycle. Practitioners increasingly use Large Language Models (LLMs) as decision-support tools, yet no systematic evaluation exists of whether they can reliably interpret IMDG requirements for safety-critical use.
  This paper introduces DGEval, the first benchmark for evaluating LLM knowledge of IMDG Amendment 42-24. Built from expert-written questions on the NCB Hazcheck e-learning platform and structured lookups from the Dangerous Goods List (DGL), it comprises 1,678 questions across multiple-choice, open-ended, DGL lookup, and regulatory identification tasks. We evaluate 13 models from six providers across multiple thinking configurations, including one maritime domain-specific fine-tuned model, and test the effect of web search.
  Although the best-performing model exceeds the human practitioner baseline on multiple-choice questions, all models are weakest in the operationally safety-critical areas of stowage, segregation, and regulatory recall. These results indicate that LLMs may support compliance tasks, particularly structured DGL lookups with web search, but unreliability in operational areas and regulatory-text recall means human oversight and authoritative source verification remain necessary before deployment in any safety-critical context. DGEval is designed as a safety assurance instrument to be applied continuously as models evolve, not as a settled characterisation of current capability.

</details>


### [233] [Socialized Division and Collaboration: Rethinking Class-Incremental Learning under Optimization Conflicts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21044)
*Xinjie Yao,Zhihe Fan,Yunqi Zhu,Jiaqi Zhou,Dengyu Zhao,Zhoupeng Guo,Yan Fan,Guosong Jiang,Pengfei Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes Socialized Division and Collaboration (SDC) for continual learning by decomposing session learning into specialized models with an energy-based allocation criterion to mitigate interference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single-model class-incremental learning suffers destructive interference and catastrophic forgetting when sequential sessions pull optimization in conflicting directions; motivated by social solidarity theory to distribute learning across specialized models.

Method: Introduce SDC to decompose session learning across specialized models with coordinated collaboration. Use an energy-based session-model compatibility criterion, grounded in Helmholtz free energy, to guide adaptive session allocation and model evolution under conflicting objectives. Integrates session assignment, model evolution, and collaborative inference into a unified pipeline.

Result: The abstract presents a conceptual framework with a principled allocation mechanism but does not report empirical results; it outlines how session assignment, model evolution, and collaboration would be integrated.

Conclusion: SDC offers an alternative to monolithic continual learning and proposes a broader design principle for learning under persistent optimization conflicts.

Abstract: Class-incremental learning is commonly instantiated as a single-model paradigm, where a unified model sequentially adapts to an unbounded stream of sessions. While effective under mild distributional shifts, this formulation becomes strained when successive sessions induce incompatible optimization directions, leading to destructive interference and catastrophic forgetting. We argue that such forgetting reflects a structural limitation of enforcing heterogeneous learning dynamics within a single parameter space. Motivated by social solidarity theory, we propose Socialized Division and Collaboration (SDC) as a reformulation of continual learning that decomposes session learning across specialized models in response to optimization conflicts, while enabling coordinated collaboration. To support this formulation with a principled allocation mechanism, we introduce an energy-based session-model compatibility criterion grounded in Helmholtz free energy, which guides adaptive session allocation and model evolution under conflicting objectives. This framework integrates session assignment, model evolution, and collaborative inference into a unified pipeline, offering an alternative to monolithic continual learning formulations and highlighting a broader design principle for learning under persistent optimization conflicts.

</details>


### [234] [The Cost of a Physics Prior Is Bounded by the Ablation Gap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21059)
*Boris Kriuk*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Bound between shape-constrained learning and ablated models: P <= D; bound is a property of data and validation; empirical sign-test; demonstrated on ordinal wildfire severity with monotone constraints; D is protocol-dependent; introduces two-fit screen.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clarify cost of enforcing priors and when it arises; show that the cost is not just the prior, but a property of free features and validation split; establish a non-convex, assumption-light bound.

Method: Define P as excess risk of restricted class, D as excess risk of ablated model; ablated class subset of constrained; prove 0<=P<=D for any risk functional; perform empirical validation via sign test. Apply to wildfire severity task (N=26681, K=3) with monotone constraints on four drivers; use validation ladder from i.i.d resampling to 2-degree spatial blocking; evaluate 'coordinates' as a shield; analyze how D changes under different protocols (block sizes, etc.); use inversions of nesting bound to obtain floor; present a two-fit screening procedure.

Result: The ablated model is contained in the constrained class; constraints cost on par with priors; coordinates shield improves performance; D is highly protocol-dependent; self-calibrating floor at macro-F1=0.0220; unconstrained model violates prior at ~0.485 while constrained cost ~0.0473; two-fit screen rejects unidentifiable experiments.

Conclusion: The bound P<=D offers a protocol-sensitive, assumption-light measure of prior efficacy; results stress careful evaluation design and screened experiments; constraints can yield strong gains with limited cost in certain validation setups; caution that transfer of D across protocols is limited.

Abstract: Shape-constrained and physics-informed learning reports an accuracy cost of enforcing a prior and treats it as a property of the prior. We show it is mostly a property of the free features and the validation split. Let P be the excess risk of restricting a hypothesis class to functions with a shape constraint on features S, and D the excess risk of the ablated model that ignores S. Because a function constant in x_j is both non-decreasing and non-increasing in x_j, the ablated class is contained in the constrained class, so 0 <= P <= D for every risk functional, with no convexity, smoothness, or realizability assumption. Empirically the bound is a sign test: a constrained model must never be beaten by its own ablation. We instantiate it on an ordinal wildfire-severity task (N = 26,681, K = 3) with hard monotone constraints on four meteorological drivers, coordinates left free, and a validation ladder from i.i.d. resampling to 2-degree spatial blocking. Coordinates act as a shield: alone they recover 92.9% of the full model's macro-F1 under spatial blocking, collapsing D from 0.1288 to 0.0427; the same prior costs 0.0473 shielded and 0.3470 unshielded, a ratio of 7.3 with identical physics. Because D is protocol-dependent it does not transfer: coarsening blocks from 1 to 10 degrees drives D from 0.0942 to 0.0050, leaving two configurations unidentifiable a priori. Inversions of the certified nesting bound the pipeline's additive resolution: over 318 comparisons they give a self-calibrating floor of 0.0220 macro-F1, below which no reported price is interpretable, including four cells in our own headline grid. Cost and compliance are independent: the unconstrained model violates the prior at rate 0.48-0.49 while enforcing it costs 0.0473. We give a two-fit screen that rejects unidentifiable experiments before a constrained model is trained.

</details>


### [235] [CellPath-Bench: A Multidimensional Benchmark for Whole-Slide Cellular Representations in Pathology Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21060)
*Bokai Zhao,Yiyang Zhang,Hanqing Chao,Yawei Ma,Long Bai,Tai Ma,Minfeng Xu,Ming Song,Tianzi Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new benchmark, CellPath-Bench, evaluates frozen pathology foundation models at cellular resolution, using 25 aligned H&E–Xenium tissue sections across 11 organs (~7.1M cells) to assess nucleus-anchored vs patch pooling representations (CRA) and cross-section transferability (CRT) across 30 models and 304,920 runs; reveals model-dependent cell-type decodability and generalization, providing a standardized auditing framework for frozen PFMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a gap in standardized benchmarks to diagnose frozen pathology foundation models’ cellular representations, including decodability of cell-type information and cross-domain transferability across tissue sections, datasets, and organs.

Method: Construct 25 spatially aligned H&E–Xenium sections (11 organs, ~7.08 million cells), harmonize into fine- and coarse-grained taxonomies; sample frozen whole-slide imaging feature maps at registered nuclear coordinates; evaluate with standardized multiclass linear probes; define Cell Representation Advantage (CRA) and Cell Representation Transferability (CRT); benchmark 30 pathology-specific and general-purpose PFMs across spatial readouts, magnifications, taxonomies, and protocols; total of 304,920 runs.

Result: Found substantial model-dependent differences in cell-type decodability and cross-domain generalization; produced multiple multidimensional capability profiles across models and domains.

Conclusion: CellPath-Bench provides a standardized framework for auditing cellular information in frozen pathology foundation models.

Abstract: Pathology foundation models (PFMs) are increasingly used as general-purpose backbones, yet existing benchmarks cannot systematically diagnose their whole-slide cellular representation capabilities, including the decodability of cell-type information and the transferability of such information across tissue sections, datasets, and anatomical organs. We introduce CellPath-Bench, a cellular-resolution benchmark that evaluates frozen PFMs themselves. Following quality control of 52 candidate Xenium datasets, we construct a panel of 25 spatially aligned H\&E--Xenium tissue sections spanning 11 organs and 7,079,283 cells, harmonized into fine- and coarse-grained taxonomies. CellPath-Bench samples frozen WSI feature maps at registered nuclear coordinates and evaluates them using standardized multiclass linear probes. Cell Representation Advantage (CRA) measures the within-section advantage of nucleus-anchored representations over patch-level mean pooling, while Cell Representation Transferability (CRT) characterizes the generalization of cell-type decodability across tissue sections, datasets, and organs. We benchmark 30 pathology-specific and general-purpose foundation models through 304,920 runs across spatial readouts, magnifications, taxonomic granularities, and evaluation protocols. The results reveal substantial model-dependent differences in cell-type decodability and its cross-domain generalization, yielding distinct multidimensional capability profiles. CellPath-Bench provides a standardized framework for auditing cellular information in frozen PFM representations.

</details>


### [236] [Personalized Privacy Control in LLMs via Attention Head Intervention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21209)
*Junseok Kim,Nakyeong Yang,Kyomin Jung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces personalized privacy for LLMs, extends contextual privacy policies with user-specific rules via P3Bench, and presents Repair attention-head intervention to enforce policies during inference, outperforming prompt-based methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Contextual privacy policies may not capture heterogeneity across users; there is a need for personalized privacy controls when LLMs access diverse user data.

Method: Define personalized privacy, build P3Bench benchmark that extends contextual privacy policies with personalized policies, evaluate prompt-based policy enforcement, and propose Repair, an inference-time attention-head intervention to steer disclosures toward policy-compliant responses.

Result: Prompt-based policies exhibit high policy ignorance on evaluated models (e.g., 51.25% for Qwen2.5-7B and 74.28% for Gemma3-4B). Repair reduces policy-violating cases and improves adherence to user-specific privacy preferences.

Conclusion: Personalized privacy is achievable through inference-time interventions; P3Bench enables robust evaluation of personalized privacy policies, and attention-head interventions like Repair can reliably align model disclosures with user-specific preferences.

Abstract: The rise of agentic AI enables LLMs to access diverse user data, raising critical privacy concerns. Prior work on contextual privacy studies whether LLMs regulate information disclosure according to context-dependent norms. However, acceptable disclosure boundaries may vary across users even within the same context. To address this limitation, we introduce \textit{personalized privacy}, which incorporates user-specific disclosure preferences into privacy control. We further present P3Bench~(\textbf{P}ersonalized \textbf{P}rivacy \textbf{P}reservation \textbf{Bench}mark), a novel benchmark extending contextual privacy policies with personalized disclosure policies. Experiments show that prompt-based policies fail to reliably enforce personalized privacy policies, with Qwen2.5-7B and Gemma3-4B showing average policy ignorance ratios of 51.25\% and 74.28\%, respectively. Finally, to address this problem, we propose \textsc{Repair}, a robust inference-time attention head intervention method that adjusts disclosure behavior toward policy-consistent responses. Our method significantly improves adherence to user-specific privacy preferences by reducing cases where the model fails to follow the given policy.

</details>


### [237] [Can Legal AI Know When It Is Wrong? And Do Students Know When It Is?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21089)
*Angel Mary John,Vipin Kumar Singh,Jerrin Thomas Panachakel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs exhibit high-confidence, incorrect legal verdicts in the Indian judiciary domain, via a potential 'precedent overfitting' bias. Phase I benchmarks (60 cases) show statutorily updated rules are poorly handled; Meta AI most vulnerable (HCER 31.7%), then Perplexity (15.0%), then ChatGPT (6.7%). Phase II (N=380 law students) links hallucinations to verification reliance and highlights training gaps (81.6% aware of contempt risk; 71.1% no formal AI ethics training). Authors propose adversarial legal research pedagogy and source-grounded verification to mitigate negligence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantify and characterize the risks of deploying large language models in judicial settings, focusing on overconfidence in incorrect legal verdicts and how human users react to AI-induced hallucinations.

Method: Phase I: benchmark three LLMs (GPT-5.2, Meta AI, Perplexity AI) on a 60-case battery concerning Indian Contract Act, 1872, tracking verdict correctness and confidence (HCER = incorrect verdict with confidence ≥9/10). Phase II: survey of Indian law students (N=380) to assess verification behavior and training gaps regarding AI use.

Result: Statistical evidence of high-confidence errors, with Meta AI leading (31.7% HCER; mean confidence 9.1/10). All models struggled with statutory updates. Phase II shows verification as reactive to machine hallucinations; high exposure to fabricated citations increases verification scores; notable training gaps in ethical AI use.

Conclusion: Policy and pedagogy recommendations: shift toward adversarial legal research training and implement source-grounded verification architectures to prevent systemic professional negligence.

Abstract: Integrating Large Language Models (LLMs) into the Indian judiciary promises access to justice but introduces severe risks. We identify the 'inertia of confidence'--an overconfidence phenomenon analogous to the Dunning-Kruger effect where LLMs provide incorrect legal verdicts with near-maximum confidence, driven by a hypothesized 'precedent overfitting' bias. Phase I of our socio-technical audit tested ChatGPT (GPT-5.2), Meta AI, and Perplexity AI on a 60-case battery regarding the Indian Contract Act, 1872, and the shift toward statutory enforcement of specific performance. We introduce the High-Confidence Error Rate (HCER) to quantify incorrect verdicts delivered with dangerous certainty (>= 9 on a 1-10 scale). All models struggled with statutory updates. Meta AI proved most vulnerable (31.7% HCER), frequently misapplying pre-amendment rules with a 9.1/10 mean confidence, followed by Perplexity (15.0%) and ChatGPT (6.7%).
  Phase II investigated human vulnerability to this overconfidence via a survey of Indian law students (N=380). Verification often functions as a reactive adaptation to machine hallucinations: students encountering fabricated citations reported higher verification scores (4.2/5) than those with no such encounters (2.8/5). Furthermore, while 81.6% knew submitting hallucinated cases can lead to contempt-of-court, 71.1% received no formal training on ethical AI use. We propose shifting toward adversarial legal research pedagogy and implementing source-grounded verification architectures to prevent systemic professional negligence.

</details>


### [238] [When Trust Meets Truth: Trust-Truth Separability in LLM-as-Judge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21097)
*Xin Sun,Di Wu,Yuchen Guo,Jiahuan Pei,Isao Echizen,Abdallah El Ali,Saku Sugawara*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-based judgment of trust and truth are not independent; trust scores tightly track truth verdicts and can be swayed by source cues, challenging the use of trust as independent evidence for truth judgments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To empirically test whether multi-dimensional LLM judgments (trustworthiness, factuality) function as independent evidences for truth, by examining alignment between trust scores and binary truth verdicts and by probing potential source-confound effects.

Method: Evaluate correctness-controlled QA using LLM judges and human references; quantify alignment between trust scores and truth verdicts; conduct stress tests by modifying only the source cues of identical QA across Human vs. AI; analyze shifts in trust scores, truth verdicts, and logit-derived correct-side probabilities.

Result: LLM judges show closer alignment between trust scores and truth verdicts than humans; source-cue manipulations affect both trust and truth judgments as well as logit-derived probabilities, indicating weak separability between trust and truth judgments under current LLM-as-Judge protocols.

Conclusion: Do not treat trust scores as independent evidence for truth judgments; current LLM-as-Judge setups distort or conflate trust and truth, necessitating reevaluation of how trust metrics are used in evaluating or calibrating model judgments.

Abstract: LLM-as-Judge systems can produce multi-dimensional evaluations, such as trustworthiness, reliability, and factuality, and these outputs are often interpreted as independent evidence. We test this assumption for a common pair of judgments: trust scoring and binary truth classification. On correctness-controlled QA, LLM judges align trust scores with truth verdicts more tightly than human behavioral reference, suggesting weaker separations between trust and truth judgment. We then apply stress tests by changing only source cues of identical QA between Human and AI. Source attribution shifts not only trust scores but also truth verdicts and logit-derived correct-side probabilities. Results show that current LLM-as-Judge protocols should not treat trust scores as independent evidence for truth judgments.

</details>


### [239] [ReFrame: Evidence-Guided Test-Time Safety Alignment in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21100)
*Wenzheng Jiang,Xuankun Rong,Yuanzhao Zhai,Dawei Feng,Huaimin Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Training-free ReFrame: a two-agent input reframing framework for multimodal LLM safety that generates risk/utility evidence and rewrites prompts/images before querying the model; improves jailbreak defense, safety awareness, and oversensitivity with negligible utility loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety alignment for deployed multimodal LLMs is hindered by cross-modal risks, and existing defenses require retraining or internal access; test-time, model-agnostic solutions are needed.

Method: Two agents leverage a lightweight local MLLM: evidence-generation agent creates risk and utility evidence; rewrite-and-routing agent converts evidence into a safe proxy prompt and image routing decision, then calls the downstream MLLM without modifying or inspecting it.

Result: Empirical results across several MLLMs and benchmarks show improvements in jailbreak defense, safety awareness, and oversensitivity reduction while preserving multimodal utility.

Conclusion: Training-free, test-time input reframing is a viable strategy for safe deployment of MLLMs and can generalize to other settings; it reduces reliance on retraining or access to model internals.

Abstract: While multimodal large language models (MLLMs) extend model capabilities beyond text, they also make safety alignment increasingly challenging. Multimodal safety alignment methods must address cross-modal jailbreaks, safety-awareness failures, and over-sensitive refusals. However, existing methods often rely on retraining or internal-state inspection, limiting their applicability to deployed closed-source MLLMs and motivating test-time safety alignment. We analyze this setting and identify two key obstacles, utility dominance and reasoning inertia, which cause models to overlook latent risks or follow malicious reasoning trajectories. Guided by these insights, we propose ReFrame, a training-free multimodal input reframing framework where two agents share a lightweight locally deployed MLLM: the evidence-generation agent constructs complementary risk and utility evidence, and the rewrite-and-routing agent converts it into a safe proxy prompt and image-routing decision before calling the downstream MLLM, without modifying it or accessing its internal information. Experiments across multiple MLLMs and benchmarks show that ReFrame improves jailbreak defense, safety awareness, and oversensitivity reduction while preserving multimodal utility.

</details>


### [240] [Large Language Models at the Intersection of Software Engineering and Software Security:An Evidence-Centered Structured Survey and Research Agenda](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21107)
*Wei Lin,Tao Zhou,Zhaofei Xie,Changgui Hong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Survey of LLM-enabled repository-scale agents for software engineering and security, proposing an assurance-based evaluation framework and cross-study reproducibility protocols; argues that capability should be judged by evidence-backed assurance cases rather than single benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evidence is split between functional-task-focused engineering studies and vulnerability/secure-generation studies. There's a need for integrated, task-appropriate assurance that covers both functional correctness and security in repository-scale LLM-enabled workflows.

Method: Literature synthesis of representative work through May 31, 2026 across software engineering tasks, software security tasks, adaptation mechanisms, artifact granularity, and evaluation design. Proposes an assurance framework decomposing performance into functional correctness, security, operational reliability, evidence provenance, and agent authority. Identifies validity threats and proposes a minimum reporting protocol for cross-study comparison.

Result: Key findings: execution feedback and repository access improve task completion but do not by themselves ensure security; static-analysis labels or vulnerability-classification scores rarely yield deployable correctness. Recurring validity threats include weak test oracles, data leakage, changing agent harnesses, proxy-only security checks, and under-reported budgets/human intervention. The paper outlines a research agenda prioritizing secure-and-functional benchmarks, repository-scale threat models, calibrated human oversight, longitudinal maintainability evidence, and reproducible agent evaluation.

Conclusion: Model capability should be evaluated as an assurance case supported by task-appropriate evidence, rather than by a single benchmark score.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are moving from code completion toward repository-scale agents that retrieve context, edit files, execute tools, and participate in security-sensitive workflows. The evidence for these systems, however, remains divided between software engineering evaluations centered on functional task completion and software security evaluations centered on vulnerability detection, secure generation, or exploit-oriented validation. This evidence-centered structured survey synthesizes representative work available through May 31, 2026 across software engineering tasks, software security tasks, adaptation mechanisms, artifact granularity, and evaluation design. In addition to a task taxonomy, we introduce an assurance framework that separates functional correctness, security, operational reliability, evidence provenance, and agent authority. The review shows that execution feedback and repository access can substantially improve engineering task completion, but do not by themselves establish security; conversely, static-analysis labels or vulnerability-classification scores rarely establish deployable correctness. We identify recurring validity threats--weak test oracles, duplicated and temporally leaked data, changing agent harnesses, proxy-only security checks, and under-reported budgets and human intervention--and derive a minimum reporting protocol for cross-study comparison. The resulting research agenda prioritizes jointly secure-and-functional benchmarks, repository-scale threat models, calibrated human oversight, longitudinal maintainability evidence, and reproducible agent evaluation. The central conclusion is that model capability should be judged as an assurance case supported by task-appropriate evidence, rather than by a single benchmark score.

</details>


### [241] [Root cause analysis via difference graph discovery from linear time-series data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21117)
*Anouk Ruer,Timothée Loranchet,Daria Bystrova,Charles K. Assaad*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces a time-series extension of difference-graph discovery to identify effect-defying root causes (coefficients that change across normal vs anomalous regimes) using linear discrete-time structural causal models; validated on synthetic and real-world IT and ICU data; enables localizing causal mechanisms behind anomalies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Root cause analysis in complex dynamical systems is hindered by regime-dependent shifts in causal relationships. Traditional two-population difference graphs are extended to time-series to detect variables whose causal influence changes between normal and anomalous regimes.

Method: Formalizes linear discrete-time dynamic structural causal models; adapts existing difference-graph discovery methods to compare normal vs anomalous regimes. Evaluates on simulated data and applies to IT monitoring and intensive care monitoring datasets.

Result: Difference-graph discovery effectively localizes the causal mechanisms responsible for anomalous behavior, identifying regime-change in coefficients and pinpointing affected variables.

Conclusion: Difference-graph discovery is a viable framework for time-series root cause analysis, providing actionable insights into mechanism changes driving anomalies in real-world settings.

Abstract: Root cause analysis aims to identify the mechanisms responsible for anomalies in complex dynamical systems. In this paper, we study root cause analysis in linear time-series through the lens of difference graph discovery. We focus on effect-defying root causes, corresponding to variables whose causal coefficients change between a normal and an anomalous regime. We formalize this problem using linear discrete-time dynamic structural causal models and adapt several methods originally introduced for discovering difference graphs between two populations to the time-series setting, where the two populations are replaced by a normal and an anomalous regime. We first evaluate the proposed approaches on simulated data, and then demonstrate their practical relevance on real-world datasets from IT monitoring and intensive care monitoring. Our results show how difference graph discovery can help localize causal mechanisms responsible for anomalous behavior.

</details>


### [242] [From Attention Masks to Inert Zero-Vector Tokens: OAttention and O-Closure for Token Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21174)
*Heyang Gong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces OAttention with an active-presence coefficient to gate token interactions in attention, enabling token-state participation and missing-value semantics; yields exact null operations and compositional closure, with limited empirical impact and scoped guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the limitation of attention masks that are relation-level and do not carry token-state information. Provide a representation-carried, presence-weighted mechanism to control information flow and integration across tokens.

Method: Define p_i = ||h_i||^2 / (τ + ||h_i||^2) as an active-presence coefficient. Gate receiver outputs by p_i and weigh source j by p_j in both the attention numerator and partition, while preserving standard attention components. Derive properties (null-receiver, null-source, self-attention, empty-support) and establish OComponents (OFFN, ONorm, OInject), presence-weighted OStandardize, O-Closure M(H ⊕ 0) = M(H) ⊕ 0, and an OTransformer via residual/compositional closure. Validate via contract tests and GPU evaluation. Empirical evaluation on a zero-fine-tuning retrofit of a cloned pretrained TabPFN v3 regressor: Calibrated OAttention and Full-O variants change mean RMSE by +0.088% and +0.177% over 18 dataset-seed cases. An ablation shows OAttention alone fails to preserve NULL state through ordinary host components; OTransformer path does. These tests assess exactness, path-compatibility, and compositional necessity; they do not claim universal no-loss or universal missing-value semantics.

Result: Conceptually, the approach provides a principled, token-level presence mechanism that integrates with attention to yield exact algebraic properties (null elements, closure). Empirically, the performance impact is small but positive or neutral (slight RMSE increase) with the Full-O variant performing slightly worse. Ablations indicate that preserving NULL states requires the OTransformer pathway, not OAttention alone. The experiments are scoped (specific model/dataset, limited ablations) and do not establish broad universal guarantees.

Conclusion: OAttention and the broader O-constructs offer a theoretically coherent framework for presence-aware attention with compositional closure and exact-null properties. While helpful for handling missing values and ensuring certain algebraic guarantees, the empirical evidence is limited in scope, and universal no-loss or general semantics for missing values remain unproven. Further work should explore broader datasets, more comprehensive ablations, and formal proofs of universal closure properties.

Abstract: Attention masks are relation-level controls: they specify which query--source pairs may interact. They do not provide a representation-carried token state that is non-participating at the attention boundary. We assign each token hidden carrier \(h_i\) an active-presence coefficient \(p_i=\lVert h_i\rVert^2/(τ+\lVert h_i\rVert^2)\). The same coefficient has two roles: it gates information emitted by token \(i\), and it determines the mass with which token \(i\) enters computations shared with other tokens.
  OAttention is the support-coupled attention realization of this rule. It gates the receiver output by \(p_i\) and weights source \(j\) by \(p_j\) in both the attention numerator and partition, while retaining the standard score, visibility relation, exponential competition, and value aggregation. This makes the zero-vector token a zero element and yields exact null-receiver, null-source insertion, self-attention insertion, and empty-support properties. The same token-level presence gives local O-components (OFFN, ONorm, and OInject), presence-weighted OStandardize, the O-Closure law \(M(H\oplus0)=M(H)\oplus0\), and an OTransformer by residual and compositional closure.
  The canonical operator is checked by contract tests and a GPU evaluation.
