In a zero-fine-tuning retrofit of a cloned pretrained TabPFN v3 regressor, calibrated hidden-carrier OAttention and Full-O variants change mean RMSE by $+0.088\%$ and $+0.177\%$, respectively, over 18 matched dataset--seed cases. A two-block ablation shows that OAttention alone does not preserve a NULL state through ordinary host components, whereas the OTransformer path does. These are scoped tests of exactness, active-path compatibility, and compositional necessity; they do not establish universal no-loss, arbitrary-host closure, learned attraction to the origin, or a general semantics for missing values.

</details>


### [243] [SENTRY: Deterministic, Intelligent Risk Assessment for IT Change Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21203)
*Daniel Arulpragasam,Christer Henrysson,Ella Ly,Deepika Anbalagan,Leo Feng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SENTRY replaces questionnaire-based risk scoring with a deterministic ML pipeline combining XGBoost on structured data and retrieval-augmented generation to summarize unstructured change requests, achieving 0.87 ROC AUC and 85% accuracy in enterprise-scale change risk assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address subjectivity and gaming of self-reported questionnaires, improve discrimination between routine and high-risk changes, and maintain determinism and explainability in a regulated change-management setting.

Method: A deterministic ML pipeline using gradient-boosted decision trees (XGBoost) fed by structured operational metadata, application dependency graphs, and historical incident records. It includes a retrieval step with hybrid semantic and lexical search over historical change requests to extract a risk signal from unstructured text, compressing this signal into a single scalar feature for model inference. SHAP values provide per-prediction explainability.

Result: On enterprise-scale change data, SENTRY achieves ROC AUC of 0.87 and 85% overall accuracy, and detects high-risk changes at roughly 3.25 times the rate of the existing process.

Conclusion: The paper discusses architectural trade-offs of this design and its implications for applying machine learning in regulated change management, highlighting integration of structured and unstructured data, determinism, and explainability.

Abstract: Technology change management in large financial institutions depends on risk assessments that are accurate, consistent, and auditable. In practice, many institutions still rely on self-reported questionnaires. Those questionnaires are subjective, easy to game, and poor at separating routine changes from the ones that later trigger major incidents. This paper presents SENTRY, a risk assessment platform that replaces questionnaire-based scoring with a deterministic machine learning pipeline built from gradient-boosted decision trees (XGBoost) and hybrid retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). The system combines structured operational metadata, application dependency graphs, and historical incident records with a hybrid semantic and lexical search over historical change requests. The retrieval step captures the risk signal in unstructured change request text, then compresses that signal into a single scalar feature before model inference. That design keeps the model deterministic and preserves per-prediction explainability via SHAP values. Evaluated on enterprise-scale change data, SENTRY achieves a ROC AUC of 0.87 and 85% overall accuracy, and it detects high-risk changes at roughly 3.25 times the rate of the existing process. We close by examining the architectural trade-offs behind this design and what they imply for the use of machine learning in regulated change management.

</details>


### [244] [Enhancing LLMs in Predictive Political QA with Semi-Structured Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21218)
*Yinan Liu,Zihan Zhou,Zichun Jin,Xinyu Wang,Bin Wang,Xiaochun Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PSL introduces a dual-view, evidence-centric approach for predictive political QA by extracting stance signals from question-relevant actor records (semantic view) and learning structure-aware actor representations from an interaction graph (vector view). This yields consistent gains across three real-world datasets and multiple LLMs, with ablations showing complementary benefits from both signals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM augmentation for predictive political QA treats external resources as general knowledge evidence and often neglects prediction-specific signals. There is a gap in modeling actor-specific issue stances and high-order dependencies among actors to improve predictive voting inferences.

Method: PSL converts semi-structured political records into inference-oriented evidence for LLMs via two views: (1) semantic view extracting stance signals from question-relevant actor records, and (2) vector view learning structure-aware actor representations from an actor interaction graph.

Result: Empirical evaluation on three real-world datasets across multiple LLMs shows PSL consistently outperforms baselines. Ablation studies demonstrate that both stance signals and structure signals contribute, with complementary gains.

Conclusion: A dual-view PSL framework effectively enhances predictive political QA by leveraging complementary stance and structure signals, improving LLM-assisted inference across diverse datasets.

Abstract: Predictive political question answering (QA), such as predicting how a political actor will vote, goes beyond factual lookup. External political resources offer rich historical evidence, but rarely contain the answer itself. Existing LLM augmentation methods, including actor-profile-based simulation and knowledge graph evidence injection, improve political reasoning but largely treat external resources as knowledge-based evidence, leaving prediction-relevant signals under-modeled. We identify two complementary signals for predictive political QA: actor stances that capture issue-specific preferences, and high-order structure signals that capture indirect dependencies among political actors. We propose PSL, a dual-view framework that converts semi-structured political records into inference-oriented evidence for LLMs. PSL extracts stance signals from question-relevant actor records in a semantic view, and learns structure-aware actor representations from an actor interaction graph in a vector view. Across three real-world datasets and multiple LLMs, PSL consistently outperforms baselines, with ablations confirming the complementary gains of stance and structure signals.

</details>


### [245] [Ontology-supported AI Model and Dataset Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21224)
*Jan Novacek,Ali Ahari,Tobias Müller,Sebastian Reiter,Alexander Viehl,Oliver Bringmann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes an ontology-based AI model exchange platform to manage AI assets and enable semantic-consistent sharing; demonstrated via a real-time critical systems use case.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To track, trace, and manage AI assets across organizations, minimize semantic gaps, and enable transparent comparisons and understanding in industrial AI contexts.

Method: Develop a platform for AI model exchange that includes an ontology to harmonize concepts related to models and datasets, enabling usage, exchange, and analysis; validated through a real-time critical systems use case.

Result: The platform supports usage, exchange, and analysis of AI models/datasets, with the ontology fostering a common understanding and practical utility demonstrated by the real-time critical systems use case.

Conclusion: Ontology-enabled AI asset exchange platforms can reduce semantic gaps and improve interoperability in industrial AI supply chains; real-time critical systems illustrate practical benefits.

Abstract: Recently, there has been a great deal of research into improving AI methods and their application. The main focus is on tracking progress, enabling transparent comparisons, and fostering a more profound understanding of AI. In that process, different organizations generate and use plenty of assets that need to be tracked, traced and managed. Moreover, it is important to discover assets relevant for the task at hand. This paper presents research aiming to contribute to answering the question of what is required to exchange and manage AI models and related assets effectively without semantic gaps in an industrial context. We introduce a platform for AI model exchange, which facilitates the usage, exchange, and analysis of AI models and datasets. The platform incorporates an ontology that can foster a more profound common understanding of what is required in these tasks and help tackle the issues mentioned above. Finally, we elucidate the utility of the platform through the illustration of a use case in the context of real-time critical systems.

</details>


### [246] [Fine-Grain GPU Parallelization of the Generalized Partition Crossover for Large-Scale Traveling Salesman Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21233)
*Swetha Varadarajan,Darrell Whitley*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Fine-grain GPU partition phase for GPX in GA-based TSP; reformulated as graph-parallel with coalesced memory and ghost-nodes; achieves 48x–625x speedups on 10k–2M-city instances, reducing memory overhead and enabling scalable operator-level parallelism on many-core GPUs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome scalability bottlenecks of GA-based TSP solvers on GPUs due to irregular memory access, graph traversals, and sequential dependencies; existing GPU solvers emphasize population-level parallelism and small sizes. A fine-grain GPU partitioning approach for GPX could enable larger-scale TSPs.

Method: Implement GPX partitioning on GPU (CUDA) by recasting partitioning as a graph-parallel problem. Use coalesced memory layouts, ghost-node transformations, and connected-component analysis. Parallelize: (i) union of parent tours, (ii) splitting of degree-4 vertices, (iii) deletion of common edges, (iv) identification of recombining components.

Result: Empirical evaluation on 10k–2M-city instances shows substantial acceleration over naive sequential CPU implementation. GPU partitioning achieves 48x–625x speedups and reduces memory overhead, demonstrating operator-level parallelism boosts GA-based TSP scalability on modern many-core architectures.

Conclusion: GPU-based GPX partitioning is effective for scaling GA-based TSP solvers to large instances. Operator-level, graph-parallel partitioning yields major speedups and memory savings, enabling more scalable TSP optimization on GPUs.

Abstract: The Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP) is one of the most extensively studied NP-hard optimization problems. Genetic Algorithm (GA)-based solvers, such as the Edge Assembly Crossover (EAX), achieve state-of-the-art performance on many benchmark instances. However, the scalability of these approaches in massively parallel architectures remains limited because crossover operations involve irregular memory access patterns, graph traversals, and sequential dependencies. Existing GPU-based TSP solvers primarily exploit population-level parallelism and are limited to relatively small problem sizes. This work presents a fine-grain GPU implementation of the partition phase of the Generalized Partition Crossover (GPX) operator for large-scale TSP instances. The proposed approach reformulates GPX partitioning as a graph-parallel problem using coalesced memory layouts, ghost-node transformations, and connected-component analysis. The im- plementation parallelizes the union of parent tours, the splitting of degree- four vertices, the deletion of common edges, and the identification of recombining components using CUDA. Experimental results on instances ranging from 10,000 to 2 million cities demonstrate substantial acceleration over a naive sequential CPU imple- mentation. The proposed GPU partitioning achieves speedups between 48x and 625x while significantly reducing memory overhead. The re- sults demonstrate that operator-level parallelism can substantially im- prove the scalability of GA-based TSP solvers on modern many-core architectures.

</details>


### [247] [CLEAR: Continuous Latent Adapter Routing for Utility-Preserving LLM Safety Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21278)
*Chengxiao Wang,Enyi Jiang,Xiaojing Liao,Sanmi Koyejo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CLEAR introduces a conditional safety adaptation via a hidden-state gate that modulates a safety low-rank adapter, reducing harmful outputs with minimal impact on benign task performance, outperforming global safety tuning on HarmBench and GSM8K on Llama-3-8B-Instruct.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Global safety tuning often degrades utility; a mechanism is needed to selectively apply safety adjustments without altering the frozen backbone.

Method: Propose CLEAR: a lightweight hidden-state gate that controls the activation of a safety LoRA-style adapter (low-rank) conditioned on input; prevents unnecessary changes to backbone; evaluate on safety and utility benchmarks; compare to SFT and standard LoRA; evaluate on Llama-3-8B-Instruct.

Result: HarmBench robustness improves; HarmBench ASR drops from 32.3% to 0.5%; GSM8K accuracy up to 7.1 percentage points higher than globally applied SFT or LoRA; toxicity reduced while preserving utility.

Conclusion: CLEAR is a promising approach for balancing safety and utility in LLM alignment by enabling continuous, input-conditioned safety adaptation without compromising base capabilities.

Abstract: Improving the safety of large language models (LLMs) often comes at the expense of utility, as globally applied safety tuning may affect model responses to both harmful and benign inputs. We propose \textbf{C}ontinuous \textbf{L}at\textbf{E}nt \textbf{A}dapter \textbf{R}outing (CLEAR), a conditional safety adaptation framework that uses a lightweight hidden-state gate to continuously control the activation strength of a safety low-rank adapter. CLEAR aims to reduce harmful completions while avoiding unnecessary changes to the frozen backbone that could degrade performance on benign prompts. Experiments on widely used safety and utility benchmarks show that CLEAR improves robustness on HarmBench while reducing the utility degradation observed with globally applied safety tuning such as SFT or standard low-rank adaptation (LoRA). On Llama-3-8B-Instruct, CLEAR reduces HarmBench ASR from 32.3\% to 0.5\%, while retaining most of the base model's utility and achieving up to 7.1 percentage points higher GSM8K accuracy than globally applied SFT or LoRA. These results suggest that CLEAR is a promising mechanism for improving the safety--utility trade-off in LLM alignment.

</details>


### [248] [AUSO: Action-Level Unified Skill Optimization from Internalization to Utilization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21292)
*Huizu Lin,Chengkai Huang,Tianqi Gao,Tao Huang,Daijiao Liu,Tongxin Li,Xiaoyan Sun,Lina Yao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AUSO unifies skill learning and use via progressive, action-aware optimization, enabling skills to transition from external guidance to decision knowledge; shows improvements on ALFWorld, WebShop, and SearchQA vs baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RL skill handling often treats skills as external modules or fully internalized, or uses noisy task signals to balance objectives, leading to fragmented training and uniform action treatment. A lifecycle-aware, integrated approach is needed.

Method: Progressive three-stage optimization: (1) joint learning from teacher guidance and environment to acquire foundational skills; (2) outcome-focused policy optimization to consolidate autonomous problem solving; (3) action-level evaluation comparing skill-conditioned vs skill-free contexts, coupling the resulting signal with trajectory advantage to emphasize beneficial skill-sensitive actions.

Result: Empirical results on ALFWorld, WebShop, and SearchQA show consistent performance gains and improved out-of-distribution generalization over competitive baselines.

Conclusion: AUSO enables skills to gradually internalize from external supervision into action-level knowledge, with a unified RL backbone across stages; demonstrates that action-level, skill-aware updates can improve learning efficiency and generalization.

Abstract: Skills play different roles as an agent's policy evolves: they should first provide learnable knowledge, then support capability formation, and finally be invoked only when they improve individual decisions. Existing methods rarely model this lifecycle. They either keep skills outside the model, fully internalize them, or select among internalization and utilization objectives through noisy task-level success rates. Such designs fragment training and assign uniform importance to actions within the same trajectory, even though skill guidance may help some decisions while distracting others. To solve these problems, we introduce AUSO (Action-level Unified Skill Optimization), which unifies skill learning and skill use through a progressive, action-aware optimization process. At the beginning of training, AUSO jointly learns from teacher guidance and environmental outcomes, enabling the policy to acquire foundational skills without losing task-oriented feedback. It subsequently emphasizes outcome-based policy optimization to consolidate autonomous problem-solving ability. As the policy matures, AUSO evaluates each sampled action under both skill-conditioned and skill-free contexts. The resulting action-level information signal is coupled with the trajectory outcome advantage, allowing beneficial skill-sensitive actions to receive stronger updates and harmful ones to be suppressed. Therefore, skills gradually transition from an external source of supervision into decision knowledge whose utilization is adapted to its action-level benefit, while reinforcement learning remains the shared backbone across all stages. Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and SearchQA show that AUSO consistently improves agent performance and out-of-distribution generalization over competitive baselines.

</details>


### [249] [From Regulation to Implementation: A Critical Evaluation of LLM-Assisted Regulatory Compliance in Industry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21317)
*Adriana Watson,Marco Bücheler,Grant Richards*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-based generation of EU compliance artifacts (DPPs and DPIAs) is affected by data extraction challenges and regulatory vagueness; benchmarking shows strict formatting yields consistent outputs but may induce hallucinations, while lax guidelines require richer prompts to maintain quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EU sustainability and privacy regulations mandate artifacts; heterogeneous industrial data and nonstandard DPIA formats hinder automated artifact creation; gaps in understanding how prompts and data constraints affect LLM outputs.

Method: Benchmark multiple LLMs against manually created ground-truth schemas for DPP-related artifacts and DPIAs, systematically varying data extraction instructions and regulatory formatting guidelines; evaluate consistency and completeness against ground truth.

Result: Lower-strict guidelines benefit from more context prompts to achieve consistency; tighter formatting for DPP-like artifacts yields consistent results across prompts but increases hallucinations; data-extraction quality strongly influences artifact fidelity.

Conclusion: Adopt standardized artifact schemas and robust data-extraction pipelines to improve LLM-generated compliance outputs; balance formatting rigidity with hallucination risk; further research should optimize prompting strategies and schema design for cross-regulation artifact generation.

Abstract: The European Union (EU) has emerged as a leading regulatory body in the development of sustainability and privacy regulations. While new regulation requirements vary, many include a documentation artifact to ensure compliance. Notably, the Ecodesign for Sustainable Products Regulation (ESPR) introduces Digital Product Passports (DPPs) for life cycle transparency, while the General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) mandates Data Protection Impact Assessments (DPIAs) to mitigate privacy risks. Creating these compliance artifacts, however, is challenging. Industrial data, which often exists in heterogeneous formats and is scattered across company and supplier systems, is required for DPPs and can be difficult to extract into compliant DPP formatting. Furthermore, DPIA documents require interdisciplinary expertise and follow no standardized format, making development difficult for novel systems. To address the particular complexity of compliance artifact creation for both regulations, researchers have proposed the use of LLMs in the generation process; however, the impact of the aforementioned problems on the output of these systems is largely unaddressed. This work investigates the existing research gap by exploring how data extraction instructions and regulatory vagueness impact the quality and consistency of LLM-produced compliance artifacts. The resulting artifacts are evaluated by benchmarking different models against manually created ground-truth schemas. The results reveal that less strict guidelines, such as DPIA formatting, require higher context prompts to maintain consistency and completeness. Stricter guidelines, such as formatting for Digital Battery Passports (DBP), result in consistent results regardless of prompt context, but may lead to more hallucinations in the output

</details>


### [250] [VIALS: A Benchmark for Visual Interpretation of Artifacts in the Life Sciences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21357)
*Elaine Lau,Thanuka Udumulla,Lee Izhaki-Tavor,Francisco Guzmán,Nicholas Magazine,Jonas Mueller*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VIALS: a 161-task VQA benchmark for professional biotech artifacts; reveals a clear gap between current vision-language models and domain experts in interpreting scientific visuals; highlights need for domain-aware AI in life sciences workflows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Domain-specific visual interpretation is critical in life sciences workflows, but existing VQA datasets and generic vision-language models fail to capture the specialized visual reasoning required for laboratory artifacts.

Method: Construct VIALS with 161 interpretation tasks across artifacts encountered in biotech workflows (gel blots, microscopy images, plasmid maps, flow cytometry plots, etc.); evaluate frontier vision-language models and domain-expert scientists on these tasks; analyze failure modes and task categories.

Result: Gen­er­al-purpose vision-language models perform poorly on these scientific artifacts compared with domain experts; there is a substantial performance gap indicating limited utility of such models in professional life sciences workflows; domain knowledge and specialized visual reasoning are key.

Conclusion: Current AI models cannot reliably interpret professional biotech visuals; to be useful in practice, AI systems must integrate domain knowledge and specialized visual reasoning. The VIALS benchmark provides a resource to measure progress and guide future research toward domain-aware AI in the life sciences.

Abstract: In professional life sciences workflows, scientists routinely interpret visual artifacts (gel blots, microscopy images, plasmid maps, flow cytometry plots, molecular structures, ...) to inform research decisions. We introduce VIALS, a visual question-answering benchmark with 161 such interpretation tasks, spanning the types of artifacts examined throughout experimental workflows in the biotech industry (rather than polished figures from publications and textbooks). While frontier vision-language models can now fluently describe natural images, we find that they are unable to accurately interpret these scientific images, reflecting limitations in domain knowledge and domain-specific visual reasoning capabilities. In contrast, scientists with relevant domain expertise find these visual interpretation tasks straightforward. AI that cannot similarly interpret such images will have limited utility in professional life sciences workflows, where such artifacts are central to how scientists reason, communicate, and make decisions.

</details>
